### [1] [EduRiskX: A Neuro-Symbolic Framework with F-Logic Reasoning for Early Academic Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26107)
*Yu Fu,Yongqi Kang,Yong Zhao,Rongfang Bie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EduRiskX: A neuro-symbolic framework combining Transformer-based temporal modeling with F-Logic symbolic reasoning to predict and explain student academic risk in online education, achieving high accuracy and early detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing student risk prediction models suffer from limited early detection capability and insufficient interpretability ("black-box" problem), hindering adoption in real-world educational settings where trust and timely interventions are crucial.

Method: Proposes EduRiskX, a neuro-symbolic framework with two components: 1) Neural component: Temporal Transformer-based predictor with class-weighted loss and dynamic weekly truncation to model longitudinal student activity sequences; 2) Symbolic component: F-Logic rule base constructed from training data, grounded in educational theories (Engagement Theory and Student Integration Model) to mimic human educator diagnostic logic. Both components are fused via logistic regression-based mechanism to learn relative contributions.

Result: On OULAD dataset with strict 80/10/10 student-level split: Achieves accuracy of 0.900 and F1-score of 0.894 at Week 38, with average early detection week of 9.32 and detection rate of 94.30%. Outperforms state-of-the-art time-series models (PatchTST, iTransformer) and deep learning baselines (LSTM, CNN) in recall and early risk identification.

Conclusion: EduRiskX effectively addresses both predictive performance and interpretability challenges in student risk prediction through its neuro-symbolic architecture, enabling earlier and more trustworthy academic risk detection with rule-based explanations linking predictions to behavioral patterns and educational theories.

Abstract: Predicting students' academic risk in online education is crucial for enabling timely interventions that can improve retention and learning outcomes. However, existing models often suffer from limited early detection capability and insufficient interpretability, leading to a "black-box" trust crisis that hinders their adoption in real-world pedagogical settings. To address these challenges, we propose EduRiskX, a neuro-symbolic framework that integrates a temporal Transformer-based predictor with F-Logic symbolic reasoning. The neural component models longitudinal student activity sequences using temporal attention, class-weighted loss, and dynamic weekly truncation. Acting as a data-driven expert system, an F-Logic rule base -- grounded in established educational theories (Engagement Theory and Student Integration Model) to mimic the diagnostic logic of human educators -- is constructed exclusively from the training data. The neural risk probability and the symbolic confidence score are then combined through a logistic regression-based fusion mechanism that learns the relative contribution of each signal. Experiments on the Open University Learning Analytics Dataset (OULAD) using a strict 80/10/10 student-level split show that EduRiskX achieves an accuracy of 0.900 and an F1-score of 0.894 at the end of the semester (Week 38), with an average early detection week of 9.32 and a detection rate of 94.30 percent. Compared with state-of-the-art time-series models (PatchTST, iTransformer) and common deep learning baselines (LSTM, CNN), EduRiskX yields improved recall and earlier risk identification under identical conditions. Beyond predictive performance, the F-Logic module provides structured rule-based explanations linking predictions to observable behavioral patterns and educational theories.

</details>


### [2] [Standalone LLM and a Pre-specified Agentic Pipeline for Explaining ICU Mortality Predictions: a Feasibility Study on the eICU Demo Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26109)
*Di Zhu,Chen Xie,Haoyun Zhang,Zihan Wei,Ziwei Wang,Jiazhao Shi,Ziyu Wang,Qiyang Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic LLM pipelines improve clinical guideline grounding and value specificity for ICU mortality explanations compared to standalone LLMs, though standalone models show better SHAP alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While ML models accurately predict ICU mortality, traditional feature-attribution methods lack the clinical narrative needed for bedside use. LLMs may bridge this gap, and multi-step agentic pipelines could further improve clinical explanations by separating data interpretation, guideline checking, and final explanation.

Method: Used eICU Demo dataset (2,353 ICU stays; 8.1% mortality) with XGBoost achieving AUROC of 0.855. Compared standalone LLM vs four-step agentic pipeline on 38-case explanation subset. Analyzed SHAP alignment, direction consistency, guideline grounding, value specificity, and plausibility for both approaches.

Result: Standalone LLM had 1 explanation with outcome leakage vs agentic pipeline had none. Standalone LLM showed higher SHAP alignment (mean Jaccard 0.171 vs 0.077) and direction consistency (92.9% vs 78.6%). Agentic pipeline showed higher guideline grounding (0.762 vs 0.143), higher value specificity (0.236 vs 0.143), and slightly higher plausibility (0.700 vs 0.671).

Conclusion: Agentic decomposition improves safety-relevant grounding and patient-specific detail for clinical explanations, but should be paired with attribution-based checks before use in high-stakes risk explanation contexts.

Abstract: Machine-learning models can predict ICU mortality accurately, but feature-attribution methods alone rarely provide the clinical narrative needed for bedside use. Large language models (LLMs) may bridge this gap, and multi-step agentic pipelines are a plausible extension because they separate data interpretation, guideline checking, and final explanation. This revised feasibility study preserves the original standalone-versus-agentic comparison while making the main clinical findings more explicit. Using the retained local eICU Demo artifact set (2,353 ICU stays; 8.1\% mortality), XGBoost achieved an AUROC of 0.855 (95\% CI 0.796--0.906) and an AUPRC of 0.332 (95\% CI 0.217--0.494). On a stratified 38-case explanation subset, the standalone LLM produced 1 explanation with explicit outcome leakage, whereas the four-step agentic pipeline produced none. Among the 14 cases that overlapped with the SHAP review subset, the standalone LLM showed higher SHAP alignment (mean Jaccard 0.171 versus 0.077) and higher direction consistency (92.9\% versus 78.6\%), while the agentic pipeline showed higher guideline grounding (0.762 versus 0.143), higher value specificity (0.236 versus 0.143), and slightly higher plausibility (0.700 versus 0.671). Clinically, the results suggest that agentic decomposition may improve safety-relevant grounding and patient-specific detail, but it should be paired with attribution-based checks before use in high-stakes risk explanation.

</details>


### [3] [Large Models for Battery Prognostics and Health Management: A Review and Future Roadmap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26111)
*Jiale Liu,Huan Wang,Weicheng Wang,Rong Zhu,Qiqi Wang,Min Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This is the first comprehensive review of Large Model (LM) applications in Battery Prognostics and Health Management (BPHM), examining how Transformer-based models with self-supervised learning address traditional BPHM limitations in data efficiency, generalization, interpretability, and automation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BPHM is critical for safe and reliable battery operation in various applications, but conventional approaches (physics-based models and task-centric deep learning) face challenges in computational efficiency, cross-domain generalization, dependence on extensive labeled data, and model interpretability.

Method: The review systematically surveys LM applications in BPHM by examining foundational technologies (Transformer architectures, self-supervised learning, large-scale multimodal datasets, PEFT techniques) and categorizing progress along four dimensions: data scarcity mitigation, generalization/robustness enhancement, domain knowledge integration for interpretability, and system-level automation.

Result: Despite promising results from LM applications, significant challenges remain in data accessibility, intelligence validation, trustworthiness, and deployment feasibility. The review identifies current progress while highlighting ongoing limitations.

Conclusion: The review establishes a systematic framework for LM-driven BPHM and proposes a roadmap focused on collaborative data ecosystems, industrial intelligence validation, physics-informed trustworthiness enhancement, and efficient on-device deployment to guide development of next-generation autonomous battery management systems.

Abstract: Battery Prognostics and Health Management (BPHM) is critical for ensuring the safe, reliable, and cost-effective operation of batteries across electric vehicles, grid storage, and consumer electronics. Conventional BPHM approaches, including physics-based models and task-centric deep learning methods, face challenges in computational efficiency and parameterization, cross-domain generalization, dependence on extensive labeled run-to-failure data, and model interpretability. Recent Large Models (LMs), built upon Transformer architectures and self-supervised pre-training, offer a transformative new paradigm to overcome these long-standing bottlenecks. This review provides the first comprehensive survey of LM applications in BPHM, systematically examining how these models address challenges in the field. We begin by elucidating the foundational technologies enabling LMs, including Transformer architectures, self-supervised learning, large-scale multimodal datasets, and PEFT techniques. We then categorize recent progress along four critical dimensions: mitigating data scarcity, enhancing generalization and robustness, integrating domain knowledge for interpretability, and enabling system-level automation. Despite promising results, significant challenges remain across data accessibility, intelligence validation, trustworthiness, and deployment feasibility. To guide future research, we propose a roadmap focused on building collaborative data ecosystems, validating intelligence for industrial applications, enhancing trustworthiness with physics-informed designs, and enabling efficient on-device deployment. This review establishes a systematic approach to understand and advance LM-driven BPHM, providing researchers and practitioners with essential insights for developing next-generation battery management systems capable of safe, reliable, and autonomous operation throughout battery lifecycles.

</details>


### [4] [PICasso: An AI-Enabled Design Framework for Autonomous Optimization of Silicon Photonic Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26113)
*Deepak Vungarala,Deniz Najafi,Abdulrahman Aljoudi,Zahra Ghanaatian,Navid Khoshavi,Gourav Datta,Arman Roohi,Mahdi Nikdast,Shaahin Angizi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PICasso is an AI-assisted framework that automates photonic integrated circuit design from natural language specifications through synthesis, verification, and optimization, achieving high success rates and improved circuit performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional photonic integrated circuit design is complex and time-consuming, requiring specialized expertise. There's a need to automate the design process and leverage AI to transform natural language specifications into manufacturable layouts while ensuring physical correctness.

Method: PICasso uses a structured pipeline: natural language → YAML → GDS generation with PDK-aware knowledge injection, automated placement and routing, DRC/LVS validation, and SAX-based photonic simulation. It also introduces PIC-Set, a benchmark of 36 parameterized design tasks for systematic evaluation.

Result: PICasso achieves structural Spec@3 of up to 92.7% and functional Spec@3 up to 52% on high-complexity circuits. It reduces mean circuit insertion loss from 4.98 dB to 3.25 dB (1.74 dB improvement). The framework significantly outperforms vanilla LLM generation and produces manufacturable layouts with competitive runtimes.

Conclusion: Structured domain constraints, physical verification, and simulation feedback transform LLMs from brittle netlist generators into practical PIC design agents capable of producing manufacturable layouts efficiently, demonstrating the viability of AI-assisted photonic design automation.

Abstract: We present PICasso, an AI-assisted framework for automated synthesis, verification, and optimization of photonic integrated circuits (PICs) from natural-language specifications. PICasso couples a structured NL -> YAML -> GDS generation pipeline with PDK aware knowledge injection, automated placement and routing, DRC/LVS validation, and SAX-based photonic simulation. To systematically evaluate AI-driven photonic design, we introduce PIC-Set, a benchmark of 36 parameterized PIC design tasks spanning core photonic primitives and multi-component circuits. Using PIC-Set, we benchmark several state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) under a unified evaluation protocol, including new metrics such as structural and functional $Spec@k$, optimization efficiency, and robustness under perturbations. Across the benchmark, PICasso significantly improves end-to-end specification satisfaction compared to vanilla LLM generation. Structural $Spec@3$ reaches up to 92.7% and functional $Spec@3$ up to 52% on high-complexity circuits. In addition, PICasso consistently reduces circuit insertion loss, lowering the mean loss from 4.98 dB to 3.25 dB (1.74 dB improvement) through simulation-guided optimization. These results demonstrate that structured domain constraints, physical verification, and simulation feedback transform LLMs from brittle netlist generators into practical PIC design agents capable of producing manufacturable layouts with competitive runtimes relative to manual GUI-based workflows.

</details>


### [5] [CIFQA: A Deterministic Tool-Grounded Multi-Agent LLM Framework for Financial Query Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26114)
*Kunjesh Parekh,Anil Kumar Tiwari,Divya Saxena*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CIFQA is a deterministic tool-grounded multi-agent LLM framework that separates language understanding from numerical execution to solve calculation-intensive financial queries, achieving over 90% accuracy on fixed deposit questions and outperforming much larger LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with calculation-intensive financial question answering despite strong natural language capabilities, often producing numerically incorrect but plausible answers due to limitations in multi-step financial calculations involving structured rates, temporal conditions, formulas, and rule-based constraints.

Method: CIFQA uses a multi-agent LLM framework with specialized agents for query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, and response generation, combined with deterministic Python-based tools for financial calculations and rule application. This separates language understanding from numerical execution.

Result: Achieves 95.54% accuracy on calculation-intensive queries and 90.87% overall accuracy on a fixed deposit benchmark, substantially outperforming direct LLM baselines even when provided with complete formulas. A 17B open-source model within CIFQA outperforms much larger frontier models, showing architectural design is more important than model scale.

Conclusion: CIFQA provides an effective generalizable framework for calculation-intensive financial reasoning that demonstrates architectural design (separating language understanding from deterministic numerical execution) is more critical for numerical reliability than model scale, while offering strong performance on fixed deposit queries.

Abstract: Calculation-intensive financial question answering requires exact reasoning over structured rates, temporal conditions, numerical formulas, and rule-based constraints. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) perform strongly on natural language tasks, they often produce numerically incorrect yet plausible answers when solving multi-step financial calculations. To address this limitation, we introduce CIFQA (Calculation-Intensive Financial Query Answering), a deterministic tool-grounded multi-agent LLM framework for financial question answering. CIFQA separates language understanding from numerical execution by assigning specialized agents to query interpretation, routing, parameter extraction, computation planning, and response generation, while deterministic Python-based tools perform financial calculations and rule application. We instantiate CIFQA for fixed deposit query answering and evaluate it on a curated benchmark of fixed deposit queries. CIFQA achieves 95.54% accuracy on calculation-intensive queries and 90.87% overall accuracy, substantially outperforming direct LLM baselines even when provided with complete formulas, rate cards, and benchmark instructions. Ablation studies show that deterministic components such as exact rate lookup, tenure computation, rolling-year adjustment, and premature-withdrawal logic are critical contributors to performance. Notably, a 17B open-source backbone operating within CIFQA outperforms substantially larger frontier models evaluated with the same financial information, demonstrating that architectural design is a more important determinant of numerical reliability than model scale. While evaluated on fixed deposit queries, CIFQA provides a generalizable framework for calculation-intensive financial reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [6] [The Artificial Experimentalist: Discovery and Control of Self-Organizing Phenomena with Autotelic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26116)
*Marko Cvjetko,Benedikt Hartl,Michael Levin,Clément Moulin-Frier,Pierre-Yves Oudeyer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARL uses closed-loop reinforcement learning to discover and control emergent patterns (solitons) in Lenia cellular automata through minimal local perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for exploring cellular automata operate in open loop—they simulate and observe without intervention. This limits discovery and control of emergent phenomena. The paper aims to develop agents that can actively intervene in complex systems to discover and manipulate self-organizing patterns.

Method: Closed-loop framework based on autotelic reinforcement learning (CARL) where agents autonomously sample diverse goals and learn goal-conditioned policies to intervene in Lenia cellular automata through minimal local perturbations. The system operates in continuous space and learns to manipulate self-organizing patterns.

Result: 1) CARL discovers stable solitons across wide range of Lenia rules at higher rate than baselines. 2) Learns to steer soliton movement direction with few interventions. 3) Humans can use trained agents to guide solitons through mazes via high-level commands. Agents generalize zero-shot to out-of-distribution conditions.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate artificial experimentalist agents that can autonomously or with human guidance discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems, suggesting a path toward more interactive exploration of complex systems beyond passive observation.

Abstract: Existing methods for exploring cellular automata and other complex systems mostly operate in open loop: they set initial conditions, execute a full simulation, and observe the outcome, without intervening during execution. We introduce a closed-loop framework based on autotelic reinforcement learning, in which an agent autonomously samples diverse goals and learns a goal-conditioned policy to intervene in a complex system through minimal, local perturbations. We instantiate this framework on Lenia, a continuous cellular automaton known for life-like self-organizing patterns, in an agentic system we call CARL, and demonstrate three capabilities. First, CARL discovers stable solitons across a wide range of Lenia update rules at a higher rate than heuristic baselines. Second, it learns to steer the movement direction of existing solitons with few interventions, showing that CARL can control self-organizing patterns, not only create them. Third, humans can use trained agents to guide solitons through maze environments in real time by specifying high-level directional commands that the agent translates into low-level interventions. Trained across diverse goals, update rules, and random initial states, the agents acquire policies that generalize zero-shot to various out-of-distribution conditions. These results suggest a path toward artificial experimentalist agents that, autonomously or with human guidance, discover and control emergent phenomena in complex systems.

</details>


### [7] [The Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox Quantifying Net Energy Loss in on-Device Energy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26134)
*Jaeik Jeong,Tai-Yeon Ku,Wan-Ki Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TL;DR: Energy forecasting models can cause net energy loss despite accuracy due to their own energy consumption and battery aging; a Total Cost of Ownership framework addresses this paradox.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from identifying the Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox in energy forecasting: highly accurate models for energy conservation can ironically trigger net energy deficits in edge environments due to their own inference energy consumption and battery aging effects.

Method: The authors propose a Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) framework for energy forecasting that treats both inference energy consumption and battery aging as unified forms of energy loss. The framework accounts for battery degradation as a physical dissipation of future energy-carrying capacity.

Result: The paper demonstrates that in thermally sensitive edge environments, the energy saved by superior precision of complex forecasting models is often outweighed by the total energy lost through their high operational intensity (including both direct energy consumption and battery aging effects).

Conclusion: The conclusion highlights that energy forecasting should minimize net energy loss rather than solely maximize accuracy, and that the TCO framework provides a more holistic approach by accounting for both immediate inference costs and long-term battery degradation effects on overall system energy efficiency.

Abstract: Energy forecasting aims to maximize accuracy to ensure energy efficiency by reducing energy waste, an objective that applies equally to on-device forecasting for mission-critical edge environments, including military systems. However, this paper identifies the Accuracy-Efficiency Paradox: high-precision energy forecasting models can ironically trigger a net energy deficit. This stems from both edge AI's inference energy consumption and battery aging. We propose a Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) framework for energy forecasting, designed to minimize net energy loss. This framework treats not only inference energy consumption but also battery aging as a unified form of energy loss, as degradation represents a physical dissipation of the system's future energy-carrying capacity. We demonstrate that in thermally sensitive edge environments, energy saved by the superior precision of complex architectures is often outweighed by the total energy lost through their high operational intensity.

</details>


### [8] [LLMs for Academic Workflows: An Evaluation of Literature Reviews Generated with Short and Long Context Windows of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26145)
*Muhammad Ali Chaudhry,Xinyuan Hao,Haifa Alwahaby*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI-generated literature reviews improve with larger context windows but still need human oversight to meet academic standards; they tend to be descriptive rather than synthetic and miss key literature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the impact of context window size on the quality of AI-generated literature reviews and understand the role of AI in supporting literature review writing.

Method: Generated 20 AI literature reviews using research sources from Semantic Scholar and Arxiv, then evaluated them across 15 dimensions with two researchers assessing quality.

Result: AI-generated reviews require human oversight; larger context windows enable broader information incorporation and coherence but exacerbate issues like content repetition, omission of critical work, and descriptiveness over synthesis.

Conclusion: AI-generated reviews provide foundational overviews but must be critically evaluated by domain experts; future research should integrate other LLMs with fine-tuning and hybrid human-AI approaches.

Abstract: Our research focuses on evaluating literature reviews generated in short and long context settings of large language models (LLMs) to investigate the impact of context window on the quality of AI-generated literature reviews and the role of AI in supporting literature review writing. Twenty AI-generated literature reviews based on research sources from Semantic Scholar and Arxiv were evaluated by two researchers across 15 dimensions. Our findings reveal that AI-generated literature reviews require human oversight to meet academic publishing standards. As context windows increase, LLMs can incorporate broader information and maintain coherence across longer inputs, but they also exacerbate issues such as content repetition, omission of critical work, and a tendency towards descriptiveness over synthesis. Our work shows that AI-generated reviews can provide foundational overviews, but their output must be critically evaluated and refined by domain experts. Future research should consider integrating other LLMs and fine-tuned models in different domains with hybrid approaches that combine human expertise with AI capabilities to address the limitations identified in this study.

</details>


### [9] [Agent Mesh: Reliability Primitives for Non-Idempotent Agent Delegation - Identity Adequacy and Evidence Adequacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26225)
*Mazhar Shaikh,Anurag Rajkumar Bombarde,Harshal Pathak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Production study reveals that service mesh primitives (retry, timeout, circuit breaking) fail for agentic software tasks due to identity/evidence adequacy problems, proposing new delegation-based reliability primitives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents performing bounded software tasks under orchestrators use service mesh primitives (retry, timeout, circuit breaking), but these mechanisms are failing in production agentic software-delivery platforms, necessitating investigation of why and how to improve reliability.

Method: Failure study of a production agentic software-delivery platform analyzing 147 numbered incidents across 81 runs, each with measured cost and mutation proofs reproducing failures. The study examines violations of three assumptions underlying service mesh primitives and quantifies their consequences through concrete failure examples.

Result: The study found all three assumptions of service mesh primitives violated: 1) loops of consecutive successful tool calls invisible to error-rate breakers, 2) constant progress signals causing false trips, 3) accumulated events making correct components unwinnable, 4) misrouted failures waking multiple components, 5) enforcement layers blocking correct work. Identified cross-cutting causes: identity adequacy failures and evidence adequacy violations.

Conclusion: Proposes seven new reliability primitives with delegation as the enforcement unit (rather than message-based), and specifies a controlled evaluation framework motivated by the study findings but not conducted in this research.

Abstract: Autonomous agents increasingly perform bounded software tasks under an orchestrator that retries, resumes, and budgets them. The machinery such orchestrators reach for is the service mesh's: retry, timeout, and error-rate circuit breaking. We report a failure study of a production agentic software-delivery platform over 147 numbered incidents spanning 81 runs, each with a measured cost and, in most cases, a mutation proof reproducing the failure. All three assumptions those primitives rest on are violated in practice, and we quantify the consequences: a loop of fifty-four consecutive successful tool calls no error-rate breaker could see; a progress signal constant by construction, guaranteeing a false trip on the third repair round and driving one run from six of six components to three; twenty-one events accumulated across six invocations of one delegation, making a correct, idempotent component unwinnable; a misrouted failure that woke five components for a two-component fault, leaving three bystanders regressing working code; and twelve incidents in which the enforcement layer blocked correct work, the most expensive costing 107 agent turns and zero accepted writes. We find one cross-cutting cause and its dual. Identity adequacy: in five separate subsystems an identity that failed to discriminate produced a confident wrong answer, and two of them derived the corrective rule independently. Evidence adequacy: a reliability decision may be taken only on evidence capable of moving, attributable to what it measures, and deterministic under identical conditions. From the findings we derive seven reliability primitives whose enforcement unit is the delegation rather than the message, and specify the controlled evaluation the study motivates but does not constitute.

</details>


### [10] [Methodological and Conceptual Framework for 5D Multi-Table Analysis: A Unified Approach for Complex Data Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26149)
*Edouard Lansiaux,Hugo Kazzi,Aurélien Loison,Slim Hammadi,Emmanuel Chazard*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT) is a novel architecture for multi-table learning that represents relational databases as hypergraphs, uses pentadimensional embeddings, and performs sparse relational attention for scalable processing of complex healthcare data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-table learning in healthcare faces challenges with large data volume, high-dimensional variables, high-cardinality categorical features, complex inter-table dependencies, and repeated temporal observations. Existing methods struggle with these complexities.

Method: Introduces Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT) that represents relational databases as hypergraphs, learns pentadimensional embeddings (PentE), and uses sparse relational attention with complexity proportional to average relational degree rather than square of number of entities.

Result: Evaluated on Synthea synthetic EHR dataset for multi-label prediction of SNOMED CT condition codes. RHT produces more semantically coherent embeddings while remaining computationally scalable. XGBoost achieved highest rare-code recall, while RHT attained strongest embedding semantic coherence.

Conclusion: RHT provides a unified, scalable architecture for relational data that handles complex healthcare data structures effectively, with clinical validation on MIMIC-IV planned. The method offers computational efficiency through sparse attention mechanisms while maintaining semantic coherence.

Abstract: Multi-table learning remains a major challenge in machine learning for healthcare and other complex information systems. Relational data combine several sources of complexity, including large data volume, high-dimensional variables, high-cardinality categorical features, complex inter-table dependencies, and repeated temporal observations. We introduce the Relational Hypergraph Transformer (RHT), a unified architecture that represents relational databases as hypergraphs, learns pentadimensional embeddings (PentE), and performs sparse relational attention with complexity proportional to the average relational degree rather than the square of the number of entities. We formally define the architecture, derive the complexity of its attention mechanism, and provide an open-source reference implementation. We evaluate RHT on the public Synthea synthetic electronic health record dataset using multi-label prediction of SNOMED CT condition codes per encounter, a task characterized by high categorical cardinality and long-tailed label distributions. Comparisons with tabular, relational, and temporal graph baselines show that RHT produces more semantically coherent embeddings while remaining computationally scalable. In this benchmark, the highest rare-code recall is achieved by XGBoost, whereas RHT attains the strongest embedding semantic coherence. We also report ablation studies quantifying the contribution of each architectural component. Clinical validation on MIMIC-IV is planned following PhysioNet credentialing. Source code and experimental protocols are provided in the accompanying repository.

</details>


### [11] [SKILL.state: Scalable Long-Horizon Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26263)
*Sanket Badhe,Priyanka Tiwari,Jonghyun Chung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SKILL.state is a runtime architecture that replaces conversational history with explicit, mutable execution state to reduce latency and context-poisoning in long-horizon LLM agent tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent runtimes suffer from latency degradation and context-poisoning failures over long horizons due to ever-growing conversational history, which continually appends observations, actions, and reasoning traces.

Method: SKILL.state replaces append-only conversational history with explicit, mutable execution state. At each step, the model receives only: 1) immutable skill specification, 2) current structured execution state, and 3) latest observation. Intermediate reasoning is discarded immediately after producing validated state updates.

Result: Across diverse datasets, models, and execution environments, SKILL.state improves task accuracy while substantially reducing cumulative token consumption compared to traditional conversational history approaches.

Conclusion: Explicit execution state is an effective and architecture-agnostic abstraction for scalable long-horizon agent skills, addressing the limitations of conversational history-based runtimes.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly act as autonomous agents executing complex, long-running procedural skills. Existing agent runtimes maintain execution by continually appending observations, actions, and intermediate reasoning traces to an ever-growing conversation history, causing latency degradation and context-poisoning failures over long horizons. We present SKILL.state, a runtime architecture that replaces append-only conversational history with an explicit, mutable execution state. At each execution step, the model receives only the immutable skill specification, the current structured execution state, and the latest observation. Intermediate reasoning is discarded immediately after producing a validated state update, preventing prompt growth with execution history. Across diverse datasets, models, and execution environments, SKILL.state improves task accuracy while substantially reducing cumulative token consumption. Our results demonstrate that explicit execution state is an effective and architecture-agnostic abstraction for scalable long-horizon agent skills.

</details>


### [12] [Leveraging Large Language Models for Systematic Literature Review of Disease Spread Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26150)
*Orhan Yagizer Cinar,Timur Emre Ozkose,Emma Von Hoene,Amira Roess,Taylor Anderson,Hamdi Kavak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM pipeline for extracting info from 536 agent-based modeling papers shows 77.95% accuracy for GPT-4.1 and 81.67% for GPT-5.0, with field-level performance varying from 32.40% to 100.00%. LLM agreement can indicate output quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Language Models (LLMs) present new opportunities to streamline and potentially automate systematic literature reviews (SLRs), particularly in research domains like agent-based modeling where processing large numbers of papers is time-consuming.

Method: Developed an LLM pipeline to extract model-relevant information from 536 peer-reviewed agent-based modeling papers, then compared the results with a human-conducted systematic literature review to assess accuracy and reliability.

Result: Paper-level accuracy: 77.95% for GPT-4.1, 81.67% for GPT-5.0. Field-level accuracy varied widely (32.40% to 100.00%), with more complex/subjective fields performing worse. Found that LLM agreement can indicate quality - low agreement may signal hallucinations, while high agreement with low accuracy may indicate human dataset errors.

Conclusion: LLMs show potential for automating systematic literature reviews in modeling and simulation, but limitations exist, especially for complex/subjective fields. The study provides practical insights for prompt development and highlights that LLM agreement can serve as a quality indicator, though human expertise remains important.

Abstract: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have created new opportunities to streamline and potentially automate many research processes, including systematic literature reviews (SLRs). This study reports an LLM pipeline development for extracting model-relevant information from 536 peer-reviewed agent-based modeling papers. We compare the results with those of a human-conducted SLR. Our results show paper-level accuracies of approximately 77.95% for GPT-4.1 and 81.67% for GPT-5.0. Field-level accuracy ranges from 32.40% to 100.00%, with more complex or subjective fields performing less reliably. Importantly, we find that agreement between LLMs is a potential indicator of output quality: low agreement may signal hallucinations, whereas high agreement combined with low accuracy may point to noise or errors in the human dataset. Overall, our study provides practical insights into prompt development and highlights both the potential and limitations of using LLMs for full-scale SLRs in the modeling and simulation domain.

</details>


### [13] [SIGMA: Structured Noise-Effect-Aware Grouped Multi-Agent Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26683)
*Li Mingqian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIGMA is a hierarchical MARL framework that exploits cooperation structures to improve robustness under noisy observations by organizing agents into adaptive groups, performing intra-group consensus, and using inter-group attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current robust MARL methods don't properly handle structured noise effects - where observation disturbances create locally correlated but globally heterogeneous impacts on cooperative decision-making, influenced by underlying task cooperation structures.

Method: SIGMA uses density-based grouping to organize agents into adaptive local structures, performs intra-group consensus aggregation to smooth agent-specific deviations while preserving shared information, and employs inter-group attention to integrate information across groups while accommodating heterogeneous contributions.

Result: Experiments on StarCraft II noisy-observation tasks validate the existence of structured noise effects and show SIGMA consistently improves robustness under observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

Conclusion: Exploiting cooperation structures through hierarchical organization (density-based grouping, intra-group consensus, inter-group attention) effectively addresses structured noise effects in MARL and improves robustness under noisy observations.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) faces significant challenges in maintaining robust coordination under noisy observations. Although observation disturbances are often introduced independently across agents, their downstream effects on cooperative decision-making can become structured through underlying cooperation structures. We characterize this phenomenon as structured noise effects, where noise-induced decision effects exhibit local correlation among agents with stronger task-related dependencies while remaining globally heterogeneous across different agents and local structures. Existing robust MARL methods, however, rarely explicitly characterize or exploit such structure-dependent noise effects. To address this limitation, we propose SIGMA, a hierarchical collaboration framework that exploits cooperation structures to learn robust representations under noisy observations. SIGMA first organizes agents into adaptive local structures through density-based grouping and performs intra-group consensus aggregation to preserve shared task-relevant information while smoothing agent-specific representation deviations. Inter-group attention then adaptively integrates information across different groups to preserve global coordination while accommodating their heterogeneous contributions. Experiments on noisy-observation tasks in StarCraft II empirically validate the structured noise effects and demonstrate that SIGMA consistently improves robustness under observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

</details>


### [14] [Explainable Artificial Intelligence for Customer Churn Prediction in Telecommunications: A Framework for CRM Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26151)
*Sandeep Gaddamwar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a churn prediction system that combines explainable AI (SHAP/LIME) with a CRM integration framework to enable actionable customer retention strategies, bridging the gap between predictive models and frontline CRM workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Telecom providers face significant revenue loss from customer churn (1.9% monthly in mature markets). While predictive models can identify at-risk customers, they're often excluded from CRM workflows because high-performing ensemble models are opaque - retention specialists can't design personalized interventions from probability scores alone without understanding why subscribers are at risk.

Method: Benchmarked four classifiers (Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, LightGBM) on IBM Telco Customer Churn data (7,043 records, 19 features) with SMOTE balancing. Applied SHAP for global feature importance and SHAP/LIME for instance-level explanations. Introduced a four-layer CRM integration architecture that converts risk scores into tiered segmentation, maps features to retention-action templates, and creates a feedback loop for retraining.

Result: Logistic Regression achieved strongest AUC-ROC (0.8411), LightGBM highest accuracy (78.42%), with all models within 0.011 AUC band. Global SHAP identified tenure, total charges, and month-to-month contracts as top churn signals. The CRM integration framework is projected to cut overall churn by 3.3-5.3 percentage points, preserving $199K-$319K per campaign cycle.

Conclusion: Explainable AI (SHAP/LIME) enables translation of model predictions into actionable CRM strategies. The proposed integration framework effectively bridges the gap between predictive models and frontline workflows, turning opaque predictions into personalized retention interventions with measurable financial impact.

Abstract: Subscriber attrition is a costly, persistent challenge for telecommunications providers, with monthly churn of roughly 1.9% in mature markets eroding billions in revenue annually. Predictive models can flag at-risk customers accurately, yet they are routinely excluded from frontline CRM workflows because high-performing ensemble and non-linear architectures are opaque: a retention specialist cannot design a personalised intervention from a probability score alone, without knowing why a subscriber is at risk. This paper addresses that gap. We benchmark four classifiers--Logistic Regression, Random Forest, XGBoost, and LightGBM--on the IBM Telco Customer Churn benchmark (7,043 records; 19 features; 26.5% churn, balanced to 50% via SMOTE on the training partition only). Logistic Regression attains the strongest AUC-ROC (0.8411) and LightGBM the highest accuracy (78.42%); all four fall within a 0.011 AUC band (0.831--0.841), and 5-fold cross-validation confirms the leading models are effectively tied. Explanations are delivered at two granularities: a global SHAP ranking identifying tenure, total charges, and month-to-month contract as the dominant churn signals, and instance-level SHAP and LIME decompositions that expose the drivers behind each prediction. Building on these outputs, we introduce a four-layer CRM integration architecture that converts risk scores and attribution vectors into tiered segmentation, maps top features to structured retention-action templates, and routes campaign outcomes into a retraining feedback loop. Targeting the highest-risk quintile is projected to cut overall churn by 3.3--5.3 percentage points, preserving an estimated $199K--$319K per campaign cycle.

</details>


### [15] [Decoupling Planning and Control for Instructable Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26788)
*Zineng Tang,Kelsey R. Allen,Sjoerd van Steenkiste,Ishita Dasgupta,Alane Suhr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Instruct-to-Act combines vision-language model planning with world-model controllers, enabling high-level instruction following with low-latency execution in unfamiliar environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current systems have complementary weaknesses: VLMs can plan but struggle with reliable, fast action execution in new environments, while world-model controllers excel at fast control but lack open-ended task guidance. The goal is to combine these strengths for robust embodied AI.

Method: The system trains a world-model controller to act autonomously at high frequency when conditioned on sparse, high-level text instructions from a VLM planner. They relabel controller policy rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize behavior-cloning with existing reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives.

Result: The approach consistently outperforms controller-only and direct VLM action-generation variants across seven embodied environments (including three multi-agent setups), preserves fast control, allows swapping different pretrained VLM planners without fine-tuning, and remains competitive with strong baselines on 6/7 tasks.

Conclusion: The decoupled architecture effectively combines the planning capabilities of VLMs with the execution capabilities of world-model controllers, creating a robust system for embodied AI that maintains fast control while enabling flexible high-level task guidance through language.

Abstract: Recent work shows that pre-trained, instruction-tuned vision-language models (VLMs) perform well at mapping from instructions and observations to high-level plans, but struggle to realize such plans as reliable low-latency action sequences in unfamiliar environments. At the same time, world-model controllers excel at fast observation-to-action control, but lack open-ended task guidance. In this work, we combine these strengths into a single system, Instruct-to-Act, where we train a world-model controller to act autonomously at high frequency when conditioned on sparse, higher-latency, and high-level text instructions generated by a VLM planner. To train controllers to be language-instructable, we relabel segments of controller policy rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize a behavior-cloning objective along with existing reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives. We evaluate our proposed approach across seven embodied environments, including three multi-agent environments where VLM planners coordinate through language while trained controllers serve as their actuators. Under matched observation and action spaces, our decoupled approach consistently outperforms controller-only and direct VLM action-generation variants, preserves fast control, and lets us swap in different pretrained VLM planners without fine-tuning, while remaining competitive with strong vision-language-action and multi-agent RL baselines on six of seven tasks.

</details>


### [16] [EEG-to-Report: An Annotation and Feature-Text Framework for Training Language Models on Clinical EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26153)
*Xuan-The Tran,Le Trung Kien Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEG-to-Report is a browser-based annotation framework that links EEG review with AI-ready datasets through feature-text pairing and includes an auto-report module using CNNs and LLMs for clinical report generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical EEG reporting is manual and time-consuming, and current EEG software ecosystems lack structured EEG-text supervision needed for training modern language models. Most toolboxes focus on visualization/preprocessing but don't support workflows for generating high-quality AI datasets.

Method: Developed EEG-to-Report, a browser-based annotation framework with: 1) multi-format EEG ingestion and channel standardization, 2) interactive viewer with multimodal annotation (typed text + voice notes), 3) feature extraction engine computing spectral, temporal, entropy, Hjorth, connectivity, and spike-related descriptors, 4) storage in portable JSON schema with aligned feature-text pairs, 5) auto-report module using ensemble of convolutional networks with large language model.

Result: The framework streamlines annotation workflows and produces editable draft reports for neurologist review. It creates aligned feature-text pairs designed to supervise multimodal EEG-language models, providing a reusable foundation for automated EEG reporting systems.

Conclusion: EEG-to-Report addresses the gap in EEG software ecosystems by providing a practical framework for generating AI-ready datasets and automated report drafting, potentially revolutionizing clinical EEG reporting workflows through structured annotation and multimodal AI integration.

Abstract: Clinical electroencephalography (EEG) reporting remains largely manual and time-consuming, and current EEG software ecosystems do not produce the structured EEG-text supervision needed for training modern language models. Most toolboxes focus on visualization or preprocessing, providing limited support for workflows that generate high-quality datasets for AI. We introduce EEG-to-Report, a browser-based annotation and feature-text framework that links routine EEG review with the construction of AI-ready datasets. The framework integrates multi-format EEG ingestion, channel standardization, and an interactive viewer with a multimodal annotation layer that combines typed text and transcribed voice notes. For each annotated segment, a feature extraction engine computes a standardized set of spectral, temporal, entropy, Hjorth, connectivity, and spike-related descriptors, stored alongside clinical descriptions in a portable JSON schema. This yields aligned feature-text pairs designed to supervise multimodal EEG-language models. The framework also includes an auto-report module that couples an ensemble of convolutional networks with a large language model to draft clinical narratives for neurologist review. Using pilot annotations, we describe how EEG-to-Report streamlines annotation workflows and produces editable draft reports, providing a reusable foundation for automated EEG reporting systems.

</details>


### [17] [LiveSim: Simulating Environment-Shaped Users in Multi-Agent Live-Stream Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26849)
*Jiaqi Xu,Yiran Qiao,Jing Chen,Qiwei Zhong,Xiang Ao,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveSim: An LLM-based framework for live-stream ecosystem simulation that refines user behavior through trajectory-grounded interactions and learns transferable environment-behavior patterns from discrepancies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing simulators use static user profiles from historical data, which are inadequate for socially intensive environments like live streaming where interaction dynamics continuously reshape user behavior. Current approaches fail to capture the evolving nature of user behavior in dynamic social ecosystems.

Method: Proposes LiveSim framework that represents users as editable behavioral hypotheses. Refines these hypotheses through trajectory-grounded interactions, where discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories reveal missing environmental shaping effects. Extracts these signals as transferable environment-behavior patterns and accumulates them in a collective behavioral memory for improved simulation fidelity.

Result: Experiments on real-world live-stream risk-control data validate LiveSim's effectiveness in improving user-level behavioral fidelity. The framework enables ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and platform intervention effects in live-streaming environments.

Conclusion: LiveSim advances multi-agent ecosystem simulation for socially intensive environments by moving beyond static user profiles to dynamic, trajectory-grounded refinement of behavior hypotheses. The approach enables more accurate simulation of live-stream ecosystems and supports practical applications like risk evolution analysis and intervention evaluation.

Abstract: User behavior simulation with large language models~(LLMs) is increasingly used to support multi-agent ecosystem simulation. Existing simulators typically rely on static user profiles inferred from historical observations, which become inadequate in socially intensive environments such as live streaming where interaction dynamics continuously reshape user behavior. We propose \textbf{LiveSim}, an LLM-based framework for live-stream ecosystem simulation. It represents users as editable behavioral hypotheses and progressively refines them through trajectory-grounded interactions, where discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories reveal missing environmental shaping effects. These signals are further extracted as transferable environment-behavior patterns and accumulated in a collective behavioral memory to improve user-level behavioral fidelity and support ecosystem-level simulation. Experiments on real-world live-stream risk-control data validate the effectiveness of LiveSim in improving user-level behavioral fidelity and enabling ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and platform intervention effects.

</details>


### [18] [Selection Bias Correction in Retail Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26156)
*Spandan Ghose Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In retail inflation estimation, stratification outperforms weighting methods when dealing with long-tail distributions where selection probabilities vary dramatically, due to violations of the Positivity Assumption in causal inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail intelligence typically focuses on popular products, potentially biasing economic indicators by ignoring the "long tail" of niche items, leading to selection bias in inflation estimation.

Method: Used 400 Monte Carlo simulations across four data scenarios (aligned step functions, smooth gradients, misaligned breaks, polynomial relationships) to compare Inverse Probability Weighting (IPW) with five specifications against stratification with varying strata counts.

Result: Stratification achieved superior performance in 3 of 4 scenarios, with sub-0.04pp median error even with misaligned boundaries (116x advantage over IPW). IPW with spline propensity models performed better only under smooth polynomial relationships. Oracle IPW with perfect knowledge still had 6.06pp error vs. 0.008pp for stratification in step-function scenarios due to Positivity Assumption violations.

Conclusion: Stratification provides a safer engineering choice for retail long-tail distributions with severe positivity violations when selection probabilities differ dramatically (e.g., 90% vs. 1%), as weighting methods operate outside their theoretical design envelope in such contexts.

Abstract: Retail intelligence often relies on monitoring popular, high-velocity products, potentially biasing economic indicators by ignoring the "long tail" of niche items. This simulation study investigates selection bias in inflation estimation and compares correction methods across diverse data-generating processes. Through 400 Monte Carlo replications spanning four scenarios--aligned step functions, smooth gradients, misaligned breaks, and polynomial relationships--we test the robustness of Inverse Probability Weighting (IPW) with five specifications against stratification with varying strata counts. Our findings reveal fundamental limits of weighting methods in retail long-tail contexts: stratification achieves superior performance in three of four scenarios, maintaining sub-0.04pp median error even when boundaries deliberately misalign with population breaks (116x advantage over IPW). However, IPW with spline propensity models wins under smooth polynomial relationships (median error 0.007pp vs. 0.013pp), demonstrating context-dependency. Critically, even an oracle IPW specification with perfect structural knowledge achieves 6.06pp error compared to stratification's 0.008pp in step-function scenarios. This reflects violation of the Positivity Assumption--a fundamental causal inference requirement--rather than IPW methodological inferiority. When selection probabilities differ dramatically (90% vs. 1%), weighting methods operate outside their theoretical design envelope. These results demonstrate that stratification provides a safer engineering choice in retail long-tail distributions with severe positivity violations.

</details>


### [19] [AI agents in Algorithmic Electricity Markets: On the Emergence of Tacit Collusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26896)
*Jakub Seredyński,Georgios Tsaousoglou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Learning-based bidding agents in electricity markets can learn to collude tacitly without explicit instructions to do so, raising concerns about algorithmic market manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing adoption of learning-based agents for bidding strategies in electricity markets creates algorithmic markets. Previous evidence from algorithmic markets in other domains shows that independent learning can lead to tacit collusion. Electricity markets are particularly vulnerable due to their oligopolistic nature and repeated interactions among a small number of participants, making them structurally susceptible to non-competitive behavior.

Method: The paper models strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring and uses multi-agent reinforcement learning to simulate participants' emergent behavior. The researchers propose a multi-dimensional set of criteria (going beyond simple profit comparisons against Nash equilibria) to assess whether resulting behavior constitutes tacit collusion.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the danger of tacit collusion is realistic for electricity markets. There are cases where learning agents do sustain supra-competitive outcomes that support tacit collusion indicators, even though the agents were never explicitly instructed to collude.

Conclusion: The research validates the hypothesis that tacit collusion can emerge in electricity markets where participants use autonomous learning-based algorithms. This highlights a significant regulatory concern as algorithmic trading becomes more prevalent in energy markets, suggesting that current market monitoring approaches may need to adapt to detect and prevent such emergent collusive behavior.

Abstract: As electricity market participants increasingly adopt learning-based agents for their bidding strategies, electricity markets are becoming algorithmic. Evidence from algorithmic markets in other domains shows that tacit collusion can arise purely through independent learning. Moreover, electricity markets are typically oligopolistic and feature repeated interaction among a small number of participants, making them structurally susceptible to non-competitive behavior. In the face of these observations, this paper investigates the hypothesis that tacit collusion may emerge in electricity markets where participants' actions are controlled by autonomous learning-based algorithms. We model strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring, and model the participants' emergent behavior using multi-agent reinforcement learning. We propose a multi-dimensional set of criteria (going beyond profit comparisons against Nash equilibria) to assess whether the resulting behavior constitutes tacit collusion. Our experimental results showcase that such a danger is realistic for electricity markets: there are cases where agents do learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes that are supportive of tacit collusion indicators, even though the agents were never instructed to collude.

</details>


### [20] [GROUND: Reducing Hallucinations in LLM-Based Enterprise Analytics Through Governed Semantic Definitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26157)
*Aravind Sasidharan Pillai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GROUND is a framework that constrains LLM-generated analytics to a governed semantic layer, preventing hallucinations and ensuring data security by validating SQL against business rules before execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production use of natural-language analytics over enterprise data warehouses is limited by problems like hallucinated metrics, invalid joins, wrong grain, unsafe data access, and unsupported explanations. Existing text-to-SQL systems don't adequately address enterprise requirements for governed business semantics like approved metrics, dimensions, join paths, filters, and row-level security.

Method: GROUND (Governed Retrieval Over Unified Normalized Definitions) is a framework that constrains LLM-generated analytics to a governed semantic layer. It supplies approved definitions, binds user intent to governed metrics and dimensions, and validates generated SQL against schema, metric, join, grain, filter, security, and cost rules before execution, retrying or abstaining on violations.

Result: In a 100-question synthetic benchmark, GROUND was the only system free of measured hallucinations across all six evaluated categories, while ungoverned systems violated row-level security. A semantic-only condition with exact metric definitions but no access policy still leaked data. Results replicated on real U.S. NHTSA vehicle-safety data show GROUND's enforced guarantees (filters and row-level security) hold with zero violations across all models tested.

Conclusion: Governance cannot be replaced by metric fidelity alone; a comprehensive framework like GROUND that enforces business rules throughout the analytics generation process is necessary for safe, reliable enterprise reporting. The system's rule-based validations provide reliable guarantees for security-critical aspects while leaving judgment-dependent behaviors fallible.

Abstract: Natural-language analytics over enterprise data warehouses is increasingly important, but production use is limited by hallucinated metrics, invalid joins, wrong grain, unsafe data access, and unsupported explanations. Existing text-to-SQL systems often ground generation in database schemas or retrieved documentation, while enterprise reporting also requires governed business semantics: approved metrics, dimensions, join paths, filters, and row-level security. This paper introduces GROUND, Governed Retrieval Over Unified Normalized Definitions, a framework that constrains LLM-generated analytics to a governed semantic layer. GROUND supplies approved definitions, binds user intent to governed metrics and dimensions, and validates generated SQL against schema, metric, join, grain, filter, security, and cost rules before execution. On violations, it retries or abstains.
  In a 100-question synthetic enterprise-reporting benchmark, GROUND is compared with direct schema-only text-to-SQL, schema-RAG, and semantic-only grounding under one shared model. GROUND is the only system free of measured hallucinations across all six evaluated categories, while ungoverned systems violate row-level security on many questions. A semantic-only condition with exact metric definitions but no access policy still leaks data, showing that governance cannot be replaced by metric fidelity alone. The findings are replicated on real U.S. NHTSA vehicle-safety data with independent hand-authored gold and tested on an adversarial set across four models from three providers. GROUND's enforced guarantees, especially filters and row-level security, hold with zero violations on every model, while judgment-dependent behaviors such as refusing undefined metrics remain fallible.

</details>


### [21] [DSA: Evidence-Aware LLM-Agent Orchestration for Multi-Market Stock Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26990)
*Linsen Zhu,Yi Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DSA is an evidence-aware orchestration framework for multi-market stock research using LLM agents, featuring structured workflow organization, evidence acquisition, model-routed analysis, and risk safeguards for generating financial reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs can summarize financial information but lack operational systems to assemble heterogeneous evidence, expose unavailable data, control how generated opinions affect final reports, and provide proper safeguards for stock research.

Method: DSA organizes workflow into: evidence acquisition, structured context construction, model-routed analysis, optional role and Strategy Skill reasoning, and report generation with selected context and diagnostics. It uses two profiles (default and agentic) with shared evidence/model services but profile-specific validation and risk safeguards. Agentic profile adds role-specific parsers and signal-eligibility partitions for Strategy Skill opinions.

Result: Reference implementation includes six regional market paths, fifteen Strategy Skills, multiple model routes, and execution surfaces. At frozen snapshot: 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests passed, with 596 cases mapped to six contract families central to the LLM-agent architecture, establishing implementation conformance.

Conclusion: DSA provides a systematic framework for operational stock research with LLM agents, focusing on evidence assembly, structured workflows, and risk controls. The validation confirms implementation conformance but doesn't claim superior report quality, forecasting accuracy, or investment returns.

Abstract: Large language models can summarize financial information, but an operational stock-research system must first assemble heterogeneous evidence, expose unavailable data and model capabilities, and control how generated opinions affect a final report. We present DSA, an evidence-aware orchestration framework for multi-market stock research with large language model (LLM) agents. DSA organizes the workflow into evidence acquisition, structured context construction, model-routed analysis, optional role and Strategy Skill reasoning, and report generation with selected context and diagnostics. A default report profile and an optional agentic profile share evidence and model-routing services but use profile-specific output validation and risk safeguards. In the agentic profile, core role outputs are processed by role-specific parsers, whereas Strategy Skill opinions undergo an additional signal-eligibility partition before synthesis; disagreement is supplied explicitly to the decision agent, followed by a conservative risk override. The reference implementation includes six regional market paths, fifteen bundled Strategy Skills, hosted and local model routes, and multiple execution and delivery surfaces. At a frozen software snapshot, a selected manifest of 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests passed; 596 cases were retrospectively mapped to six contract families central to the reported LLM-agent architecture. This evidence establishes implementation conformance for the tested software contracts, not superior report quality, forecasting accuracy, or investment returns.

</details>


### [22] [SAREF-based Ontology for Distributed AI Workflows across the Edge-Fog-Cloud Continuum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26160)
*Viorica Rozina Chifu,Tudor Cioara,Vasile Ofrim,Liana Toderean,Ionut Anghel,Laura Daniele,Cornelis Bouter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a SAREF-compliant ontology for representing distributed AI workflows across edge-fog-cloud environments, enabling semantic interoperability, automated reasoning, and resource-aware orchestration with high deployment success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current semantic models have limited support for representing distributed AI workflows across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. Incompatible semantic representations affect interoperability, orchestration, and reuse of AI processes and resources.

Method: The authors extend the SAREF4SYST ontology with concepts for modeling AI pipelines, executable AI jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints, and communication relationships. They create a unified semantic model for both AI workflows and heterogeneous computing infrastructures.

Result: The ontology enables semantic interoperability and resource-aware orchestration. Evaluation shows deployment success rates of 90-100% with average orchestration decision times below 80 ms across heterogeneous edge-fog-cloud environments. All competency questions were successfully validated using SPARQL querying and semantic reasoning.

Conclusion: The proposed SAREF-compliant ontology effectively addresses interoperability challenges for distributed AI workflows across edge-fog-cloud continuum, providing automated reasoning capabilities while remaining fully aligned with the ETSI SAREF ecosystem.

Abstract: Nowadays semantic models provide limited support for representing distributed AI workflows and their execution across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. Therefore, AI processes and resources are often described using incompatible semantic representations, affecting the interoperability, orchestration, and reuse. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a SAREF-compliant ontology for representing distributed AI workflows across the edge-fog-cloud continuum. We extend the SAREF4SYST ontology with concepts for modeling AI pipelines, executable AI jobs, computational resources, deployment constraints, and communication relationships, providing a unified semantic model of both AI workflows and heterogeneous computing infrastructures. The ontology enables semantic interoperability, automated reasoning, and resource-aware orchestration of distributed AI applications while remaining fully aligned with the ETSI SAREF ecosystem. The ontology is evaluated using proof-of-concept smart grid energy services orchestration scenarios and validated using competency questions showing its ability to support AI workflow deployment, execution reasoning, and workload adaptation across heterogeneous edge, fog, and cloud environments. All competency questions were successfully validated using SPARQL querying and semantic reasoning. Experimental results demonstrate deployment success rates of 90-100% with average orchestration decision times below 80 ms across heterogeneous edge-fog-cloud environments, highlighting its effectiveness on ensuring semantic interoperability for distributed AI orchestration.

</details>


### [23] [A Contract-Centered Architecture for Scalable and Manageable Agentic Runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27086)
*Yaxiao Liu,Pengbo Liu,Yiwen Liu,Yihua Guan,Zhenghe Hou,Jiaxing Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a contract-bounded runtime architecture for enterprise AI deployment with four responsibility objects (Skill, Harness, Scaffold, data substrate) and a falsifiable hypothesis about cost-aware capability-capacity separability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI deployment is a complex coordination problem across multiple organizational units. Current use-case benchmarks only measure individual task completion but don't address how capabilities, models, runtime mechanisms, capacity, and enterprise data should be managed together across the organization.

Method: The paper introduces four responsibility objects as shared organizational contracts: Skill (reusable capability asset), Harness (runtime compiler/governor), Scaffold (execution boundary/NFR owner), and an independent data substrate. The core runtime is A = <S, H, X>. The central hypothesis P1 about cost-aware capability-capacity separability is formalized with six design conditions as measured obligations.

Result: The paper proposes a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment design with balanced order, reset/washout, repeated seeds and failure regimes, and cluster-aware uncertainty. The experiment would yield a four-state verdict: supported, falsified, conditional-engineering, or inconclusive. No implementation, experiment, dataset, or measured results are reported.

Conclusion: The paper contributes a contract-bounded runtime architecture for enterprise AI, a source-preserving data substrate, and a falsifiable measurement protocol. The approach aims to provide organizational coordination mechanisms and verifiable contracts for managing AI capabilities and capacity changes in enterprise settings.

Abstract: Enterprise AI deployment is a coordination problem across business units, application and AI teams, testing, platform engineering, infrastructure, security, operations, and data governance. Use-case benchmarks show whether one agent completes one task, but not how changing capabilities, models, runtime mechanisms, capacity, and enterprise data should be owned, changed, admitted, or evidenced together.
  We present four responsibility objects as shared organizational contracts: Skill (reusable, versioned capability and workflow asset), Harness (runtime compiler and governor), Scaffold (execution/control boundary and NFR owner), and a stack-external data substrate under independent CIO-governed semantics and telemetry. The runtime core is A = <S, H, X>, with the data substrate outside that stack.
  The central contribution is one bounded, falsifiable hypothesis, P1 (cost-aware capability-capacity separability): within a declared operating region, changing activated capability preserves the capacity-response interaction within a preregistered equivalence margin, while changing compatible Scaffold capacity preserves capability semantics up to a non-inferiority margin, and the required controls stay within a declared enforcement budget. Six design conditions become measured obligations whose coverage, violations, uncertainty, cost, and exclusions determine whether P1 is decidable.
  We propose a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment (balanced order, reset/washout, repeated seeds and failure regimes, cluster-aware uncertainty) with a four-state verdict: supported, falsified, conditional-engineering, or inconclusive. This paper contributes a contract-bounded runtime architecture, a source-preserving data substrate, and a falsifiable measurement protocol. It reports no completed implementation, experiment, dataset, or measured result.

</details>


### [24] [A Safety-Gated Multimodal AI Backend for Mental-Health Support: Hierarchical State Representation, Conservative Risk Fusion, and Controlled Generation in Anian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26162)
*Lei Wang,Xiao Wang,Lei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Anian: A safety-gated multimodal AI system for perinatal mental-health support that blocks generative AI responses at moderate/high risk levels, using a 4-layer structured pipeline with risk fusion and safety gating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical mental-health support systems need to distinguish when supportive conversation is appropriate vs when free-form generation should be blocked to prevent harm in sensitive perinatal contexts.

Method: Modular pipeline places generative AI downstream of structured state representation, conservative risk fusion, and response gating. User text/voice transcripts mapped into 4 layers: L1 emotion states, L2 psychosocial constructs, L3 safety risk, L4 intervention routes. Uses highest-risk-priority fusion rule S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external). Blocks AI responses at moderate/high risk.

Result: Evaluation with 858,295 normalized records showed Micro-F1: 0.9604 for L1 emotion, 0.9144 for L2 psychosocial constructs, 0.9742 for L4 routing. In 233-sample safety stress test, L3 achieved high-risk recall of 1.0000. Findings support internal feasibility but not clinical validity.

Conclusion: Anian demonstrates internal feasibility of a safety-gated architecture for perinatal mental-health support but requires expert-reviewed and real-world validation to establish clinical validity, diagnostic accuracy, and real-world safety.

Abstract: Safety-critical mental-health support systems must distinguish when supportive conversation is appropriate from when free-form generation should be blocked. This paper presents Anian, a safety-gated multimodal AI backend for perinatal mental-health support and mindfulness-intervention routing. Anian is not intended to diagnose psychiatric conditions or replace clinical care or crisis intervention. Its modular pipeline places generative AI downstream of structured state representation, conservative risk fusion, and response gating. User text or voice-derived ASR transcripts are mapped into four linked layers: L1 emotion states, L2 psychosocial constructs, L3 safety risk, and L4 intervention routes. Local text- and rule-based safety evidence is fused with external voice-derived evidence using a highest-risk-priority rule, S_fusion = max(S_local, S_external). At moderate or high fused risk, ordinary AI-generated responses and text-to-speech delivery are blocked and replaced by fixed safety content and prompts for human support. An internal prototype evaluation used approximately 858,295 normalized records from public emotion, dialogue, mental-health-related, and Chinese dialogue corpora within a weak-label and rule-derived framework. Micro-F1 scores were 0.9604 for L1 emotion classification, 0.9144 for L2 psychosocial constructs, and 0.9742 for L4 routing. In a controlled safety stress test of 233 samples, the L3 rule engine achieved high-risk recall of 1.0000 within predefined scenarios. These findings support the internal feasibility of the label framework and gating logic but do not establish clinical validity, diagnostic accuracy, real-world safety, or effectiveness. We report the architecture, ontology, safety-fusion mechanism, prototype evaluation, error-analysis plan, and roadmap for expert-reviewed and real-world validation.
