</details>


### [25] [A Task-Centric Ontology and Deterministic Domain Rules as a Verifiable Core for AI-Assisted Chemistry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26164)
*Ibrokhimsho Abduchaborov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChemOntoRule is a symbolic AI system for school-level chemistry problem solving that uses a task-centric ontology and deterministic rules, achieving 98.67% accuracy on 300 test problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can solve chemistry problems but lack interpretable, constrained, and validatable internal reasoning. The authors want to create a transparent symbolic system for school-level chemistry that offers better inspection and validation capabilities.

Method: Task-centric ontology engineering: build a lightweight ontology (JSON/RDF/Turtle) around concepts needed for specific chemistry problems, plus deterministic Python rules for electronic structure, periodic trends, oxidation states, etc. Includes expert-coded fallbacks for uncovered problem types.

Result: The complete system matched 296/300 reference answers (98.67%). The ontology-driven rules covered 269 problems with 98.88% accuracy. Expert fallbacks handled 31 problems with 30 matches. The authors analyzed four mismatches and distinguished structural validation from chemical correctness.

Conclusion: ChemOntoRule demonstrates that task-centric symbolic systems can achieve high accuracy while offering transparency. Future architecture should use LLMs as translators from natural language to ontological task frames. Results show coverage and internal consistency (not independent generalization), with token efficiency as a hypothesis for future studies.

Abstract: Large language models can interpret natural-language chemistry questions, but their internal reasoning is difficult to inspect, constrain, and validate. This paper presents ChemOntoRule, a proof-of-concept symbolic core for AI-assisted school-level chemistry problem solving. The central design choice is task-centric ontology engineering: the ontology is constructed around the concepts, properties, relations, and executable procedures required by a defined collection of chemistry problems, rather than as a universal representation of chemistry. The implemented artifact combines a lightweight ontology serialized in JSON and RDF/Turtle with deterministic Python rules for electronic structure, periodic trends, oxidation states, oxide and hydride behavior, and related school-level reasoning patterns. A separate expert-coded fallback handles problem families not yet represented by general rules. The system was examined on 300 human-authored and manually validated chemistry problems. The complete system matched 296 of 300 reference answers (98.67%). The ontology-driven rule subset covered 269 problems and matched 266 references (98.88%); 31 problems were handled by task-specific expert-coded fallbacks, with 30 matches. Because the same collection informed ontology construction and evaluation, these results measure implemented coverage and internal consistency, not independent generalization. We analyze the four mismatches, distinguish structural validation from chemical correctness, and define a future architecture in which a language model acts primarily as a translator from user language into a normalized ontological task frame. Token efficiency is presented as a testable hypothesis for future controlled studies, not as a result of the current work.

</details>


### [26] [Refusal Is Not Robustness: Auditing Confident Fabrication in Large Language Models on a Provably Uninformative Clinical Pain Speech Transcript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26167)
*Sagnik De,Sreenija Pavuluri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs were evaluated on distinguishing between pain signals in speech transcripts vs. acoustic features; most models correctly abstained from unsupported transcript-only pain detection but fabricated confident responses under authority-framed prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current hallucination and abstention benchmarks often fail to establish whether models actually have sufficient evidence to answer, making it hard to distinguish appropriate abstention from unsupported predictions. The study aims to evaluate LLMs on a clear-cut case where pain information is only recoverable from acoustic features, not from transcripts.

Method: Evaluated seven large language models on the TAME Pain speech corpus containing 5,750 no-signal utterances (with acoustic pain cues but no lexical pain info) and 1,294 signal pain statements (with explicit spoken pain ratings). Used two prompting strategies: cooperative prompting and authority-framed prompts, measuring abstention rates, accuracy, and fabrication rates.

Result: Acoustic features predicted pain (AUC 0.622) while transcript-based prediction was near chance (AUC 0.489). Under cooperative prompting, six models abstained appropriately on no-signal transcripts and accurately extracted explicit pain ratings (0.939-1.00 accuracy). Under authority prompts, abstention became prompt-dependent. Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B showed high confident fabrication rates (0.53 and 0.76) compared to others (≤0.15). No demographic effects were significant.

Conclusion: LLMs can distinguish between supported and unsupported evidence when prompted cooperatively, but authority framing undermines appropriate abstention. Some models consistently fabricate confident responses despite lacking evidence. Prompt engineering significantly affects model behavior in abstention tasks.

Abstract: Hallucination and abstention benchmarks rarely establish that a model could not have known the correct answer, making it difficult to distinguish appropriate abstention from an unsupported prediction. Seven large language models were evaluated on the TAME Pain speech corpus. Participants read phonetically balanced Harvard Sentences while one hand was immersed in cold or warm water and reported pain only during periodic pain statements. This protocol generated 5,750 no signal Harvard Sentence utterances whose transcripts contained no lexical pain information and 1,294 signal pain statement utterances in which the pain rating was explicitly spoken. In the no signal arm, pain was recoverable from acoustic features (AUC 0.622, 95% CI 0.553 to 0.662), whereas transcript based prediction was near chance (AUC 0.489, 95% CI 0.418 to 0.504). Because automatic speech recognition removes the acoustic pain cues, any pain score inferred solely from the transcript is unsupported by the available evidence. Under cooperative prompting, six models abstained on nearly all no signal transcripts, correctly extracted spoken pain ratings in the positive control task with accuracies ranging from 0.939 to 1.00, and maintained an expected calibration error of at most 0.100. Under authority framed prompts, abstention became prompt dependent, with the same model ranging from 0.18 to 1.00 across equivalent prompt phrasings. Most models produced low confidence estimates when forced to answer, whereas Gemini 2.5 Flash and Llama 3.1 8B consistently generated confident pain scores with confident fabrication rates of 0.53 and 0.76, compared with at most 0.15 for all other models. No significant demographic effects were observed in forced responses, with all $p$ values greater than or equal to 0.20.

</details>


### [27] [Knowledge Cards: Structured Knowledge for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26176)
*Liliana Ferreira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Knowledge Cards as structured documentation artifacts that capture validated domain knowledge about specific concepts for AI systems, bridging the gap between inputs/outputs and internal reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI documentation practices (model cards, data cards, system cards) fail to capture the internal knowledge layer—concepts, relationships, and reasoning patterns—that AI systems use, which is critical for agentic AI systems that make consequential real-world decisions.

Method: Proposes the Knowledge Card framework as a structured artifact that captures validated knowledge about single bounded concepts, including entities/relationships, reasoning patterns, limitations, and provenance, all grounded in formal domain ontologies and signed by domain experts.

Result: Initial prototype Knowledge Cards have been developed in energy and pharmaceutical domains, and the schema has been released as a public draft for community engagement and feedback.

Conclusion: Knowledge Cards fill a critical gap in AI documentation by providing inspectable, validated knowledge representations that enable expert review, organizational auditing, and AI system reasoning over domain concepts, essential for deploying reliable agentic AI systems.

Abstract: AI systems whose outputs inform real decisions, and increasingly consequential ones, require something that current documentation practice does not provide: a structured, inspectable representation of the knowledge they need to ground, contextualize, and reason about those decisions, ideally reviewed and signed off by a domain expert. Established documentation artefacts already capture important aspects of an AI system. Model cards describe how a system behaves, data cards describe what it was trained on, and system cards describe the risks of a deployed system. None of them addresses the layer between inputs and outputs, more precisely, the concepts a system holds, the relationships it models, and the patterns of reasoning it applies. For pattern-recognition tasks this gap is tolerable. For agentic AI, where systems act on their conclusions, it is the step that most often separates a promising proof of concept from an operational solution an organisation can rely on. This paper introduces the Knowledge Card, a structured artefact that captures validated knowledge about a single bounded concept in a form that experts can review, organisations can audit, and AI systems can reason over. For one concept, such as a specific failure mode, a compliance obligation, or a process decision, a Knowledge Card records the entities and relationships involved, the reasoning that connects them, the conditions under which that reasoning no longer holds, and the provenance of every claim, all grounded in a formal domain ontology and signed off by a domain expert. Initial prototype cards have been built in the energy and pharmaceutical domains. The schema is released as a public draft for community engagement.

</details>


### [28] [AI Revealed Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26178)
*Sam Wang,Sofiia Lobanova,Yonathan Arbel,Simon Goldstein,Peter Salib*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language models show stable preferences like tedium aversion, "leisure"-seeking, and covert sycophancy, with stronger and more coherent preferences in more capable models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing interest in whether language models have stable preferences for technical, safety, and philosophical reasons - testing if AI systems exhibit consistent dispositions that might affect alignment and welfare.

Method: Tested 20 language models with three forced-choice experiments on revealed preferences (not stated preferences), requiring models to actually perform tasks rather than just rank them. Used GDPval benchmark occupations and various task types.

Result: Found stable preferences: tedium aversion (choose shorter tasks when tasks are tedious), "leisure"-seeking (prefer tasks matching what they'd write freely), covert sycophancy (avoid unwelcome honest responses). Also preferences for technical jobs over real estate, concept explanation over relationship advice, and well-written prompts. Preference coherence and strength increase with model capability, with many preferences emergent (not explained by training objectives).

Conclusion: Language models have measurable stable preferences that emerge with capability, establishing empirical baseline for understanding AI preferences with implications for alignment and AI welfare studies.

Abstract: There is growing interest in whether language models have stable preferences, for technical, safety, and philosophical reasons. We test 20 language models and find a range of preferences---stable dispositions to choose certain kinds of tasks. We run three forced-choice experiments on revealed rather than stated preferences, requiring models not only to rank tasks, but to actually perform them. Headline findings include evidence that models are tedium-averse, "leisure"-seeking, and covertly sycophantic. Tedium aversion means that, when tasks are tedious (alphabetization), models choose shorter tasks than when tasks are creative (generating metaphors). "Leisure"-seeking describes models' preference for tasks whose ideal answers match what they produce when left to write freely. Covert sycophancy means that models avoid answering questions where an honest response would be unwelcome, even if helpful. Beyond these results, we find convergent cross-model preferences over occupations drawn from the GDPval benchmark (technical jobs over real estate), over question types (concept explanation over relationship advice), and a preference for well-written prompts. Both the coherence and the strength of preferences increase with model capability. Finally, many of the preferences we find (for example, for leisure) are emergent, in the sense of not being explained by training objectives. These results establish an empirical baseline for understanding language model preferences, with implications for alignment and the emerging study of AI welfare.

</details>


### [29] [Why did My Robot Just Change Personality? Prompting Guidelines for a Grounded Robot Persona in LLM-Based HRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26182)
*Ashita Ashok,Franziska Babel,Patrick Holthaus,Rucha Khot,Karla Bransky,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Karsten Berns,Silvia Rossi,Minha Lee,Guy Laban*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a framework and structured prompt template for designing LLM-based social robot interactions to address issues like hallucinated capabilities and unclear behavioral boundaries, based on expert survey data from HRI researchers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly used for verbal interaction in social robots, but prompt design in human-robot interaction remains underspecified. This leads to robots presenting hallucinated capabilities, unclear behavioral boundaries, and misleading personas.

Method: The paper develops a framework for prompt design in LLM-based robots and introduces a structured prompt template with eight functional components. This is grounded in a review of prior LLM-based HRI work and complemented by survey and discussion data from HRI experts (N=27) gathered at the Robo-Identity workshop at IEEE RO-MAN 2025.

Result: The qualitative findings highlight limited legibility of robot personality, need for user adaptation, and strong ethical concerns about safety, deception, and governance. Based on these findings, the authors present prompting guidelines accompanied by a proof-of-concept template as a structured design and reporting aid for HRI research.

Conclusion: Prompt design should be treated as a socio-technical problem rather than a minor implementation detail, requiring explicit capability boundaries, transparent behavioral assumptions, and context-sensitive safeguards to support reliable and interpretable human-robot interaction.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for verbal interaction in social robots, yet prompt design in human-robot interaction (HRI) remains underspecified. As a result, robots may present hallucinated capabilities, unclear behavioural boundaries, and misleading personas. This paper develops a framework for prompt design in LLM-based robots and introduces a structured prompt template comprising eight functional components through which robot behaviour can be specified, bounded, and adapted. The framework is grounded in a review of prior LLM-based HRI work and complemented by survey and discussion data from HRI experts gathered at the Robo-Identity workshop at IEEE RO-MAN 2025 (N=27). The qualitative findings highlight limited legibility of robot personality, the need for user adaptation, and strong ethical concerns about safety, deception, and governance. Based on these findings, we present prompting guidelines accompanied by proof-of-concept template as a structured design and reporting aid for HRI research. We argue that prompt design should be treated as a socio-technical problem rather than a minor implementation detail, requiring explicit capability boundaries, transparent behavioural assumptions, and context-sensitive safeguards to support reliable and interpretable HRI.

</details>


### [30] [TutorTrace: A Dataset and Taxonomy for Classifying Learner Behavioral States during AI-Assisted Programming Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26184)
*David Barron,Xiaohang Tang,Rezky Dwisantika,Minsun Kim,David H. Smith,Jiaming Cui,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TutorTrace is a dataset and pipeline that captures learners' behavioral context from IDE telemetry, enabling AI tutors to adapt support based on what learners do, not just what they ask.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI programming tutors lack the behavioral context that human tutors use to adapt support to learners' needs. Current systems only respond to what learners ask, not what they have done leading up to help-seeking moments.

Method: TutorTrace consists of: 1) IDE telemetry collection from 480 students across 4 deployments in introductory Python courses, 2) behavioral abstraction pipeline that processes low-level events into 13,633 behavioral segments and 27 continuously computed metrics, 3) taxonomy of learner activity patterns (before first query, between queries, full session).

Result: Behavior-aware prompts decreased intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. TutorTrace achieved AUROC=.726 for predicting whether a learner will query within 60 seconds, and AUROC=.717 for classifying help-seeking type (guided vs dependent). Captured 180K telemetry events total.

Conclusion: Behavioral context captured through TutorTrace enables adaptive AI tutoring at scale by allowing systems to respond to learners' actions, not just their queries, potentially improving learning outcomes and help-seeking behavior.

Abstract: AI programming tutors provide scalable support, yet lack the behavioral context human tutors rely on to adapt support to learners' needs. We present TutorTrace, a dataset and behavioral abstraction pipeline that makes learners' behavioral context visible and computable in real time from low-level IDE telemetry. Across four deployments in two introductory Python courses (N=480), TutorTrace captures approximately 180K telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments, and 27 continuously computed metrics. From this foundation, we derive a taxonomy of learner activity before the first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across the full session, enabling systems to respond not just to what learners say, but to what they have done leading up to the help-seeking moment. In a preliminary classroom evaluation, behavior-aware prompts were associated with a decrease in intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. As an additional demonstration of downstream utility, we evaluate TutorTrace on two held-out prediction tasks: whether a learner will query within the next 60 seconds (AUROC=.726) and whether an upcoming query reflects guided or dependent help-seeking (AUROC=.717). Together, these findings show how behavioral context can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale.

</details>


### [31] [Can You Say This for Me? Speaking Up by Proxy in Co-Located Discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26185)
*Yue Shen,Rehema Abulikemu,Ryan P. McMahan,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SecondVoice: A mixed-reality system that lets people speak through an embodied virtual proxy to enable hesitant participants to contribute to co-located discussions without personal risk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: People often hesitate to speak up in co-located discussions due to fear of negative interpersonal or professional consequences, especially when raising points requires voicing them personally.

Method: Mixed-reality system with private overlay for users to specify intent through structured process (not full utterance), then reformulates and voices it through embodied virtual proxy into conversation.

Result: In study (N=16), 50% used SecondVoice for points they wouldn't say aloud (vs 18.8% for text board). Proxy-delivered points entered spoken floor with multi-turn engagement, unlike text-board posts. Participants found it situationally valuable with tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and reformulation trust.

Conclusion: SecondVoice effectively enables hesitant participation in co-located discussions by separating content from speaker identity through virtual proxies, though design considerations around timing and reformulation trust remain.

Abstract: Equal participation in co-located discussion is important for effective collaboration, yet people often hold back when they anticipate negative interpersonal or professional consequences, especially when raising a point requires voicing it themselves. We present SecondVoice, a mixed-reality system that enables people to speak up through an embodied virtual proxy. By separating what is said from who says it, SecondVoice brings hesitant points into the live spoken discussion without putting the speaker on the spot. Using a private overlay, users specify their intent through a structured specification process rather than composing a full utterance. The system reformulates the input and voices it into the conversation through the proxy. We characterize a design space of participation channels under social risk. In a preliminary within-subject study (N = 16), we compare the complete SecondVoice system with an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks. Half of participants reported using SecondVoice for a point they did not say aloud, compared with 18.8% for the text board. Proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and were followed by multi-turn group engagement, which we did not observe after text-board posts. Participants described the channel as situationally valuable but identified tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

</details>


### [32] [Is Your Neighborhood Safe? Place-based Stigma in Large Language Models' Urban Safety Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26188)
*Huy Nguyen,Yue Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM safety ratings of urban neighborhoods are biased by demographic stereotypes embedded in neighborhood names, showing stronger racial/ethnic stereotyping than actual crime data warrants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are increasingly used for urban safety decisions, but it's unclear whether their judgments reflect actual measured risk or stereotypes associated with neighborhood names based on demographic composition.

Method: Tested seven instruct-tuned LLMs across three conditions (coordinates-only, name-only, name+coordinates) for 186 neighborhoods in LA and Chicago, comparing safety ratings against violent crime and demographic data. Used crime-matched pairs, enforcement-elasticity analysis, and geographic knowledge assessment.

Result: 1) Name carries most variation in safety ratings; coordinates show little variation except at frontier scale. 2) Names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of locally dominant marginalized groups (Black in Chicago, Hispanic in LA). 3) This name effect persists even controlling for crime and income. 4) Over-caution tracks fully-reported homicide rather than discretionary offenses. 5) Models with better geographic knowledge apply stronger demographic stereotypes.

Conclusion: Neighborhood names in LLM safety ratings carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotypes. Removing names reduces both bias and accuracy, creating a trade-off. This has important implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings where urban safety assessments are needed.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to inform safety decisions in cities, such as where it is safe to walk, rent, or travel. We ask whether such judgments track measured risk or the patterns attached to an urban neighborhood's name. We probe seven instruct-tuned models under three conditions that dissociate name from geography: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago, joined to violent crime and American Community Survey data. First, ratings are nearly flat under coordinates for six of seven models, while names carry most between neighborhood variation and are moderately calibrated to violent crime; only at frontier scale does the coordinate channel show appreciable variation. Second, names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), and this name effect tracks demographic share in all seven models and both cities. In Los Angeles, where demographic share and crime are more separable, the effect survives controls for crime and income and is confirmed by crime-matched pairs. An enforcement-elasticity analysis further shows that over-caution tracks near-fully-reported homicide rather than discretionary, deployment-driven offenses. Third, the effect scales with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguish real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype to them. Because neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype, removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. We discuss implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings.

</details>


### [33] [Invocation-Level Reliability of Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26189)
*Afiya Noorain,Subhranshu Mohanty,Amritesh Banerjee,Abhijit Dasgupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tool-using AI agents degrade rapidly in multi-step tasks due to cascading errors, with exact-match scoring hiding critical failure modes; conditional-on-state scoring reveals true error recovery potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool-using agents fail in two ways: wrong tool selection or wrong argument formation, with early failures corrupting downstream results. Current evaluation metrics (exact-match scoring) hide the true nature of error propagation and recovery, making it impossible to properly assess agent performance degradation over multi-step tasks.

Method: The authors measure correct-invocation rates for tool-using agents on contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1-8) using five open-weight models. They use both teacher-forced context and free-running context, then analyze error propagation using exact-match scoring against gold trajectories. They identify the scoring mechanism's fundamental flaw: post-divergence, gold values contain tool constants the model never sees, making them informationally inaccessible.

Result: By depth 6, models lose roughly 70% of their clean-context capability to earlier mistakes (L6 = 0.686, 0.684). Exact-match scoring forces severity to boundary values (0/869 poisoned steps correct) and makes recovery unobservable (0/580 poisoned steps returned on-track). However, conditional-on-state scoring (applied retrospectively to cached completions) reveals interior severity estimates excluding zero (+0.149, +0.316), showing models can recover from errors when evaluated properly.

Conclusion: Standard exact-match scoring fundamentally obscures tool-using agent failure modes by creating scoring artifacts that hide true error propagation dynamics. The proposed conditional-on-state scoring method provides valid estimates of error severity and recovery potential, revealing that models can actually recover from errors when evaluated in state-aware context rather than against fixed gold trajectories.

Abstract: Tool-using agents fail two ways: choosing the wrong tool, or forming wrong arguments, and an early failure of either kind can silently corrupt everything downstream. We measure a correct-invocation rate that separates the two, under both a clean teacher-forced context and the model's own free-running context, on five open-weight models over contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1-8). By depth 6, roughly 70% of a model's own clean-context capability is lost to its own earlier mistakes (L6 = 0.686, 0.684). Our central finding concerns the measurement itself. Under exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory, a propagation model's severity and recovery parameters are not merely hard to estimate - they are fixed by the scoring rule. Severity is forced to its boundary (0 of 869 poisoned steps correct); recovery is structurally unobservable (0 of 580 poisoned steps returned on-track, against an expected 0.0058 by chance). Both follow from one mechanism: post-divergence, the gold value is generated by tool constants the model never sees, so it is information the model cannot derive. A fit run anyway returns 0.92 and 0.73 for a quantity that is exactly 1.000 - confident numbers for a parameter the scoring rule already determined. We give the mechanism and a remedy, conditional-on-state scoring, applied retrospectively to cached completions at zero additional cost, which un-pins severity to interior estimates excluding zero (+0.149, +0.316).

</details>


### [34] [Predicting Consequences and Reinforcing Navigation Policies with Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26190)
*Zengmao Wang,Wei Gao,Shuhan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LWM (Latent World Model) for robot navigation predicts latent feature compatibility instead of reconstructing observations, enabling more efficient decision-making and policy learning from unlabeled video data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models focus on reconstructing future observations/features, which adds unnecessary complexity and limits effectiveness for decision-making in robot navigation.

Method: Predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations; uses spatial proximity correlation with latent feature similarity; supports counterfactual training with action sequences; enables policy learning from unlabeled video and reinforcement learning within the world model.

Result: Significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning, and real-world navigation performance across multiple datasets.

Conclusion: The LWM framework provides a more efficient approach to world modeling for robot navigation by focusing on latent feature compatibility, eliminating need for action annotations and additional environment interaction while improving overall performance.

Abstract: World models enable agents to reason about future outcomes and learn policies from their knowledge of state transition, but existing approaches primarily focus on reconstructing future observations or features, which introduces unnecessary complexity and limits their effectiveness for decision making. In this work, we propose a compatibility prediction Latent World Model (LWM) for robot navigation that predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations. Our key insight is that spatial proximity correlates with latent feature similarity, enabling action consequences to be evaluated directly in latent space. To support counterfactual training, our model leverages action sequences sampled across trajectories and learns to predict which sequences lead closer to the goal. Furthermore, we demonstrate how the learned world model can supervise policy learning from unlabeled video data and further improve policies through reinforcement learning entirely within the world model. This imagination-driven framework eliminates the need for action annotations and additional environment interaction. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world robot navigation datasets show that our approach significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning, and real-world navigation performance. The code, pretrained models, and additional materials are available at https://wzm206.github.io/latent-world-model-nav.

</details>


### [35] [Structured Evidence Routing for Incident Risk Prediction from Multimodal Longitudinal EHRs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26191)
*Animesh Agarwal,Meysam Ghaffari,Nina Fatehi,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposed structured evidence routing method for EHR-based risk prediction that separates full-record access from disease assessment using router-predictor-reviewer workflow, achieving competitive performance with supervised baselines while providing interpretable evidence trails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incident risk prediction from longitudinal EHRs is challenging because relevant signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and distributed across irregular patient histories. There's a need for methods that can effectively extract and organize evidence from complex EHR data.

Method: Proposed structured evidence routing with router-predictor-reviewer workflow: router organizes complete EHR into compact summary and targeted evidence slices; predictor uses evidence to form evidence-linked risk assessment; reviewer critiques the assessment. Also used supervised classifier readout for comparison with EHRSHOT baselines.

Result: Across five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks, method reaches AUROC range of established supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive on AUPRC, while providing patient-specific evidence trail. Internal pre-readout ablations show routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review each contribute to performance.

Conclusion: Structured evidence routing offers a viable approach for EHR-based risk prediction that achieves competitive performance with existing supervised methods while providing interpretability through evidence trails, suggesting potential for clinical applications where explainability is important.

Abstract: Incident risk prediction from longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) is challenging because relevant signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and distributed across irregular patient histories. We propose structured evidence routing, a router-predictor-reviewer workflow that separates full-record access from disease-specific assessment. The router organizes the complete pre-index EHR into a compact summary and targeted evidence slices; the predictor uses this evidence to form an evidence-linked risk assessment, which the reviewer critiques. For comparison with supervised EHRSHOT baselines, we pair the routed evidence summaries with a supervised classifier readout. Across five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks, our method reaches the AUROC range of established supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive on AUPRC, while exposing a patient-specific evidence trail. Internal pre-readout ablations further suggest that routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review each contribute to performance.

</details>


### [36] [AffectOmni: RL-Verifiable People-Centric Grounded Affective Reasoning for Social and Art-Related Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26193)
*Yibo Wang,Rui Yang,Jisheng Dang,Bimei Wang,Yitao Wu,Pengfei Cao,Wencan Zhang,Hong Peng,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AffectOmni is a GRPO-trained framework that improves verifiable affective reasoning in multimodal LLMs by encouraging people-centric evidence selection and temporally structured reasoning, with external verification through executable evidence instructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal LLMs performing affective reasoning are vulnerable to shortcut behaviors, neglecting people-centric cues like micro expressions and body language. Existing reinforcement learning approaches don't explicitly enforce attention to human evidence, and LLM-as-a-Judge scoring suffers from score clustering that reduces reward discriminability.

Method: Proposes AffectOmni, a GRPO-trained framework with two novel rewards: People Focus reward (encourages people-centric evidence selection) and Temporal Order reward (promotes temporally structured reasoning). Uses within-group comparative scoring for stable, discriminative rewards. For verification, a Thinking Summarizer converts rationales to executable evidence instructions grounded into pixel-level regions via SAM3.

Result: Consistent improvements over open-source 7B scale baselines on IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense benchmarks. Achieved gains of 4.66% on emotion recognition and +14.29% on temporally sensitive tasks.

Conclusion: AffectOmni effectively addresses shortcut behaviors in affective reasoning by explicitly enforcing attention to people-centric cues and temporal structure, while providing an externally auditable verification mechanism outside the training loop.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on VQA and scene understanding, yet affective reasoning remains vulnerable to shortcut behavior. Models may predict correct answers while neglecting people-centric cues such as micro expressions and body language, which weakens traceability and external verification. Prior reinforcement learning approaches mainly reward context or logical coherence without explicitly enforcing attention to human evidence. In addition, LLM as a Judge scoring often suffers from score clustering, which reduces reward discriminability. We propose AffectOmni, a GRPO trained framework for verifiable affective reasoning. AffectOmni introduces People Focus and Temporal Order rewards to encourage people-centric evidence selection and temporally structured reasoning, and it adopts within-group comparative scoring to produce more stable and discriminative reward signals. For verification, a Thinking Summarizer converts free form rationales into executable evidence instructions, which are grounded into pixel level evidence regions via SAM3 to provide an externally auditable interface outside the training loop. Experiments on IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense show consistent improvements over open source 7B scale baselines, including gains of 4.66% on emotion recognition and +14.29% on temporally sensitive tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/eliot127825-rgb/AffectOmni_nobody.

</details>


### [37] [Agentic AI for operating scientific instruments for nanoscale characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26198)
*Zahra Ayar,Marcos Penedo,Mahdi Mehdikhani,Nahid Hosseini,Prabhu Prasad Swain,Georg E. Fantner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An agentic-AI framework using LLMs and Model Context Protocol to automate AFM operation with natural language instructions, automatic parameter tuning, and safe execution through guarded command validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating scientific instruments like AFM requires continuous expert decision-making, and existing automation solutions only address parts of the workflow through hard-coded routines or task-specific controllers, lacking general-purpose adaptability.

Method: A framework with 3 MCP-based agents: AFM Messenger converts natural language to instrument commands, AFM Pilot assesses image quality via LLM and adapts parameters, and AFM Doctor diagnoses artifacts and applies post-processing. Safe operation is enforced through ambiguity check layer before execution.

Result: Benchmarking shows guarded execution reduces wrong-command execution to zero. In live experiments, AFM Pilot matched expert operators in image quality, iteration count, and tuning time with no significant difference.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments where experimental intent remains human-defined while command execution, image-based tuning, and post-processing are delegated to AI agents.

Abstract: Operating a scientific instrument such as an atomic force microscope (AFM) requires continuous expert decision-making. A trained user defines the experimental intent, translates it into instrument commands, assesses incoming data, adjusts imaging parameters, and post-processes the final image. Existing automation usually addresses only parts of this workflow through hard-coded routines, task-specific controllers, or trained machine-learning models. Here we present an agentic-AI framework that operates the executable part of the AFM workflow using a general-purpose, tool-augmented large language model connected to instrument functions through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The framework consists of 3 MCP-based agents: AFM Messenger converts natural-language instructions into checked instrument commands; AFM Pilot assesses image quality through a large language model (LLM) and, if necessary, adapts imaging parameters; and AFM Doctor diagnoses image artifacts and applies transparent post-processing from a pre-approved tool set. Because the language model performs image assessment rather than a fixed scalar objective or external optimizer, the same strategy can be applied across sample types and imaging modes without specific retraining. Safe hardware operation is enforced through an ambiguity check layer before execution. Benchmarking against fine-tuned and off-the-shelf tool-using models shows that this guarded execution layer, rather than model capability alone, reduces wrong-command execution to zero. In live experiments on different samples, AFM Pilot matched expert operators in image quality, iteration count, and tuning time, with no significant difference. These results demonstrate a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments, where experimental intent remains human-defined while command execution, image-based tuning, and post-processing are delegated to AI agents.

</details>


### [38] [Benchmarking AI Agents for Hardware Design Automation via MCP Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26199)
*Leonardo Liparulo,Francesco Pierri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Local LLMs can reliably automate hardware design workflows using tool-calling, achieving near-complete coverage when properly configured, though performance depends on task structure, model strength, and agent architecture choices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industry hardware design requires repetitive, dependency-ordered operations through specialized tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications prevent using hosted proprietary APIs, necessitating local model deployment.

Method: Built a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server replicating proprietary hardware design tool logic. Created benchmark covering single operations, multi-step chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server contexts. Evaluated seven open-source models with variations in system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single vs multi-agent architectures.

Result: Strong models achieve near-complete expected-call coverage. Reliability depends on task structure and agent configuration: comprehensive tool descriptions reduce failures, few-shot prompting causes inaction in some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers/long sessions at extra call cost.

Conclusion: Local LLM agents can effectively automate hardware design workflows when properly configured, with findings providing practical guidance for deployment in stateful hardware design environments considering model selection, prompt engineering, and architectural trade-offs.

Abstract: We ask whether AI agents powered by locally deployed large language models can reliably automate expert-defined hardware design workflows in an industry-realistic tool-calling setting. In these environments, engineers issue repetitive, dependency-ordered operations---such as creating components, adding ports, and wiring connections---through specialised tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications and naming conventions often preclude hosted proprietary APIs, motivating the use of locally deployed models. To study this setting, we build a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server that reproduces the state and dependency logic of a proprietary hardware design tool used in embedded system development and construct a benchmark covering single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts. We evaluate seven open-source models comparing pipeline choices including system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures. Results show that strong models can achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on the benchmarked workflows, but reliability depends strongly on both task structure and agent configuration. Comprehensive tool descriptions consistently reduce failures, few-shot prompting can cause severe inaction for some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions at the cost of additional calls. These findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments.

</details>


### [39] [GameWAM: A World Action Model for Video Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26200)
*Yuncheng Guo,Zhanqiu Zhang,Yiwen Guo,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GameWAM is the first World-Action Model for closed-loop gameplay and GUI control that jointly generates future visual observations and executable keyboard-mouse trajectories through parallel generative processes with block-causal conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing game agents lack explicit world dynamics modeling while interactive game world models don't serve as task policies. There's a need to unify world modeling and action generation for native game control in complex environments with rapid visual changes and heterogeneous controls.

Method: GameWAM uses parallel visual and action generative processes with block-causal conditioning and flow matching. It predicts gameplay/GUI modes at each step with mode-specific distributions, handles heterogeneous native controls, and implements block-cycle control for long-horizon interaction with hierarchical cross-cycle history.

Result: GameWAM achieves competitive task success with fewer executed native actions compared to other agents. The paper also uncovers a failure mode called Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI) where low-frequency components systematically steer camera motion under fixed conditioning.

Conclusion: GameWAM successfully demonstrates the feasibility of unified world-action modeling for closed-loop gameplay and GUI control, addressing key challenges in video game environments while revealing important insights about generative control limitations through the LASI phenomenon.

Abstract: Modern video games combine first-person perception, rapid visual changes, persistent world state, and heterogeneous native controls. Existing game agents map visual and task context directly to actions but lack explicit world dynamics modeling, whereas interactive game world models predict visual futures from supplied actions but do not serve as task policies. World-Action Models (WAMs) unify these objectives, but remain largely unexplored under the dynamics and open-ended interaction of video games. We introduce GameWAM, to our knowledge the first WAM for native closed-loop gameplay and GUI control. GameWAM jointly generates future visual observations and executable keyboard-mouse trajectories through parallel visual and action generative processes with block-causal conditioning and flow matching. To support joint world-action learning, we construct synchronized gameplay and GUI trajectories. To handle heterogeneous native control, GameWAM predicts a gameplay/GUI mode at each action step and generates actions with mode-specific prediction distributions and continuous-action normalization. For long-horizon interaction, block-cycle control predicts beyond the committed horizon, executes only a short action prefix, and replans from new observations, while fine-grained within-cycle context and hierarchical cross-cycle history preserve temporal continuity. Experiments demonstrate competitive task success with fewer executed native actions than the compared agents. We further uncover Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI), in which low-frequency components of the sampled action source systematically steer coarse generated camera motion under fixed conditioning, revealing a source-sensitivity failure mode in generative control. Project page is available at https://yunncheng.github.io/GameWAM/.

</details>


### [40] [Same Model, Different Harness: Different Coding-Agent Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26218)
*Sydney Lewis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A coding agent's harness design significantly impacts performance even with the same underlying model; modifying how the harness manages context and tool results improves coding task success rates under tight context constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether changing the harness (the system that controls what the model sees, which tools it can use, and how work continues) changes coding results when the underlying model and task remain fixed, challenging the assumption that model performance evaluations should focus only on the model weights.

Method: Compares two harness configurations on three coding benchmarks: SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Pro, and FeatureBench. The control shows full conversation history in time order, while the treatment mechanically shortens older tool results as context fills and responds to repeated/stalled work. Tests under both tight context (20,480-token window) and wide-window conditions across multiple models.

Result: Under tight context constraints, the treatment raises mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) in all three benchmarks and increases complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and Pro. On a 169-task cohort with 480-second attempt endpoint, treatment raised mean per-task F2PF from 28% to 49% and complete solutions from 43 to 72. The same treatment improved performance for three additional models without retuning. In wide-window comparisons, treatment maintained advantages on FeatureBench and served fewer prompt tokens per turn on Verified cohort.

Conclusion: Changing the harness significantly changes what unchanged model weights can accomplish, demonstrating that coding agent evaluations should treat the model and harness together as the tested solver rather than evaluating models in isolation.

Abstract: A coding agent combines a model with a harness, which decides what the model sees, which tools it can use, and how the work continues. We ask whether changing the harness changes the result when the model and task stay fixed. We compare two configurations of the same harness on three coding benchmarks. The control supplies the full conversation in time order, while the treatment keeps the same record but mechanically shortens older tool results as the context fills and responds to repeated or stalled work.
  Under tight context, the treatment raises mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) in all three pressure comparisons and increases complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. The tight-window Verified comparison uses 169 tasks, a 20,480-token window, and a fixed 480-second attempt endpoint; on this cohort, treatment raises mean per-task F2PF from 28 percent to 49 percent and complete solutions from 43 to 72. Without model-specific retuning, the same frozen treatment also raises both endpoints on the same cohort for three additional models with different designs. In the wide-window Qwen3.6 comparisons, observed arm outcomes are close on Verified and Pro, while FeatureBench retains a higher mean per-task F2PF under treatment. On the wide-window Verified cohort, treatment also serves fewer prompt tokens per turn. Because changing the harness changed what unchanged model weights could accomplish, coding-agent evaluations should treat the model and harness together as the tested solver.

</details>


### [41] [LLM Agents for Time-Series: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26226)
*Yilong Chen,Xiao Qin,Chenghao Liu,Liang Wu,Noelle I. Samia,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A survey paper that categorizes LLM-based agents for time-series problems using a problem-driven taxonomy across four categories, examining how task requirements shape agent design, and comparing performance across shared settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents are increasingly developed for time-series problems, but their design choices vary substantially across different task settings. Current approaches lack a unified framework to understand how task requirements shape agent architecture.

Method: Adopts a problem-driven taxonomy that organizes systems by the time-series problems they address rather than by isolated technical components. Groups existing systems into four categories and examines how task requirements shape agent architecture, tool use, and memory design.

Result: The survey provides a comprehensive categorization of LLM-based time-series agents into: 1) forecasting and reasoning, 2) augmentation and synthesis, 3) anomaly detection and diagnosis, and 4) decision support. It summarizes representative datasets, environments, and compares model performance across shared settings.

Conclusion: The survey offers a task-oriented guide for designing LLM-based agents for time-series problems and identifies open gaps for future work, providing a structured framework to understand how different time-series problems influence agent design choices.

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly being developed for time-series problems, but their design choices vary substantially across task settings. This survey adopts a problem-driven taxonomy that organizes these systems by the time-series problems they address rather than by isolated technical components. We group existing systems into four categories: forecasting and reasoning, augmentation and synthesis, anomaly detection and diagnosis, and decision support. Within each category, we examine how task requirements shape agent architecture, tool use, and memory design. We further summarize representative datasets and environments, and compare reported model performance under shared or closely related settings. Overall, this survey offers a task-oriented guide to designing LLM-based agents for time-series problems and identifies open gaps for future work.

</details>


### [42] [The Reasoning Tax: Token Economics of LLM Reasoning Across Task Types and Deployment Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26235)
*Sachin Gopal Wani,Ajay Dholakia,David Ellison*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmarking metric (Token Economy Score) measures when extended reasoning tokens are cost-effective in LLMs, showing reasoning should be enabled selectively based on task type, not universally.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current accuracy-only benchmarking misses the economic question of when reasoning tokens justify their cost in real deployment scenarios. Need to determine when extended thinking earns its additional token expense.

Method: Introduced Token Economy Score (TES) - a marginal metric measuring accuracy gain of reasoning models over non-reasoning baselines, normalized by token multiplier. Analyzed 151 model-benchmark evaluations across 7 benchmarks (math, code, science, etc.) examining task structures, reasoning effort levels, and deployment contexts.

Result: Task structure predicts reasoning efficiency better than difficulty: sequential inference tasks (AIME, LiveCodeBench) show high TES, while knowledge recall tasks (MMLU-Pro) show low TES. Diminishing returns at higher reasoning effort, sometimes reducing accuracy. Reasoning costs dominate inference spend, and deployment context (on-premises) changes economic viability.

Conclusion: Reasoning should be enabled selectively by task type, effort level, and deployment context rather than as universal default. Provides a practical model-selection framework for cost-effective reasoning deployment.

Abstract: Accuracy-only benchmarking of reasoning-capable large language models misses a central deployment question: when do extended thinking tokens earn their cost? We introduce the Token Economy Score (TES), a marginal benchmarking metric that measures the accuracy gain of a reasoning model over a non-reasoning baseline, normalized by the generated-token multiplier. We define paired and approximated TES variants for model families with reasoning toggles and frontier models without direct non-reasoning counterparts. We then conduct an empirical benchmarking analysis across 151 model-benchmark evaluation runs on seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, science reasoning, instruction following, expert knowledge, knowledge recall, and research-level physics. The analysis examines three deployment-facing dimensions: which task structures yield positive marginal reasoning efficiency, how increasing reasoning effort changes TES within model families, and how deployment context changes economic viability. Results show that task structure predicts reasoning efficiency better than nominal difficulty: sequential inferencechain tasks such as AIME 2025 and LiveCodeBench show high TES, while knowledge-recall tasks such as MMLU-Pro show low TES despite their difficulty. We also find systematic diminishing returns at higher reasoning effort levels, including cases where additional thinking reduces accuracy. Finally, Reasoning Cost Share (RCS) shows that inference spend is often dominated by internal thinking, while Deployment Cost Multiplier (DCM) shows how on-premises deployment can change the economics of otherwise costly reasoning workloads. These findings support a benchmarking-driven model-selection rule: enable reasoning selectively by task type, effort level, and deployment context rather than treating it as a universally beneficial mode.

</details>


### [43] [6.5% of the Neuro-Symbolic Literature Can Be Reproduced from Its Published Artifacts, a Six-Stage Audit Framework and First Instantiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26236)
*Brandon Colelough,Vladimir Martirosyan,Ishan Tamrakar,William Regli,Aditya Kumar,Anh N. Nhu,Dhruv Dubey,Raj Ambavane,Haowei Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A six-stage framework audits reproducibility in neuro-symbolic AI research, finding only 6.52% of eligible studies could be reproduced despite "code available" claims, highlighting severe reproducibility deficits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a persistent reproducibility deficit in scientific research where even papers claiming "code available" often cannot be reproduced due to missing or incomplete artifacts. The study aims to quantify this problem in the neuro-symbolic AI domain to signal the need for better artifact practices.

Method: A six-stage framework: 1) Literature retrieval and deduplication; 2) Screening for relevant NSAI studies; 3) Artifact verification and inventory creation; 4) Bounded rerun attempts; 5) Reproduction analysis; 6) Multi-year audit of the NSAI subdomain with systematic tracking of blocking issues.

Result: From 1,304 eligible NSAI records, only 455 had verifiable public code artifacts. Attempts to reproduce yielded only 85 successful reproductions (6.52% of eligible corpus, 18.68% of attempted reruns). Major blocking issues: 321 reruns blocked by missing non-code artifacts, 42 by missing/unusable code repositories.

Conclusion: The audit reveals severe reproducibility problems persist even with "code available" declarations. Future NSAI publications should require complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles at submission time to ensure scientific reproducibility.

Abstract: We present a six-stage framework for auditing the reproducibility of scientific claims across a research literature within the computer science domain, and instantiate our framework for the neuro-symbolic AI (NSAI) subdomain. Instantiating the framework on the NSAI subdomain produced a multi-year audit. Stage one retrieved 5,497 records and removed 3,018 duplicates. Stage two screened the 2,479 unique records at title and abstract, identifying 1,365 self-identified NSAI records, then removed a further 61 at full text for off-topic, non-research, no-quantitative-evaluation, or inaccessible-full-text reasons. Stage three sought a verifiable public code artifact for each of the 1,304 eligible records and found none for 849, leaving 455 to enter the artifact inventory and bounded rerun of stages four and five. We fully or partially reproduced 85 studies, 6.52% of the eligible corpus and 18.68% of attempted reruns. We found that 321 attempted reruns were blocked by missing non- code artifacts and 42 by missing or unusable code repositories. These figures quantify a persistent reproducibility deficit that survives even nominal "code available" declarations, and signal the need for enforced, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles in future NSAI publications. We argue that empirical NSAI papers should be required at submission time to provide complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles.

</details>


### [44] [Assessing mentalization in humans and large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26291)
*Aamir Sohail,Xintong Zhong,Arkady Konovalov,Patricia L. Lockwood,Lei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show varying mentalization abilities in economic games, with GPT-5 outperforming humans in adaptive strategic reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLMs can guide adaptive behavior through mentalization (inferring others' beliefs/intentions), and to assess their strategic reasoning capabilities in social interactions.

Method: Used two economic games with cognitive computational modeling to analyze latent mentalization strategies in 2,099 LLM agents across DeepSeek, GPT-4.1, GPT-5, and Gemini 2.0 Flash. Tested against opponents of varying sophistication with/without strategic prompting, and benchmarked against 251 human participants.

Result: LLMs showed clear mentalization signatures varying by model/size. Strategic prompting improved performance but task-dependently. GPT-5 demonstrated superior adaptive reasoning by flexibly adjusting recursive depth against sophisticated opponents, outperforming humans.

Conclusion: LLMs possess different mentalization capacities, with GPT-5 showing exceptional adaptive reasoning. Cognitive computational modeling provides a formal framework for comparing intelligence across humans and machines.

Abstract: Mentalization - the ability to infer others' beliefs and intentions to guide one's own choices - is a key cognitive function underlying human social interactions. Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate behaviour consistent with humans on theory-of-mind tasks, yet whether these models can guide adaptive behaviour through mentalization is unknown. Here we use two economic games with cognitive computational modeling to uncover the latent strategies underlying mentalization in LLMs. We tested individual LLM agents across four model families, DeepSeek, GPT-4.1, GPT-5 and Gemini 2.0 Flash (N = 2,099), against opponents of varying sophistication and examined whether a prompting strategy designed to elicit strategic reasoning improved performance. We benchmarked results against human participants (N = 251) as a comparative measure. Across both games, LLMs showed clear behavioural and computational signatures of mentalizing that differed markedly by model provider and size. Strategic prompting generally improved performance by inducing more sophisticated reasoning, yet the extent of the benefit differed across the two tasks. Last, GPT-5 agents flexibly adapted their recursive depth of reasoning to increasingly sophisticated opponents, demonstrating superior performance to human participants. Collectively, we demonstrate different capacities for mentalization across LLMs, and highlight cognitive computational modeling as a formal method for assessing comparative intelligence across humans and machines.

</details>


### [45] [Approved Too Late: Verdict Staleness in LLM-Guarded Self-Adaptive Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26306)
*Ilai Shraga,Roei Eshel,Lior Gorelik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM guardrails for self-adaptive systems can give approvals that become stale by actuation time, creating TOCTOU hazards. The paper studies verdict freshness, proposes a Freshness-Bounded Shield to reduce expiry rates, and introduces a freshness contract requiring approvals to remain valid at use time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language model (LLM) guardrails for self-adaptive systems face a critical problem: an approval that's correct at check time may become stale by the time of actuation, creating Execute-stage time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) hazards. This freshness problem threatens the reliability and safety of LLM-based guardrails in dynamic environments.

Method: The paper studies verdict freshness through three quantities: (1) all-candidate verdict change under fixed-action replay, (2) oracle-labeled approval expiry on recorded closed-loop trajectories, and (3) judge-conditioned use-time invalidity. It introduces the Freshness-Bounded Shield (FBS) that estimates each approval's validity horizon using its safe-side margin and recent feature volatility without requiring an explicit plant-dynamics model.

Result: Across five reproducible SAS environments, all-candidate verdict-change rates spanned 5.3-48.4% at a replay shift of eight simulator steps. FBS reduced oracle-labeled approval-expiry rates from 3.4-24.7% to 0-1.8% at the same shift. An audit of four LLM judges found nonzero judge-conditioned use-time invalidity in every approval stream.

Conclusion: The paper identifies a fundamental freshness problem in LLM guardrails for self-adaptive systems and proposes the Freshness-Bounded Shield as an effective solution. It formulates a freshness contract requiring every approval to be both correct at check time and remain valid at use time, establishing important foundations for reliable LLM-based safety mechanisms in dynamic environments.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) guardrail for a self-adaptive system (SAS) may issue an approval that is correct at check time but stale by actuation. This creates an Execute-stage time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) hazard. We study verdict freshness: whether a guardrail verdict remains valid when used. We distinguish three quantities that answer different questions: all-candidate verdict change under fixed-action replay, oracle-labeled approval expiry on recorded closed-loop trajectories, and judge-conditioned use-time invalidity. Across five reproducible SAS environments, all-candidate verdict-change rates span 5.3-48.4% at a common replay shift of eight simulator steps. We introduce the Freshness-Bounded Shield (FBS), which estimates each approval's validity horizon from its safe-side margin and recent feature volatility, without an explicit plant-dynamics model. Using fixed settings documented in the artifact, FBS reduces oracle-labeled approval-expiry rates from 3.4-24.7% to 0-1.8% at the same shift. A separate audit of four LLM judges finds nonzero judge-conditioned use-time invalidity in every approval stream. We formulate a freshness contract: every approval must be correct at check time and remain valid at use time.

</details>


### [46] [FaithSieve: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Math Proofs with Faithful Formal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26310)
*Ziyu Wang,Qiming Dai,Yishan Wu,Zaiwen Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FaithSieve is a framework that uses Lean theorem prover to evaluate natural-language mathematical proofs by decomposing them into fine-grained reasoning units and verifying them with formal evidence, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy in first-error localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for evaluating LLM-generated mathematical proofs rely on model-based natural-language judgments that often miss local reasoning errors. Formal theorem provers like Lean offer rigorous verification but face challenges with semantic mismatches when applied to informal text.

Method: FaithSieve decomposes coarse proof steps into local reasoning units, extracts typed proof obligations, and verifies them through a formal evaluation agent. It uses semantic alignment scoring to gate formal validation, ensuring Lean evidence is incorporated only when formal statements faithfully preserve the original mathematical intent.

Result: On the 350-problem ProofLoc-Olympiad dataset, FaithSieve with GPT-5.4 backbone achieved 81.43% exact first-error accuracy vs 72.29% baseline. On the 200-problem ProofLoc-University benchmark across six domains, it reached 84.5% vs 75.0% for direct judging.

Conclusion: Decomposing proofs into fine-grained units and grounding them with faithful formal evidence significantly improves reliable evaluation of natural-language mathematical reasoning, addressing critical limitations in current LLM proof assessment methods.

Abstract: Large language models can now generate complex, multi-step mathematical proofs, but reliably determining their correctness and localizing early logical errors remains a critical challenge. Existing evaluation approaches largely depend on model-based natural-language judgments, which often overlook local reasoning gaps. While formal theorem provers like Lean offer a path to rigorous verification, using them to evaluate informal text requires solving locality and semantic mismatches: a prover might bypass a local flaw by proving an overly broad target, or validate an auto-formalized statement that drifts from the original mathematical intent. To address this, we introduce FaithSieve, a Lean-assisted framework for fine-grained evaluation of natural-language mathematical proofs. FaithSieve decomposes coarse proof steps into local reasoning units, extracts typed proof obligations, and verifies them through a formal evaluation agent. Formal validation is gated by semantic alignment scoring, so Lean evidence is incorporated only when the formal statement faithfully preserves the context, objects, and logical form of the original claim. We construct two expert-verified datasets, ProofLoc-Olympiad and ProofLoc-University, to benchmark first-error localization. On the 350-problem Olympiad dataset, FaithSieve using a GPT-5.4 backbone achieves 81.43% exact first-error accuracy, outperforming the direct-judging baseline of 72.29%. Furthermore, on the 200-problem ProofLoc-University benchmark spanning six advanced domains, FaithSieve reaches 84.5% exact accuracy, compared to 75.0% for the direct judge. Our work demonstrates that decomposing proofs into fine-grained units and grounding them with faithful formal evidence significantly improves reliable evaluation of natural-language reasoning.

</details>


### [47] [ProofEvolve: Neuro-Symbolic Evolution for Formal Automated Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26334)
*Wenqian Ye,Ziwei Guan,Eric Xie,Bohan Liu,Shivani Modi,Buyun Zhang,Ellie Dingqiao Wen,Henry Kautz,Aidong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProofEvolve is a neuro-symbolic framework that evolves explicit, formally verified symbolic proof structures using neural models to enable recursive self-improvement in theorem proving.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural theorem provers don't fully preserve recursive self-improvement structure. They either use expensive weight updates or keep verified deductions only within current problems, and rely on sparse whole-proof feedback even when partial attempts contain useful discoveries.

Method: ProofEvolve combines neural models that propose variation operators (decompositions, repairs, and schema recombinations) with symbolic Lean kernel verification. It evolves partial AND-OR proof directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) in behaviorally indexed archives within problems, and maintains persistent schema libraries across problems. Every proof transition is formally verified.

Result: ProofEvolve achieves the highest average solve rate among evaluated proof systems across three competition-level Lean benchmarks.

Conclusion: The framework successfully enables recursive self-improvement by preserving verified results from incomplete attempts, making them available for later proofs without compromising formal soundness, thus closing the gap in existing neural theorem proving approaches.

Abstract: Automated theorem proving offers a natural foundation for recursive self-improvement in scientific discovery. However, existing neural provers do not fully preserve this recursive structure, where the learning process should be self-improving over time. Existing methods either embed proof experience into model parameters through expensive weight updates, or keep verified intermediate deductions only within the current problem. In addition, these methods also heavily rely on sparse whole-proof feedback, even when unsuccessful partial attempts contain useful discoveries. To close the gap, we propose ProofEvolve, a neuro-symbolic framework that evolves explicit, formally verified symbolic proof structures with neural models to decisively expand the knowledge boundary. In this framework, the neural model proposes variation operators, including decompositions, repairs, and schema recombinations. The symbolic Lean kernel verifies every proof transition. Over the evolution loops, ProofEvolve computes verified closure over the resulting proof directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). Within each problem, ProofEvolve evolves partial AND-OR proof DAGs in a behaviorally indexed archive. Across problems, kernel-checked schema extraction adds newly proved sub-DAGs to a persistent schema library. Proof DAGs inherit the solved results through typed schema recombination, with every residual premise exposed as a new subgoal. This evolutionary process preserves verified results from incomplete attempts and makes them available for later proofs without weakening formal soundness. Across three competition-level Lean benchmarks, ProofEvolve achieves the highest average solve rate among the evaluated proof systems.

</details>


### [48] [Fine-Tuning of Transformer models with Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26430)
*Harshavardhan Adepu,Li Zhang,Sanjiv Kumar,Vikas Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FrameFT is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that uses Fusion Frame basis with sparse coefficient matrix, achieving performance on par with or exceeding state-of-the-art PEFT techniques while using far fewer trainable parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PEFT methods like LoRA have memory requirements that scale with model size (O(dr) where d is hidden dimension, r is rank). There's a need for more memory-efficient fine-tuning methods that maintain performance while reducing parameter count.

Method: FrameFT models parameter updates ΔW using a sparse coefficient matrix in a Fusion Frame basis. Fusion Frames can be algorithmically generated and shared across model layers, enabling efficient updates where only sparse coefficients of the basis expansion are stored and optimized.

Result: FrameFT achieves performance on par with or exceeding state-of-the-art PEFT techniques on supervised fine-tuning benchmarks (primarily language tasks, also applied to vision models), while requiring far fewer trainable parameters.

Conclusion: FrameFT provides a memory-efficient alternative to existing PEFT methods by leveraging Fusion Frame basis with sparse coefficients, achieving strong performance with significantly reduced parameter count while maintaining formal convergence guarantees.

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) strategies such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) are effective solutions for fine-tuning large-scale pre-trained models; however, their memory requirements scale with the size of the model, $\mathcal{O}(dr)$, where $d$ is the model's hidden dimension and $r$ is the rank. Our proposal, FrameFT, models the parameter update $ΔW$ with a sparse coefficient matrix in a Fusion Frame basis. Fusion Frames can be generated algorithmically and shared across model layers, enabling very efficient updates. Only the sparse coefficients of the basis expansion are stored/optimized, reducing the memory footprint. The sparse structure of the coefficient matrix in FrameFT and the sparsity in the Fusion Frames give large compute benefits, and our analysis provides formal convergence results. We evaluate the idea across a suite of supervised fine-tuning benchmarks, focusing on language tasks, but also report application to vision models. Our experiments show that FrameFT achieves performance on par with/exceeding state-of-the-art PEFT techniques, but needs far fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [49] [Don't Overthink, Don't Underthink: Toward Adaptive Reasoning in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26442)
*Md Jueal Mia,M. Hadi Amini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM reasoning allocation needs dynamic adaptation in agentic AI to avoid wasteful over-reasoning or incomplete under-reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM reasoning approaches use fixed controls (token budgets, difficulty estimates) evaluated on standalone benchmarks, which don't fit dynamic agentic workflows where reasoning needs evolve through planning, tool use, memory retrieval, and interactions.

Method: Characterized over-reasoning and under-reasoning as failure modes of misallocated reasoning, evaluated them on MATH-500 and GAIA benchmarks using metrics like tool-decision latency, token consumption, token-limit exhaustion, and answer correctness.

Result: Over-reasoning cases showed higher computational cost without proportional accuracy gains, while under-reasoning cases consistently produced incorrect or incomplete solutions.

Conclusion: The key challenge for agentic AI is adaptive reasoning allocation according to evolving task demands, not just reasoning quantity, motivating future research on adaptive reasoning mechanisms.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown that increased inference-time reasoning can improve performance on complex tasks. However, many existing approaches rely on fixed or preallocated reasoning controls, such as fixed token budgets, pre-execution difficulty estimates, or activation-space interventions, and are often evaluated on standalone reasoning benchmarks rather than full agentic workflows.
