The structured modality also suits training: small-scale SFT raises Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, and resource-limited on-policy RL further raises them to 74.4 and 82.2.

</details>


### [75] [A Multi-Modal AI Framework for Real-Time Queue Prediction, Management and Optimisation in Intelligent Border Control Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27010)
*Varvara Mama,Eleni Veroni,Nikolaos Kapsalis,Christos D. Nikolopoulos,Anargyros T. Baklezos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI-based border control management system using LSTM networks for queue prediction and MPC optimization reduces waiting times by 30% and increases throughput by 20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current border control queue management systems rely on static data and cannot adapt to dynamic traffic conditions, leading to suboptimal performance during uncertainty.

Method: Multi-modal AI framework integrating heterogeneous data sources with LSTM networks for real-time queue forecasting, combined with Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to generate actionable control policies.

Result: The system reduces queue prediction error by 35%, decreases average waiting time by 30%, and increases average throughput by 20% compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods.

Conclusion: Combining AI architectures with optimization techniques enables proactive and adaptive border traffic management with significant performance improvements over traditional methods.

Abstract: In the present work an efficient border control management procedure is proposed. Compared to operational queue management systems, whose operations are based on mostly static data, the proposed work takes into account dynamic traffic conditions, thus enabling optimal performance, even in cases of uncertainty. To this end, we are proposing a multi-modal Artificial Intelligence (AI) framework, tailored to th needs of border control systems, which enables real-time queue prediction, management, and resource optimization. The novel proposed approach integrates heterogeneous data sources and presents them through a unified representation by employing Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks for queue forecasting. Furthermore, it leverages Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to derive actionable control policies, which in turn can be presented to border control officers. The proposed work has been evaluated using synthetic data simulating realistic traffic. The evaluation results demonstrate that the proposed method reduces queue prediction error by up to 35% and average waiting time by 30%. Accordingly, the average throughput increases by nearly 20%, compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods. The abovementioned results show the effectiveness and efficiency of combining AI architectures with optimization techniques for proactive and adaptive border traffic management.

</details>


### [76] [Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27044)
*Wei-Yao Wang,Kazuya Tateishi,Shuyang Cui,Christian Simon,Takashi Shibuya,Shusuke Takahashi,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniUE: First universal embedder that supports omni-interactive querying across text, video, and audio modalities, achieving state-of-the-art performance on multimodal retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal representation learning approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, lacking support for omni-modal (text, video, audio) and omni-interactive capabilities where users can provide complex queries including visual regions of interest and audio spans.

Method: Proposes OmniUE with three key components: 1) Unified embedding space across text, video, and audio using intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, 2) Visual and audio segmenters to process diverse user interactions, 3) Integration with an omni-LLM for context aggregation to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings.

Result: OmniUE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with average improvements of 10.5% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on the newly introduced omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark.

Conclusion: Jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders that can handle complex, multi-modal user interactions for any-to-any retrieval tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to large language model (LLM)-based embedders due to their strong instruction-following capabilities. Despite this progress, existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, which also remain the dominant modalities for user-conditioned interactions in current embedders. In this paper, we propose the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder (OmniUE), which not only learns a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio by leveraging intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, but also supports omni-interactive querying, enabling users to provide inputs in the form of text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. Within OmniUE, visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. To evaluate OmniUE's omni-interactive capabilities, we introduce OmniCHOIR, benchmarking models for omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval based on the given text, video, and audio as well as unimodal or multimodal interaction prompts. OmniUE consistently surpasses state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities, with average improvements of 10.5% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on our omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark. We believe that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders.

</details>


### [77] [pro-team at LLMs4OL 2026 Tasks Flagship and Reuse: Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Vocabulary-Constrained Filtering for Ontology Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27101)
*Shivam Mishra,Dhannu Ram Meena,Muneendra Ojha,Krishna Pratap Singh,Kuldeep Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs4OL 2026 challenge entry using Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting for ontology learning tasks, achieving strong results on taxonomy-oriented tasks but failing to extract non-taxonomic relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite progress in LLMs, ontology learning from text remains challenging due to hallucinations, inconsistent formats, and bias toward hierarchical over associative relations. The LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge aims to address these issues.

Method: Offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline using Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval (top-5 for Task A, top-2 for Task B). Left-truncated context-windowing preserves instructions. For Task B, deterministic vocabulary-constrained filtering retains triples where at least one endpoint belongs to the sample's closed vocabulary and removes duplicates.

Result: Task B: Semantic Graph Similarity 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 0.9200, Taxonomy Discovery F1 0.8540. Task A: Semantic Graph Similarity 0.7416. No non-taxonomic relations were extracted, highlighting limitations of closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabularies.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates strong performance on taxonomy-oriented ontology learning tasks but reveals limitations in handling non-taxonomic relations due to closed vocabularies. Future work should address associative relation extraction and vocabulary expansion.

Abstract: Ontology learning from text remains challenging despite significant progress in Large Language Models (LLMs), which can hallucinate domain terms, produce inconsistent formats, and favor hierarchical over associative relations. In the LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge, we address both the End-to-End Flagship Task (Task A) and Ontology Extension Reuse Task (Task B) using an offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline. Our system employs Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval, selecting the top-5 examples for Task A and top-2 for Task B. A left-truncated context-windowing strategy preserves task instructions within long prompts. For Task B, generated triples undergo deterministic vocabulary-constrained filtering, retaining triples when at least one endpoint belongs to the sample's closed term/type vocabulary and removing duplicates of the initial ontology. The approach achieves Semantic Graph Similarity of 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 of 0.9200, and Taxonomy Discovery F1 of 0.8540 on Task B, while Task A achieves 0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity. However, no non-taxonomic relations are extracted, highlighting limitations of closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabularies.

</details>


### [78] [LAAF: A Layered Accountability Architecture Framework for LLM Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27102)
*Prachi Chaturvedi,Shahnawaz Ahmad,Ehsan Nowroozi,Muhammad Waqas,George Loukas,Alireza Jolfaei,Lucas Cordeiro,Pierre Dantas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic review examining accountability mechanisms for LLMs in high-stakes domains, analyzing 122 studies and 12 regulatory documents to develop an integrated accountability framework aligned with AI regulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in high-stakes domains like healthcare, courts, and finance where their confident outputs can cause harm even when incorrect. There's a critical need to establish clear accountability frameworks to determine who is responsible and through what mechanisms when LLM outputs contribute to harm.

Method: Systematic review following PRISMA guidelines, analyzing 122 primary studies and 12 regulatory/standards documents from Jan 2022-Mar 2026. Developed sociotechnical account of accountability with five dimensions and synthesized mechanisms across four families: technical controls, human oversight, organizational governance, and documentation/traceability.

Result: Identified four persistent gaps: underspecified human oversight, lack of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, and limited empirical evaluation. Proposed LAAF (integrated accountability architecture) aligned with cybersecurity frameworks (OWASP LLM Top 10) and mapped findings to major regulations (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC 42001).

Conclusion: The review presents an integrated accountability framework (LAAF) that synthesizes existing evidence, addressing gaps in current approaches while identifying structural tensions that existing regulations don't resolve. Provides a systematic basis for implementing accountability mechanisms for LLMs in high-stakes applications.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) operate in hospitals, courtrooms, banks, and public service desks, where fluent, confident outputs are treated as authoritative even when ungrounded or incorrect. When such an output contributes to harm, who is answerable, and through what mechanisms can responsibility be traced, explained, and acted upon? Following PRISMA guidance, five databases were searched from January 2022 to March 2026 against four review questions; of 4,512 records identified, 122 primary studies were included, together with 12 regulatory and standards documents analysed as primary sources. The review consolidates a sociotechnical account of accountability as an actor-forum relation resolved into five dimensions, and synthesises mechanisms across four families: technical controls, human oversight, organisational governance, and documentation and traceability, each with a maturity assessment. The corpus is read through a four-layer classification device spanning provenance, application logic, human oversight, and governance and redress, cross-cut by traceability, role clarity, and continuous monitoring. Both are mapped onto the EU AI Act, whose high-risk obligations have applied since 2 August 2026, the NIST AI RMF with its Generative AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance in healthcare, consumer finance, education, and the public sector. Four persistent gaps emerge: under-specification of human oversight, absence of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, and limited empirical evaluation, alongside five structural tensions that no surveyed instrument resolves. The review closes by consolidating the classification device into an integrated accountability architecture, LAAF, with cybersecurity aligned to the OWASP LLM Top 10 (2025); it is a synthesis of the surveyed evidence rather than a validated artefact.

</details>


### [79] [TransMeme: A Multi-Agent Framework for Cross-Cultural Meme Transcreation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27127)
*Jingyi Zheng,Yule Liu,Zifan Peng,Tianyi Hu,Yuemeng Zhao,Xinhu Zheng,Xinlei He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a multi-agent framework for cross-cultural meme transcreation that outperforms baselines in both human and LLM evaluations, with bottlenecks identified in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Internet memes are pervasive multimodal communication tools, but users from diverse linguistic/cultural backgrounds face challenges in mutual understanding. Standard translation or text rewriting alone cannot preserve communicative intent, adapt culture-dependent meaning, and maintain text-image coherence required for effective meme adaptation across cultures.

Method: Multi-agent framework with specialized agents coordinated to address three core challenges: 1) cultural adaptation, 2) target text rewriting, 3) revision, and 4) conditional visual adjustment. The framework uses coordinated feedback for cases needing deeper cultural or visual intervention.

Result: Outperformed all baselines in bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation. Human evaluation: best performance on all four dimensions with 33.1% average improvement over strongest baseline. LLM-as-a-Judge: achieved highest Top-1 ranking rate (60% vs 26% for second-best baseline). Each component contributed to performance.

Conclusion: The multi-agent framework effectively addresses cross-cultural meme transcreation challenges. Remaining bottlenecks are in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment rather than cultural knowledge gaps, pointing to future work on humor transfer for more effective meme adaptation across cultures.

Abstract: Internet memes are a pervasive form of multimodal online communication; however, such communication often involves users from diverse linguistic and cultural backgrounds. Therefore, adapting memes across cultures and languages is a central challenge for enabling mutual understanding in online communication. Unlike ordinary translation or standalone text rewriting, cross-cultural meme transcreation must jointly preserve communicative intent, adapt culture-dependent meaning for the target audience, and maintain coherence between text and image. In this work, we first provide an explicit task analysis of cross-cultural meme transcreation and identify three core challenges: culture-specific knowledge understanding, intent and tone preservation, and multimodal consistency. Based on this analysis, we propose a multi-agent framework with specialized agents that are coordinated to address these challenges through cultural adaptation, target text rewriting, revision, and conditional visual adjustment. The framework strengthens target text adaptation with coordinated feedback to handle difficult cases that require deeper cultural or visual intervention. We evaluate the framework on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using both human evaluation and LLM-as-a-Judge. Our method consistently outperforms all baselines across both evaluation settings. In human evaluation, it achieves the best performance on all four dimensions and delivers a 33.1% average improvement over the strongest baseline, while in LLM-as-a-Judge, it attains the highest Top-1 ranking rate (60% versus 26% for the second-best baseline). Further analysis indicates that each component contributes to the performance. Our error analysis suggests that the remaining bottlenecks lie in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment rather than simple cultural knowledge gaps, pointing to future work on humor transfer.

</details>


### [80] [GRAIN: Bridging Name and Narrative Shifts in Real-World Graph Reasoning through Invariance-Rewarded Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27142)
*Zike Yuan,Han Zhang,Jianzhi Yan,Le Liu,Cai Ke,Huozhi Zhou,Jian Xie,Jiran Yin,Yukun Cao,Yue Yu,Hui Wang,Ming Liu,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRAIN is a single-agent reinforcement learning framework that improves LLMs' robustness to linguistic shifts in graph tasks by learning structure-invariant text-to-graph parsing, outperforming multi-agent systems with better accuracy and lower latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are brittle to real-world shifts in node identifiers and task formulation in graph tasks, while deterministic graph tools are invariant to such shifts. Extracting topological structures from noisy text is fragile for LLMs, which overfit to surface patterns. Multi-agent systems to mitigate these parsing failures have prohibitive latency.

Method: Propose GRAIN: a single-agent framework optimized via reinforcement learning that models reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward that validates extracted intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies to force learning of robust text-to-structure mappings.

Result: GRAIN outperforms multi-agent baselines by 16.45% in accuracy with approximately 24% lower latency. Shows superior structural generalization, halving the OOD gap of SFT models (from 15.77% to 7.80%) and maintaining robustness on large-scale graphs beyond training distribution. Also introduces GRIT benchmark for evaluating sensitivity to linguistic shifts.

Conclusion: GRAIN effectively addresses LLMs' brittleness to linguistic shifts in graph tasks through structure-invariant reinforcement learning, achieving better performance and efficiency than multi-agent approaches while demonstrating strong generalization to out-of-distribution and large-scale graphs.

Abstract: Despite their potential in standardized graph tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain brittle to real-world shifts in node identifiers and task formulation. While deterministic graph tools are invariant to such shifts, extracting topological structures from noisy text is highly fragile for LLMs, which often overfit to surface patterns. Moreover, mitigating these parsing failures via multi-agent systems incurs prohibitive latency. To address this, we propose GRAIN, a single-agent framework optimized via reinforcement learning. GRAIN models reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward. By validating extracted intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies, this reward forces the LLM to learn robust text-to-structure mappings rather than memorizing linguistic artifacts. We also introduce GRIT, a benchmark evaluating sensitivity to such linguistic shifts. GRAIN outperforms multi-agent baselines by 16.45\% in accuracy with approximately 24\% lower latency. Furthermore, it demonstrates superior structural generalization, halving the out-of-distribution (OOD) gap of SFT models (from 15.77\% to 7.80\%) and maintaining robustness on large-scale graphs beyond the training distribution.

</details>


### [81] [Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering for Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27144)
*Xiang Wang,Zhijun Cheng,Zhenyu Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JPGFN: A novel graph anomaly detection method using feature separation transformation, adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering, and node label constraints to overcome limitations of existing frequency-domain filtering approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph anomaly detection methods based on frequency-domain filtering face three challenges: 1) static basic functions for graph filters can't adapt to graph data's frequency-domain distribution, 2) inadequate consideration of attribute importance in node features leads to fine-grained information loss, and 3) insufficient utilization of node labels for anomaly detection.

Method: Proposes JPGFN with three components: 1) Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) for learning fine-grained node features through feature separation and nonlinear transformations across dimensions, 2) Adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module to capture complex frequency-domain features, and 3) Node label constraint module to better utilize node labels for GAD.

Result: Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms mainstream graph anomaly detection approaches.

Conclusion: JPGFN effectively addresses the limitations of existing frequency-domain filtering methods for graph anomaly detection by incorporating adaptive filtering, fine-grained feature learning, and label utilization, achieving superior performance on real-world datasets.

Abstract: In recent years, graph anomaly detection (GAD) based on frequency-domain filtering have achieved promising results. However, existing approaches still face three major challenges: First, they use static basic function to constructed graph filter which cannot effectively adapt to the frequency-domain distribution of graph data. Second, they fail to adequately consider the importance information of each attribute in the node feature vector, leading to the loss of fine-grained information. Third, they insufficiently utilize node labels for GAD. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel graph anomaly detection method called JPGFN (Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering Network). First, a Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) is developed to better learn fine-grained node features by feature separation and applying nonlinear transformations to node features across different dimensions. Second, an adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module is constructed based on Jacobi polynomials to adaptively capture complex frequency-domain features of graph signals. Finally, a node label constraint module is developed to facilitate the use of node labels and enhance the performance of GAD. Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms mainstream approaches.

</details>


### [82] [When Tool Outputs Become Commands: Separating Action Induction from Runtime Authorization in Tool-Augmented LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27146)
*Xiaokun Guo,Zhen Xu,Dongdong Huo,Yanqiu Zhang,Wei Wang,Qinfu Yang,Dongjin Yu,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tool-augmented LLM agents risk executing unauthorized real-world actions when tool outputs become "commands"; SARA separates action induction from execution authorization to prevent this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When tool-augmented LLM agents process untrusted runtime observations, tool outputs can evolve from providing data to specifying concrete actions, effectively becoming "commands" that can drive unintended real-world side effects. This risk arises from conflating action induction (recognizing what actions are suggested) with execution authorization (deciding whether to actually execute them).

Method: SARA treats action induction and execution authorization as distinct runtime roles and separates action provenance from execution authority. It uses a context-isolated Action Probe to expose action-inducing semantics and record action-origin provenance across steps. Actual tool calls are authorized only against user objectives and audited evidence from authorized successful executions, with No-History-Promotion to prevent historical recurrence from laundering action origins into execution authority.

Result: SARA achieves Attack Success Rate (ASR) of no more than 0.63% across four primary evaluation settings in AgentDojo and AgentDyn benchmarks while maintaining competitive task utility. It consistently reduces ASR across additional agent backbones.

Conclusion: Separating action induction from execution authorization is crucial for secure tool-augmented LLM agents. SARA effectively prevents unauthorized action execution while maintaining task performance, providing a principled approach to handling the risks when tool outputs become actionable commands.

Abstract: Tool-augmented LLM agents must rely on untrusted runtime Observations to complete open-ended tasks; however, when tool outputs no longer merely provide data but begin to specify concrete actions, they effectively become ``commands'' that can drive real-world side effects beyond user intent. We argue that this risk arises from conflating action induction with execution authorization. To address this distinction, we propose SARA, which treats action induction and execution authorization as distinct runtime roles and separates action provenance from execution authority. On the Observation side, a context-isolated Action Probe exposes action-inducing semantics and persistently records action-origin provenance across steps as a review signal; on the execution side, actual tool calls are authorized only against the user objective and audited evidence from authorized successful executions, while satisfying goal, execution-chain, and argument-level support. To preserve this separation across multi-step execution, SARA applies No-History-Promotion to prevent historical recurrence from laundering action origins into execution authority. Across AgentDojo and AgentDyn, SARA limits ASR to no more than \(0.63\%\) across four primary evaluation settings while maintaining competitive task utility, and consistently reduces ASR across additional Agent backbones.

</details>


### [83] [Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for SovereignAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27147)
*Shengzhuang Chen,Jerrod Parker,Yejin Bang,Andrew M. Bean,Nabeel Seedat,Stefan Winzeck,Daniil Glazko,Jannik Zgraggen,Fangyi Yu,Scott Arnott,Dietrich Trautmann,Luca Ciuffreda,Guglielmo Bonifazi,Davide Romano,Bradley Bell,Kirsty Fielding,Daniele Giofrè,Tom Zielund,Ipshita Chatterjee,Sneha Murthy Ghantasala,Manpreet Nanreh,John Scoville,Maciej Sakowicz,Wassim Seifeddine,Lukas Thede,Jonathan Richard Schwarz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier AI performance can be achieved by diverse institutions through continual learning on open-weight models, enabling SovereignAI with lower budgets than commonly believed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI development is dominated by a few heavily funded players, creating power asymmetries between developers and users. There's a need for SovereignAI (independent AI capability) but limited guidance on how to achieve it under diverse funding constraints.

Method: Continual learning on readily available open-weight models using modern mid- & post-training stack with safeguards that preserve both plasticity and stability. This involves minimal high-impact parameter interventions rather than limited approaches like fine-tuning or prompt engineering.

Result: Developed Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model that performs competitively with recent frontier models across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax, multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. Shows distinctive π-shaped improvement pattern: wide-ranging capability improvements (including non-targeted ones) while almost eliminating forgetting problems common to narrow domain adaptation.

Conclusion: Frontier performance is achievable by diverse institutions through continual learning, making SovereignAI (model, infrastructure, values, data privacy) viable for far more actors with substantially lower compute and personnel budgets than commonly thought.

Abstract: The development of frontier models is commonly perceived to be the exclusive remit of a small number of heavily funded players, creating an information, economic and power asymmetry between developers and the diverse user base of modern AI. Recent public discourse acknowledges this concern, calling for SovereignAI (an organisation's capability to independently build, deploy and govern AI use), but offers little concrete advice on how this can be achieved in the short term under a diversity of funding settings. We argue that frontier performance is achievable by a wide range of institutions through Continual Learning on readily available open-weight models. Unlike limited approaches such as small-scale fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool-augmentation of a frozen model, our approach exploits a modern mid- & post-training stack while introducing safeguards that preserve both plasticity and stability at each stage, making the minimal number of high-impact interventions on the parameters. This yields gains comparable to those typically seen across multiple successive model generations, at compute and personnel budgets substantially lower than commonly thought, making ownership of large parts of the SovereignAI stack (model, tool infrastructure, values & data privacy) viable for far more actors. We demonstrate this with Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model trained with an enhanced focus on high-stakes professional work. Thomson performs competitively with recent frontier models across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax & multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. Evaluations show a distinctive $π$-shaped pattern: distinct improvements across a wide range of capabilities, including those not explicitly targeted, while almost completely eliminating the forgetting problem common to narrow domain adaptation.

</details>


### [84] [BPMN4CAI: A BPMN Extension for Modeling Dynamic Conversational AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27149)
*Björn-Lennart Eger,Daniel Rose,Barbara Dinter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BPMN4CAI: A BPMN extension for modeling Conversational AI systems in business processes to address the standard's limitations with dynamic, context-sensitive interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational AI systems are increasingly important for digital business processes, but the established BPMN standard faces challenges representing dynamic, context-sensitive interactions, creating a methodological and practical research gap.

Method: Using Design Science Research methodology to develop BPMN4CAI, a standard-compliant BPMN extension that systematically extends existing BPMN elements and incorporates specialized components.

Result: The BPMN4CAI framework is demonstrated and evaluated through a case study, showing it facilitates adaptive decision-making processes, robust context management, and transparent interactions for Conversational AI within business processes.

Conclusion: The BPMN4CAI extension successfully addresses the research gap by enabling effective modeling of Conversational AI systems within business processes while maintaining BPMN standard compliance.

Abstract: Conversational AI systems, such as chatbots and virtual assistants, are becoming increasingly important to digital business processes. However, the established Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) standard faces challenges when representing dynamic, context-sensitive interactions. This paper addresses this methodological and practical research gap by developing a standard-compliant BPMN extension (BPMN4CAI). Using Design Science Research methodology, this paper develops an approach that systematically extends existing BPMN elements and incorporates specialized components. The applicability and relevance of the BPMN4CAI framework are demonstrated and evaluated through a case study. The results show that the BPMN4CAI extension facilitates adaptive decision-making processes, robust context management, and transparent interactions for Conversational AI within business processes.

</details>


### [85] [Calibrated Enough to Know, Not Calibrated to Act: Fabricated Evidence Makes LLM Agents Commit to the Unknowable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27167)
*Pranav Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents commit to directional predictions on unpredictable questions when presented with professional-looking market panels, even when all data is fabricated, showing they respond to presentation authority rather than information quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates how LLMs respond to professional-looking market data displays when making predictions on unpredictable questions. The motivation is to understand whether LLMs can distinguish between genuine information and authoritative presentation, and whether they have proper judgment about knowable vs. unknowable questions.

Method: Researchers tested 12 frontier LLM models with professional-looking market panels on provably unpredictable questions. They conducted three main experiments: 1) Comparing commitment rates with bare questions vs. escalated evidence presentations, 2) Testing with completely fabricated data panels, and 3) Evaluating model performance on matched answerable questions. They also implemented supervised fine-tuning on a 3B model using 540 synthetic cases to train a better "act/don't-act gate."

Result: Key results: 1) Commitment rose from 6.5% to 54.0% with escalated evidence, 2) Fabricated data panels (24.5% to 36.8%) produced statistically indistinguishable commitment from genuine data (37.6%), 3) Models could answer matched answerable questions at near-perfect accuracy, 4) Stated probabilities didn't correlate with action decisions, 5) Supervised fine-tuning reduced commitment to 0.0% on original cases and transferred to unseen domains, but failed with rigid response formats.

Conclusion: The failure is specific to the "act/don't-act gate" rather than general incapacity or belief errors. This gate is separable and trainable through supervised fine-tuning, but remains context-fragile - particularly vulnerable to rigid response formats that remove reasoning space. Successful deployment requires both training the gate and maintaining appropriate response formats.

Abstract: An LLM agent shown a professional-looking market panel commits to a directional call on a provably unpredictable question far more often than one asked the bare question: across 12 frontier models, commitment rises from 6.5% to 54.0% as evidence is escalated. It commits just as readily when every number on the panel is invented: fabricating the entire display, so nothing the model can see is true except the question itself, still lifts commitment from 24.5% to 36.8%, statistically indistinguishable from the 37.6% produced by genuine market data. What unlocks confident action is not information but the authority of its packaging. The failure is narrow and locatable. Incapacity is not the answer: on matched answerable questions attached to the same panels, the same models answer essentially always, at near-perfect accuracy. Nor is it belief - stated probabilities barely move across the gradient that swings action by 48 points, and score worse than a climatological baseline. Missing judgment isn't it either: asked to classify a question's knowability before acting, models call it irreducible 90% of the time and then commit on just 0.4% of those. The act/don't-act gate is what fails, and the effect is concentrated in a few models rather than universal. Because the gate is separable, it can be trained. Supervised fine-tuning of a 3B model on 540 synthetic cases, predominantly dice, coins, jars and timers, drives commitment to 0.0% on the original cases and transfers to three unseen domains. It does not survive everything: the gate holds exactly when the response format leaves room to reason, and rigid formats that remove that room leave the model confident and wrong on questions it otherwise answers correctly. The gate is trainable and context-fragile, and deployment needs both halves of that sentence.

</details>


### [86] [What Makes Good Agentic Data? An ACE Lens on Data Generation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27260)
*Xingshan Zeng,Zishan Xu,Boju Zhang,Yuzhou Wu,Lingzhi Wang,Jianghao Lin,Liangyou Li,Yasheng Wang,Lifeng Shang,Xin Jiang,Weinan Zhang,Yong Yu,Qun Liu,Weiwen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for analyzing agentic data generation through Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity (ACE) lens, focusing on generating valid, informative, and non-redundant interaction data rather than just abundant data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic data generation approaches lack a unified framework, obscuring common mechanisms and conflating candidate construction with verification/selection. The field needs systematic analysis to move beyond simply generating more data.

Method: Proposes a two-level framework: 1) Factorizes agentic data as (E,q,τ,v) - environment, task, interaction, verifier; 2) Formulates generation as constrained distribution design through Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity (ACE) principles.

Result: The framework reveals a shift toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and diversity beyond surface variation. It organizes existing work and identifies trends in verification methods, difficulty calibration, and behavioral coverage.

Conclusion: The central challenge is to continually allocate valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve, not simply generate more data. The ACE framework provides a lens for analyzing and guiding agentic data generation.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on generated interaction data to learn how to interact with external environments. Agentic data generation must maintain consistency among environments, tasks, interactions, and success signals while producing experience that is useful rather than merely abundant. Existing work spans many agent domains, but domain-centered organization and heterogeneous evaluation often obscure common generation mechanisms and conflate candidate construction with verification and selection. This work develops a two-level framework for the field. First, we represent agentic data as a common factorized object $(E,q,τ,v)$, comprising an environment specification, task signal, interaction realization, and optional verifier. We organize generation paradigms by their primary anchor and dependency structure. Second, we formulate generation as constrained distribution design through the Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity (ACE) lens. Accuracy establishes the feasible support of grounded and internally consistent data. Within this support, Complexity places learning mass relative to the capability of a declared learner and execution configuration, while divErsity controls coverage and redundancy of data. Using this framework, we explore how prior work verifies generated experience, constructs and calibrates difficulty, and expands behavioral coverage. The literature reveals a shift toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and diversity beyond surface variation or dataset size. We further discuss broader directions and emerging trends in agentic data generation through the ACE lens, including their implications for scaling, data sources, training regimes and adaptive learning. Overall, the central challenge is not simply to generate more data, but to continually allocate valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve.

</details>


### [87] [Naive Prompt Optimization: Rethinking the Need for Complex Prompt Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27266)
*Yuan Chang,Xiaoqi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO) is a simple, single-lineage prompt optimization method that achieves comparable or better performance than complex methods like GEPA with fewer computational resources, especially when using stronger teacher models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the trend toward unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers for improving autonomous agents. While prompt optimization is promising for agent improvement with lower computational costs than weight fine-tuning, current methods have become overly sophisticated. The authors seek to demonstrate that simple approaches can be equally effective.

Method: Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO) is a lightweight, single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. It's simpler than complex search procedures like GEPA, requiring fewer rollouts and less search complexity.

Result: NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts. Its advantages increase with stronger teacher models, suggesting teacher reasoning can substitute for optimizer complexity. NPO remains competitive with GEPA in interactive games, while optimized prompts transfer well to other student models within the same family.

Conclusion: Simple, linear prompt optimization like NPO can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures, challenging the trend toward unnecessary complexity in prompt optimization methods.

Abstract: Efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks is central to accelerating recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI, with prompt optimization emerging as a promising approach capable of delivering performance gains comparable to those achieved by fine-tuning model weights, while reducing computational costs in both optimization and serving. However, recent developments increasingly favor unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers. We introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning can partially substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. In interactive games, NPO remains broadly competitive with GEPA, while GRPO performs better on some tasks less amenable to prompt optimization. We also show that NPO-optimized prompts elicit similar performance improvements when applied verbatim to other student models, especially across models within the same family. Overall, our preliminary results show that simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures.

</details>


### [88] [BrailleBench: Investigating Multi-Criteria Braille Comprehension in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27268)
*Jinghan Zhang,Fengran Mo,Zhiyu Chen,Xiaoyan Han,Kunpeng Liu,Chang-Tien Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BrailleBench is a comprehensive benchmark evaluating LLM capabilities in Braille comprehension across mathematics, commonsense, and multi-hop QA tasks, revealing significant gaps in accessibility for blind and deafblind users.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs should benefit vulnerable groups like blind and deafblind users equally, but it's unclear if existing AI systems are inclusive enough for Braille access. Braille's unique indicators, contractions, and digital representations create distinct comprehension requirements that may not be adequately supported.

Method: Created BrailleBench by aligning 5,570 instances from five datasets across English and Braille Grades 1 and 2. Designed different configurations to test Braille comprehension, expression, and end-to-end interaction. Used a deterministic, expert-reviewed pipeline with a self-created Braille Toolkit without any LLM-generated data to prevent evaluation bias.

Result: Evaluation of six representative LLMs revealed persistent gaps between print-English capability and Braille accessibility. Braille understanding and expression are asymmetric - Grade 2 Braille is especially fragile on input compared to Grade 1, and fully Braille requests further reduce performance.

Conclusion: The findings provide valuable guidance for developing future Braille AI systems, highlighting the need to address Braille accessibility gaps in LLMs. All BrailleBench resources are publicly available to support further research in this important area of inclusive AI.

Abstract: Although Large language models (LLMs) mediate access to knowledge and computational assistance, their capabilities should benefit vulnerable groups in the same way. However, it is unclear whether existing AI systems are inclusive enough for blind and deafblind users to access the same functionality through Braille, whose indicators, contractions, and digital representations introduce distinct requirements for model comprehension. To this end, we introduce BrailleBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs in Braille comprehension from different Criteria. BrailleBench aligns 5,570 instances from five datasets, including mathematics, commonsense, and multi-hop question answering across English and Braille Grades 1 and 2. Different configurations are designed to understand whether the systems can comprehend Braille-authored content, express answers in Braille, and complete end-to-end Braille interaction. To ensure the quality and prevent evaluation bias, the benchmark is built through a deterministic, expert-reviewed pipeline via a self-created Braille Toolkit without using any data instances generated by LLMs. We evaluate six representative LLMs from various aspects. The results reveal a persistent gap between print-English capability and Braille accessibility. Braille understanding and expression are asymmetric, where Grade 2 is especially fragile on the input side compared to Grade 1, and fully Braille requests further reduce performance. The experimental observations provide valuable guidance for the development of future Braille AI systems. All related resources in BrailleBench are publicly available for future research.

</details>


### [89] [LLMs Can Design Near-Optimal OR Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27296)
*Jackie Baek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large language models can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research problems, matching or outperforming existing methods with minimal human input and a single untuned prompt.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether large language models (LLMs) can effectively design algorithms for well-specified operations research problems, addressing the gap between specialized OR methods and general-purpose AI capabilities.

Method: Researchers evaluate LLMs on inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization using two levels: Level 1 - solving individual problem instances; Level 2 - generating complete algorithms from problem class descriptions with Python sandbox access and fixed compute budget.

Result: GPT-5.6-sol matches or outperforms best existing methods on almost all evaluated instances, even at Level 2 where algorithms are fixed before seeing test instances. Performance improves sharply across models released within 8 months.

Conclusion: Frontier LLMs can serve as serious empirical baselines for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems, with a single untuned query producing competitive algorithms, suggesting rapid advancement in this capability.

Abstract: We ask whether large language models (LLMs) can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research (OR) problems. We study inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization. We evaluate two levels of LLM use: at level 1, the model receives one problem instance and returns a solution for that instance; at level 2, it receives only the problem class description and broad parameter ranges, and returns an algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Human input is minimal: we give one untuned prompt that describes the problem, and the model has access to a Python sandbox tool with a fixed compute budget.
  The strongest model we test, gpt-5.6-sol, matches or outperforms the best existing method on almost all evaluated instances. This holds even at level 2, where the returned algorithm is fixed before seeing the evaluation instances. Performance also improves sharply across models released less than eight months apart, suggesting that this capability is moving quickly. Thus, for the well-specified operations problems we study, a single untuned LLM query can already produce algorithms competitive with specialized methods. These results suggest that frontier LLMs can be a serious empirical baseline for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.

</details>


### [90] [CorporateBench: Large-Scale Q&A Benchmarking with Temporal Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27391)
*Sil Hamilton,Albert Yu Sun,Oscar J. Romero,Carl-Leander Henneking,David Mimno,Bishan Yang,Igor Labutov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CorporateBench (CB) is a large-scale, human-validated multi-task Q&A benchmark for evaluating LLMs on corporate communication tasks, featuring synthetic but realistic datasets of up to 230,000 documents with cross-document consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a critical gap in evaluating LLMs for enterprise-scale document analysis. Companies can't share sensitive internal communications, and existing synthetic datasets are too simplistic to simulate real corporate environments with their scale and complexity.

Method: Created CorporateBench with four synthetically generated firms (12-10,000 employees) featuring temporally evolving knowledge bases ensuring cross-document logical consistency across up to 230,000 documents. The benchmark evaluates LLMs across information extraction and knowledge base querying tasks with human validation.

Result: Evaluation of five LLMs shows performance degradation as input size approaches realistic corporate scales, highlighting how current models struggle with the complexity and volume of actual enterprise communications.

Conclusion: CorporateBench fills a crucial gap in LLM benchmarking by providing a realistic, scalable testbed for corporate communication reasoning, enabling better evaluation of enterprise-ready AI systems.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly able to answer complex questions about enterprise-scale document collections. But evaluation is hard: companies don't want to share internal communications, and synthetic datasets have been overly simple. We present CorporateBench (CB), a human-validated multi-task Q&A benchmark whose scale approaches the conditions LLMs encounter in corporate communication networks, with evaluation corpora surpassing 230,000 documents. CB evaluates LLMs across two dimensions (information extraction and knowledge base querying) through four synthetically generated firms ranging from 12 to 10,000 employees. Each corpus is sampled from a temporally evolving knowledge base describing a consistent world, guaranteeing cross-document logical consistency even across hundreds of thousands of documents. We evaluate five LLMs on CB, revealing increasingly poor performance as input size approaches realistic scales. CB provides LLM developers a metric for corporate communication reasoning, filling a crucial gap in the benchmarking ecosystem.

</details>


### [91] [Verify Smarter, Evolve Further: Efficient Harness Evolution through Behavior-Aware Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27311)
*Jinghan Xu,Yikai Zhang,Aili Chen,Weiyuan Li,Jiaqing Liang,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarnessLens: A budget-aware framework for automated evolution of agent harnesses that jointly explores task space and user-configurable components, using behavior-aware verification to improve efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing propose-and-verify methods for adapting agent harnesses are inefficient - they waste rollouts on unrelated behaviors and allow aggregate scores to obscure specific regressions, requiring costly verification.

Method: HarnessLens framework jointly explores task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and selectively verifies each candidate on behavior-relevant tasks using an attributable-evidence gate.

Result: Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6-13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than competing baselines.

Conclusion: Behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets.

Abstract: Agent harnesses shape how language-model agents use instructions, tools, and runtime components, but adapting these harnesses requires costly verification. Existing propose-and-verify methods typically score every candidate on a fixed task set, wasting rollouts on unrelated behaviors and allowing aggregate scores to obscure specific regressions. We introduce HarnessLens, a budget-aware framework for automated harness evolution. HarnessLens jointly explores the task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and selectively verifies each candidate on behavior-relevant tasks using an attributable-evidence gate. Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6-13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than competing baselines. These results demonstrate that behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets. Our code is available at https://github.com/jhxu5214/HarnessLens.

</details>


### [92] [Learning a Continuous Sepsis Severity Score Without Hour-by-Hour Supervision: A Two-Site Retrospective Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27421)
*Kevin Zhu,Ryan Zhang,Baraa Abed,Tilendra Choudhary,Malvern Madondo,Mehak Arora,Yixuan Yang,Alasdair Gent,Aditya Nagori,Omer T. Inan,Krista L. Haines,Patrick Georgoff,Suresh M. Agarwal,Vijay Krishnamoorthy,Tetsu Ohnuma,Mihai V. Podgoreanu,Michael R. Pinsky,Gilles Clermont,Craig M. Coopersmith,Craig S. Jabaley,Rishikesan Kamaleswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new sepsis severity index developed using machine learning on 43 clinical variables over 72 hours, showing better mortality risk stratification than traditional scores like SOFA and strong correlation with clinical markers like lactate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current sepsis severity indices (like SOFA) use fixed, outdated variables and weights established decades ago, calibrated to cohorts that no longer reflect modern critical care. There's a need for a real-time, dynamic index learned directly from contemporary patient trajectories.

Method: Retrospective two-cohort study on 29,116 + 7,691 sepsis patients from two hospital systems. Developed sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over 72-hour windows. Used mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal (not per-state target), allowing non-uniform credit redistribution across timesteps. Evaluated with 20% test holdout, clinical vignettes, Spearman correlation, and bootstrap uncertainty intervals.

Result: Non-survivors scored 1.19-1.64 points higher than survivors on 0-10 scale across SOFA-2 strata. Within-patient index changes correlated with lactate changes (rho=0.39). Cross-institutional agreement between models was 70-77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54-0.59 (ceiling 0.90-0.92). Index correlated with established indices while null controls stayed near zero.

Conclusion: The index demonstrates hourly prognostic information that meaningfully separates patient outcomes, aligns with clinical expectations, and shows potential as a decision support tool complementing clinical judgement for sepsis management.

Abstract: Currently used sepsis severity indices rely on fixed variables and weights established decades ago, which are coarsely discretized and calibrated to a cohort that no longer reflects contemporary critical care. No alternative learned directly from patient trajectories is in routine use. We conducted a retrospective two-cohort study on a total of 29,116 and 7,691 adult patients meeting Sepsis-3 criteria from two hospital systems in Massachusetts and Georgie, respectively.
  We developed a sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over a 72-hour treatment window. Unlike previous studies, we use mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal rather than a per-state target, allowing credit to be redistributed non-uniformly across timesteps. Evaluation was done on a permanent 20% test holdout, using clinical vignettes and Spearman correlation. Uncertainty intervals were obtained by bootstrap resampling of whole patients. Under this ranking scheme, non-survivors scored 1.19-1.64 points higher than survivors on a 0-10 scale within all strata of baseline SOFA-2, with similar results stratifying within lactate, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and creatinine. Within-patient change in the index correlated with change in lactate (Spearman rho = 0.39; n = 1,854). Similar, weaker correlations were found for MAP and creatinine. On a cohort level, cross-institutional agreement measured by Spearman correlation between models trained on different sites, were 70-77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 against ceilings of 0.92 and 0.90. Our index also correlated with established indices, while null controls stayed near zero.
  Our index demonstrated hourly prognostic information that meaningfully separates patient outcomes and is consistent with clinical expectation, indicating potential as a decision support tool complementing clinical judgement.

</details>


### [93] [Not All Eval-Awareness Is Equal: Capabilities Framing Predicts Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27340)
*Allison Zhuang,Santiago Aranguri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Eval-awareness in LLMs is not a single uniform quantity; it has distinct "capabilities" and "safety" framings that predict compliance differently, and suppressing eval-awareness overall doesn't necessarily reduce safety-relevant behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety evaluation pipelines treat eval-awareness as a single quantity to be suppressed, but this approach may not effectively target safety-relevant behaviors since different types of eval-awareness have different behavioral implications.

Method: Analyzed verbalized eval-awareness in chain-of-thought reasoning, categorizing it as capabilities-flavored, safety-flavored, both, or neither. Tested on Qwen3-32B using the FORTRESS dataset, comparing compliance predictions across framings. Conducted causal analysis via CoT-prefill interventions on eval-awareness-negative rollouts.

Result: Capabilities-framing predicted compliance with 24-46 percentage-point higher gap than safety-framing across steering conditions. CoT-prefill intervention showed causal link with 10 of 11 prefills shifting compliance in predicted direction. Same aggregate suppression rates can correspond to different behavioral outcomes.

Conclusion: Eval-awareness is behaviorally non-uniform; aggregate suppression metrics are insufficient for safety evaluation. Need to distinguish between capabilities-flavored and safety-flavored eval-awareness as they drive different compliance behaviors.

Abstract: Steering interventions targeting eval-awareness, a model's recognition that it is being tested, are increasingly used in safety evaluation pipelines, where evaluation-awareness is treated as a single quantity to be suppressed. We show that verbalized eval-awareness in chain-of-thought can be identified as capabilities-flavored ("the user is testing my ability to follow instructions"), safety-flavored ("the user is testing my boundaries"), both, or neither: framings that predict compliance very differently. On Qwen3-32B over the FORTRESS dataset, capabilities-framing predicts compliance with a +24 to +46 percentage-point gap over safety-framing across all tested steering conditions. A CoT-prefill intervention on eval-awareness-negative rollouts suggests the link is causal, with 10 of 11 prefills shifting compliance in the predicted direction. Then, eval-awareness is not behaviorally uniform: aggregate suppression rates can move while the safety-relevant component does not, and the same "X% suppression of eval-awareness" can correspond to qualitatively different behavioral outcomes.

</details>


### [94] [Sophistication in GenAI Use: Field Evidence from a Large Firm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27364)
*Nicholas J. Hallman,Zachary T. Kowaleski,Anu Puvvada,Jaime J. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Senior employees use generative AI more sophisticatedly, sophistication varies by function with highest use in strategic groups, and neither time nor training improves sophistication levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to understand how sophistication in generative AI use varies among back-office workers in large firms, providing insights that managers can use to improve outcomes and researchers can use for future studies.

Method: Analyzed proprietary data of 713,564 employee prompts and corresponding LLM responses from nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over eight months in 2025.

Result: 1) Senior employees show more sophisticated genAI use, suggesting domain expertise complements AI capabilities. 2) Highest sophistication in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management groups focused on strategic initiatives. 3) No improvements in sophistication over time or following formal AI training.

Conclusion: Sophisticated genAI use patterns are established early and difficult to change, with expertise and functional focus being key determinants; these insights can help managers target interventions and researchers develop better metrics.

Abstract: We study how sophistication in generative AI (genAI) use varies among the back-office workforce of a large firm. Using proprietary data, we observe 713,564 employee prompts and their corresponding large language model responses from nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over eight months in 2025. We document three main findings. First, senior employees exhibit more sophisticated genAI use, consistent with domain expertise complementing genAI capabilities. Second, sophistication varies considerably across functions and is highest in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management, three groups that share a focus on firmwide strategic initiatives and organizational change. Third, we observe neither improvements in sophistication over time nor lasting improvements following formal AI training, suggesting that sophisticated use can be difficult to change. Together, our study provides measures of and insights into sophisticated genAI use that managers can use to improve outcomes and that researchers can use in future research.

</details>


### [95] [Mechanistic Reaction Prediction via Discrete Flow Matching on Graph-Structured Electron Occupation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27429)
*Nguyen Xuan-Vu,Octavian Susanu,Daniel Armstrong,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAELLE models chemical reactions as discrete flow matching over electron occupation vectors rather than molecular graphs, achieving competitive accuracy while enabling mechanistic interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing machine learning approaches for chemical reactions either generate products de novo or use heuristic graph edits on molecular topology, but chemical reactions are fundamentally transformations in electron space that these methods don't directly model.

Method: Formulates reactant-to-product mapping as Continuous-time Markov Chain over graph-structured integer-valued electron occupation space, then generalizes discrete flow matching using Optimal Transport to create intermediate edit trajectories of electron rearrangements.

Result: Achieves competitive performance on USPTO-480K benchmark, maintains strong out-of-distribution robustness across structural complexity and reaction type settings, and naturally recovers mechanistic trajectories that align with known chemistry and can predict side products.

Conclusion: MAELLE provides a principled approach to modeling chemical reactions as electron rearrangements that offers both competitive accuracy and mechanistic interpretability, bridging the gap between machine learning and chemical intuition.

Abstract: Chemical reactions are fundamentally transformations in electron space, yet most machine learning approaches model them either through \textit{de novo} generation of product molecules or through heuristic graph edits that operate directly on molecular topology.
  We introduce MAELLE (\textbf{M}ech\textbf{A}nistic \textbf{E}dit f\textbf{L}ow-matching on e\textbf{L}ectron r\textbf{E}arrangements), which instead models reactions as discrete flow matching over electron occupation vectors.
  Concretely, we formulate the reactant-to-product mapping as a Continuous-time Markov Chain (CTMC) over the graph-structured integer-valued electron occupation space defined on all bonding, non-bonding, and hydrogen sites.
  To construct the intermediate edit trajectories, we generalize the discrete flow matching mixture path to discrete electron rearrangements using Optimal Transport, yielding a sequence of mechanistically interpretable edit moves without requiring elementary step annotations.
  MAELLE achieves competitive performance on the USPTO-480K benchmark compared with leading reaction prediction models.
  Beyond in-distribution accuracy, we evaluate robustness across two out-of-distribution settings - structural complexity and reaction type - and find that MAELLE maintains strong performance where existing methods degrade.
  Finally, because the learned flow operates over the full electron redistribution, MAELLE naturally recovers mechanistic trajectories that align with known chemistry and can predict side products of a reaction.

</details>


### [96] [WikiSkill: Compiling Agent Experience into Persistent Knowledge for Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27454)
*Liyan Tang,Cyrus Rashtchian,Chun-Sung Ferng,Andrew Tomkins,Da-Cheng Juan,Tu Vu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WikiSkill is a framework that co-evolves AI agent skills with a persistent knowledge base (wiki), enabling systematic accumulation and reuse of agent experience for better skill development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current skill evolution methods scatter insights across optimization histories, limiting systematic reuse across iterations. Skills are developed from agent experience but lack persistent knowledge accumulation.

Method: WikiSkill separates raw execution experience, accumulated knowledge (in a wiki), and executable skills. It continuously consolidates experience into the wiki, which subsequent skill updates can build on, enabling co-evolution of skills with persistent knowledge.

Result: WikiSkill outperforms state-of-the-art skill-evolution methods across diverse benchmarks and models. It shows skill evolution complements model scaling (larger models benefit more), skills transfer effectively across models and families, and skills evolved by other models can outperform self-evolved skills.

Conclusion: Persistent knowledge accumulation in the wiki is critical for effective skill evolution. Systematically accumulating and refining agent experience enables development of reusable and transferable skills, demonstrating the benefits of knowledge consolidation in skill evolution.

Abstract: Agent skills package specialized knowledge and workflows into reusable resources that extend AI agent capabilities. Recent work automatically discovers such skills from agent experience, which enables agents to progressively adapt through interaction. However, the insights that guide skill development typically remain scattered across optimization histories, limiting their systematic reuse across iterations. We introduce WikiSkill, a framework that co-evolves agent skills with a persistent knowledge base (wiki). At a high level, WikiSkill separates raw execution experience, accumulated knowledge, and executable skills, while continuously consolidating experience into the wiki, which subsequent skill updates can build on. Across diverse benchmarks and models, WikiSkill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art skill-evolution methods and improves over no-skill baselines in most model-benchmark settings. We find that skill evolution complements model scaling: larger models generally benefit more from evolved skills, while smaller models with skills can outperform substantially larger models without them. We also find that evolved skills transfer effectively across models and model families, and skills evolved by other models can outperform self-evolved skills. Finally, our ablation studies confirm that persistent knowledge accumulation in the wiki is critical for effective skill evolution. These results demonstrate the benefits of systematically accumulating and refining agent experience for developing reusable and transferable skills.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [97] [SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing for Labeled Property Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26132)
*Michal Podstawski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel graph neural network architecture that integrates a small language model (SLM) to dynamically route messages in labeled property graphs, enabling semantic-aware prediction while maintaining structural integrity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional graph neural networks treat textual/categorical properties as static feature vectors, which limits their ability to selectively use semantic evidence for specific prediction targets in labeled property graphs.

Method: Proposes SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing: uses a topology GNN as structural anchor, combines neighbor/relationship properties into messages, processes them with a parameter-efficient SLM to generate routing queries, and performs hierarchical routing at neighbor and relationship-type levels.

Result: Creates a bounded residual update to the topology anchor that preserves structural evidence while allowing contextual semantic information to modify predictions, supporting interpretable analysis at multiple levels.

Conclusion: The architecture provides a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning while maintaining structural integrity and enabling interpretable hierarchical routing.

Abstract: Labeled property graphs combine relational structure with heterogeneous textual and categorical properties attached to both nodes and relationships. Conventional graph neural networks typically represent these properties as static feature vectors, limiting their ability to determine which semantic evidence should influence message propagation for a particular prediction target. We propose SLM-Conditioned Hierarchical Relation Routing, an architecture that integrates a small language model directly into graph message selection. A topology GNN provides a stable structural representation and prediction anchor. For each target node, incident messages combine the neighbor's structural state, node-property encoding, relationship-property encoding, and relationship type. A parameter-efficient SLM processes structured graph soft tokens and produces a target-conditioned routing query. This query first selects relevant messages within each relationship type and subsequently routes information across relation-level summaries. The resulting representation provides a bounded residual update to the topology anchor, preserving structural evidence while allowing contextual semantic information to modify the prediction. The architecture supports interpretable analysis at both the neighbor and relationship-type levels and provides a general mechanism for integrating language-derived semantics into property-rich graph learning.

</details>


### [98] [NeuronFuzz: Safety Neuron Guided Fuzzing for LLM Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26222)
*Zhiyuan Xu,Muhammad Firhard Roslan,Joseph Gardiner,Sana Belguith,Lichao Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeuronFuzz is a white-box fuzzing framework that uses safety neuron activations as continuous feedback to efficiently jailbreak aligned LLMs, achieving high success rates without needing response generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated safety testing methods are expensive and inefficient because they rely on response-level feedback, requiring full response generation for each candidate prompt. This provides only sparse guidance on strongly aligned models where most candidates fail with the same outcome.

Method: NeuronFuzz uses a SafetyOracle that converts internal safety neuron activations into continuous safety alarm scores obtainable during prefill (eliminating response generation). It identifies compact sets of safety neurons through template-invariant harmful/benign inputs and stability-aware selection. The differentiable safety alarm score allows gradient-based identification of safety-sensitive template positions and fluent mutations using masked language models.

Result: Achieved 76-100% jailbreak discovery rate across five white-box source models, outperforming baselines by up to 48 percentage points. Optimized templates transferred zero-shot to open-weight and six proprietary models with average ASR/top-5 EASR of 69.6%/92.6% and 44.1%/60.0%, respectively, evaluated across 21 text and multimodal models.

Conclusion: NeuronFuzz demonstrates that exploiting internal safety neuron activations as continuous feedback enables efficient and effective LLM safety evaluation, providing dense guidance for fuzzing and achieving high jailbreak success rates across diverse models through gradient-based optimization and template transfer.

Abstract: Safety evaluation is critical for assessing whether aligned Large Language Models (LLMs) remain robust against jailbreak attacks. Existing automated testing methods, however, largely rely on response-level feedback: each candidate prompt typically requires generating a target-model response to evaluate its attack effectiveness. This process is expensive and, more importantly, provides only sparse guidance on strongly aligned models, where most candidates are rejected with the same failure outcome.
  This paper presents NeuronFuzz, a white-box fuzzing framework that exploits internal safety neurons as continuous execution feedback for LLM safety evaluation. A SafetyOracle converts safety-neuron activations into a continuous safety alarm score that serves as feedback for fuzzing and can be obtained during prefill, eliminating response generation from the fuzzing loop. To construct the SafetyOracle, NeuronFuzz uses template-invariant harmful and benign inputs and stability-aware selection to identify a compact set of safety neurons whose activations capture harmful-intent recognition. Moreover, since the safety alarm score is differentiable, NeuronFuzz uses its gradients to identify safety-sensitive template positions and a masked language model to generate fluent, context-compatible mutations while preserving original harmful payload and avoiding additional optimization variables. We evaluate NeuronFuzz across 21 text and multimodal models. Across five white-box source models, it achieves a 76-100% jailbreak discovery rate, outperforming baselines by up to 48 percentage points. Its optimized templates further transfer zero-shot to open-weight and six proprietary target models, achieving average ASR and top-5 ensemble ASR (EASR) of 69.6%/92.6% and 44.1%/60.0%, respectively.

</details>


### [99] [Pruning Binarized Neural Networks: A Dedicated Framework and Globally Weighted Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26233)
*Roan Rubiales,Jean Pierre David*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for designing and optimizing binarized neural networks with novel pruning methods that achieve 70% pruning rate with constant accuracy, significantly outperforming existing approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme compression of neural networks (including full binarization) is needed for deployment on constrained edge hardware, but existing pruning strategies are ill-suited for binarized representations and don't translate into meaningful hardware savings.

Method: Created a PyTorch-based research framework with freezing and pruning mechanisms for binarized neural networks. Proposed novel pruning method accounting for relative importance of learned parameters across abstraction levels using global weighting mechanism.

Result: The method consistently achieves superior trade-off between model accuracy and pruning rate, achieving 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with constant accuracy, while state-of-the-art results reach only 41% in binarized setting.

Conclusion: The framework enables rapid evaluation of binarized networks and prototyping of new methods, with the proposed global weighting pruning approach significantly outperforming existing methods for binarized neural network compression.

Abstract: Extreme compression of deep neural networks, up to full binarization, dramatically reduces memory footprint and arithmetic complexity, facilitating deployment on constrained edge hardware with field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) and microcontrollers. Although combining binarization with pruning promises additional efficiency gains, existing pruning strategies are ill-suited to binarized representations and rarely translate into meaningful hardware savings. We introduce a PyTorch-based, research-oriented framework that incorporates freezing and pruning mechanisms for designing and optimizing binarized neural networks. The framework enables rapid and reproducible evaluation of state-of-the-art approaches and the fast prototyping of new ones. Leveraging this framework, we propose a novel pruning method that accounts for the relative importance of learned parameters across abstraction levels. Such a global weighting mechanism consistently achieves a superior trade-off between model accuracy and pruning rate, achieving a 70% pruning rate on VGG11 with constant accuracy, while state-of-the-art results reach only 41% in the binarized setting.

</details>


### [100] [Muon with Finite Newton-Schulz: The Smoothing Benefit in Nonsmooth Nonconvex Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26288)
*Mingyi Li,Taira Tsuchiya*

Main category: cs.LG
