</details>


### [126] [Technical Comparative Benchmarking Study: Advanced AI Hybrid Methods for Renewable Energy Farm Optimization and Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26613)
*Majid Masoumi,Asghar Dashtiy,Mohammad Dehghan,Mina Rajabi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Benchmark study comparing various ML approaches for renewable energy forecasting shows no single architecture is universally optimal; tree ensembles work best for structured data, graph networks for spatial interactions, and ensemble-recurrent hybrids for combined temporal-spatial problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide comprehensive benchmarking of various machine learning approaches for renewable energy forecasting under complementary scenarios, identifying which architectures work best for different types of wind energy conversion (WEC) data and tasks.

Method: Compared conventional ML, ensemble learning, deep neural networks, recurrent architectures, Transformers, graph-based models, and hybrid ensemble deep learning approaches using three datasets: large-scale WEC dataset, 16 WEC dataset, and operational 10-min SCADA measurements from Penmanshiel wind farm.

Result: 1) Tree ensembles (especially Extra Trees) performed best for structured WEC layout data with 63.7% MAE reduction vs MLP baseline. 2) STGCN achieved MAE=167.0 kW and R=0.93 by learning spatial-temporal interactions. 3) RF BiLSTM hybrid was overall best with MAE=150.5 kW, representing 75% reduction vs standalone LSTM and 10% improvement over STGCN.

Conclusion: No single AI architecture is universally optimal: randomized/boosted ensembles work best for structured WEC surrogate modeling, graph networks excel when explicit spatial interactions dominate, and ensemble recurrent hybrids provide strongest balance when nonlinear tabular relationships and temporal dynamics coexist.

Abstract: This study provides a comprehensive benchmarking of conventional machine learning (ML), ensemble learning, deep neural networks, recurrent architectures, Transformers, graph based models, and hybrid ensemble deep learning approaches under complementary renewable energy scenarios. Three datasets are considered: a large scale WEC dataset, a 16 WEC dataset, and operational 10 min SCADA measurements at the Penmanshiel wind farm. For structured WEC layout data, tree ensembles exhibited a clear advantage over conventional ML and neural predictors because randomized partitioning and boosting efficiently captured nonlinear layout power interactions without requiring explicit feature representation learning. The Extra Trees was the strongest model, achieving considerable results. Relative to the MLP baseline, this corresponds to an approximately 63.7% reduction in MAE, demonstrating the suitability of randomized tree ensembles for high dimensional structured WEC data. Also, STGCN reduced the MAE to approximately 167.0 kW and achieved R = 0.93 by explicitly learning spatial and temporal turbine interactions. The best overall forecasting accuracy was obtained by the RF BiLSTM hybrid, with an MAE=150.5 kW. Compared with standalone LSTM, this represents an approximately 75% reduction in MAE, while improving on STGCN by approximately 10.0%. Finally, the experiments reveal that no single AI architecture is universally optimal: randomized and boosted ensembles are particularly effective for structured WEC surrogate modeling, graph networks become advantageous when explicit spatial interactions dominate, and ensemble recurrent hybrids provide the strongest balance when nonlinear tabular relationships and temporal dynamics coexist.

</details>


### [127] [Robust Neural Stimulation Response Modeling Through Meta-Learning and Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26649)
*Matthew J Bryan,Daniel C Muir,Felix Schwock,Azadeh Yazdan-Shahmorad,Rajesh P N Rao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Meta-learning (MAML) applied to neural stimulation response modeling reduces catastrophic forecasting failures and cuts calibration requirements by 50-90%, enabling more robust closed-loop stimulation systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model-based closed-loop neural stimulation faces two major obstacles: 1) forecasting models often fail catastrophically on meaningful fractions of sessions, and 2) per-session calibration requirements are incompatible with clinical time constraints.

Method: Extended temporal basis function models (TBFMs) with cross-session pretraining using a novel architecture and algorithm based on model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML). Evaluated on 40 sessions of optogenetic stimulation in primary sensorimotor cortex of two non-human primates.

Result: Meta-learning substantially reduces catastrophic forecast failure: sessions with test R-squared < 0.05 drop from 16/40 (single-session training) to 1/40 (MAML-pretrained). Calibration requirements reduced by 50-90% at matched accuracy, enabling experiments otherwise infeasible within clinical constraints.

Conclusion: Cross-session structure in stimulation responses is consistent enough to support pretraining, providing first empirical evidence that meta-learning approaches are viable for neural stimulation. Gains in robustness and sample efficiency directly address obstacles to deploying model-based stimulation controllers.

Abstract: Objective: Model-based closed-loop neural stimulation holds promise for therapeutic applications ranging from Parkinson's disease to sensory restoration, but deployment has been limited by two obstacles: 1) forecasting models for predicting the consequences of stimulation fail catastrophically on a meaningful fraction of sessions, and 2) per-session calibration requirements are often incompatible with clinical constraints. We address both by demonstrating, for the first time, that meta-learning and pretraining can be applied to neural stimulation response modeling. Methods: Temporal basis function models (TBFMs) forecast state-dependent neural responses to stimulation. We extend TBFMs with cross-session pretraining using a novel architecture and algorithm based on model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML), evaluating them on 40 sessions of optogenetic stimulation in primary sensorimotor cortex of two non-human primates. Results: Meta-learning substantially reduces catastrophic forecast failure: for a 1k calibration set size, sessions with test R-squared < 0.05 drop from 16 of 40 (single-session training) to 1 (MAML-pretrained), and prediction intervals become significantly narrower (p < 0.05). Calibration requirements are reduced by 50-90% at matched accuracy, enabling experiments otherwise infeasible within clinical session-time constraints. Conclusion: Our results demonstrate that cross-session structure in stimulation responses is consistent enough to support pretraining, providing the first empirical evidence that meta-learning approaches are viable for neural stimulation. Significance: The robustness and sample efficiency gains directly address known obstacles to deploying model-based stimulation controllers. Our results motivate community efforts to assemble standardized multi-site stimulation datasets and to further explore meta-learning for robust closed-loop stimulation.

</details>


### [128] [When Privacy Hurts Mergeability: Geometry-Aware Model Merging under Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26655)
*Jin Liu,Junkang Liu,Ning Xi,Yinbin Miao,Dawei Wei,Ke Cheng,Jianfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DP-Merging: A geometry-aware framework that improves merging of differentially private task models by addressing local sharpness and reference drift through sharpness-aware optimization and alignment regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model merging enables multi-task models from independently fine-tuned task models without centralized data, but differentially private (DP) fine-tuned models are difficult to merge effectively due to privacy constraints. There's a gap in understanding how DP affects model merging, and current methods don't address geometric challenges specific to private task models.

Method: 1) Identifies two geometric obstacles: local sharpness (sensitivity to parameter displacement) and reference drift (displacement from pretrained initialization). 2) Proposes DP-Merging framework with: a) DP-compatible sharpness-aware objective to guide task models toward flatter loss regions, and b) reference-based alignment regularizer to keep task models close to shared pretrained initialization. 3) Derives theoretical merge-gap upper bound relating curvature and reference drift to merging performance.

Result: Experiments on vision and language tasks across multiple privacy budgets show DP-Merging consistently improves private merged-model performance while preserving the underlying DP guarantees. The theoretical analysis shows reduced local curvature and reference drift tighten the bound on loss increase induced by merging.

Conclusion: DP-Merging effectively addresses geometric challenges in merging differentially private task models through sharpness-aware optimization and alignment regularization, enabling better multi-task performance while maintaining privacy protection. The framework demonstrates practical improvements across domains while preserving DP guarantees.

Abstract: Model merging promises to construct a single multi-task model from independently fine-tuned task models without accessing the original task data. This makes it attractive when task data cannot be centralized, but released task models may still leak private fine-tuning data. Differential privacy (DP) provides a principled mechanism for limiting such leakage, yet its effect on model merging remains poorly understood. In this paper, we study the geometry of differentially private model merging and identify two geometric obstacles that make private task models difficult to merge: \emph{local sharpness}, which makes task losses sensitive to the parameter displacement induced by merging, and \emph{reference drift}, which measures the displacement of private task models from the shared pretrained initialization and amplifies cross-task interference. Based on these observations, we propose \textbf{DP-Merging}, a geometry-aware framework that improves the mergeability of differentially private task models. DP-Merging uses a DP-compatible sharpness-aware objective to guide each private task model toward flatter loss regions, and a reference-based alignment regularizer to keep task models close to the shared pretrained initialization. We derive a merge-gap upper bound showing that reducing local curvature and reference drift tightens the bound on the loss increase induced by merging. Experiments on vision and language tasks across multiple privacy budgets show that DP-Merging consistently improves private merged-model performance while preserving the privacy guarantees of the underlying DP fine-tuning procedures.

</details>


### [129] [Simple Actors and Deep Critics for Scalable Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26659)
*Guhyeon Kang,Jaehwi Lee,Minhae Kwon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LAC proposes a lightweight actor with deep critic architecture for offline RL, achieving state-of-the-art performance with 4x lower inference latency than diffusion policies, without distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current expressive offline RL actors (diffusion/flow-matching policies) require multiple denoising steps per action, causing high inference latency. Since critics are only used during training while actors run at every decision step, shifting capacity from actors to critics can improve deployment efficiency.

Method: Proposes LAC with three key ingredients: 1) residual MLP backbone for stable optimization of deep critics, 2) n-step bootstrap targets to reduce bootstrap-noise amplification, and 3) categorical cross-entropy loss to prevent value-range drift. Uses lightweight deterministic actor paired with deep critic.

Result: On OGBench, LAC matches performance of diffusion and flow-matching baselines while achieving up to 4x lower inference latency. Its critic recipe transfers across different actor parametrizations and achieves inference speed comparable to one-step distilled policies without requiring distillation.

Conclusion: Investing capacity in critics rather than actors is favorable for offline RL deployment efficiency. The proposed deep critic architecture with three stabilizing ingredients enables effective offline RL with lightweight actors, bridging the performance gap between expressive generative actors and efficient deployment.

Abstract: Recent progress in offline reinforcement learning (RL) has been driven by expressive generative actors such as diffusion and flow-matching policies, which capture multimodal behavior in offline datasets. However, these actors require multiple denoising or integration steps per action and thus incur substantial overhead at every decision in deployment. In this work, we revisit where capacity should be invested in an offline actor--critic method. Since the critic is used only during training and is discarded at deployment while the actor runs at every decision step, allocating capacity to the critic rather than the actor is more favorable for inference-time efficiency. However, scaling MLP critics in offline RL is known to introduce several distinct instabilities that have, in practice, kept critics shallow. We identify three distinct failure modes that arise when critics are deepened in offline RL---optimization, bootstrap-noise amplification, and value-range drift---and address each with a corresponding ingredient: a residual MLP backbone, n-step bootstrap targets, and a categorical cross-entropy loss. Combining these ingredients with a lightweight deterministic actor, we propose LAC (Light Actor, deep Critic). On OGBench, LAC matches the strongest diffusion- and flow-matching baselines while achieving up to 4x lower inference latency, comparable to one-step distilled policies without distillation. Its critic recipe also transfers across actor parametrizations.

</details>


### [130] [Neural Regression with Embeddings for Numerical Attribute Prediction in Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26729)
*Rupesh Sapkota,Louis Mozart Kamdem Teyou,Moshood Yekini,Caglar Demir,Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LitEm enables transductive knowledge graph embedding models to predict numerical attributes, and a co-training framework jointly trains KG embedding models with LitEm to improve both link prediction and numerical attribute prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge graphs contain rich numerical attributes that most embedding models neglect, limiting their ability to represent real-world knowledge graphs with diverse information. Current transductive knowledge graph embedding models focus mainly on link prediction but ignore numerical attributes.

Method: Proposes LitEm (neural regression model) that enables transductive KG embedding models to predict numerical attributes. Also introduces a co-training framework that jointly trains state-of-the-art transductive KG embedding models with LitEm.

Result: LitEm achieves best or second-best results on most attributes across FB15K-237, YAGO15K, DB15K, and Mutagenesis datasets. Co-training improves link prediction performance (mainly for bilinear models) while enabling numerical attribute prediction. Literal-awareness evaluation shows models encode attribute information in a "literal-aware" manner.

Conclusion: The proposed LitEm model effectively addresses the limitation of existing KG embedding models by incorporating numerical attribute prediction, and the co-training framework demonstrates that gains are not merely due to additional parameters but from encoding attribute information meaningfully.

Abstract: In recent years, transductive knowledge graph embedding models have been applied to tasks such as link prediction and query answering. Although knowledge graphs often contain rich numerical attributes, most embedding models neglect them, limiting their ability to represent real-world knowledge graphs with diverse information. In this work, we propose a neural regression model (LitEm) that enables transductive knowledge graph embedding models to predict numerical attributes within knowledge graphs. Experimental results demonstrate that LitEm achieves the best or second-best results on most attributes across FB15K-237, YAGO15K, DB15K, and Mutagenesis. Furthermore, we propose a co-training framework that jointly trains state-of-the-art transductive knowledge graph embedding models with LitEm, which improves link prediction performance mainly for bilinear models and simultaneously enables them to predict numerical attributes. In addition, the literal-awareness evaluation demonstrates that co-training helps models to encode and exploit attribute information in a "literal-aware'' manner, suggesting that the observed gains are not merely due to additional parameters. We publicly release our implementation at https://github.com/dice-group/dice-embeddings.

</details>


### [131] [Rethinking Message Passing as Retrieval for Text-Attributed Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26732)
*Jintang Li,Yuhong Chen,Ruofan Wu,Binli Luo,Jiayi Ji,Hui Li,Rongrong Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RTA replaces message passing in GNNs with label-aware retrieval and propagation, achieving comparable or better performance than GNNs while being more efficient and robust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional GNNs based on message passing are computationally expensive and sensitive to imperfect graph structures, despite their empirical success. The paper seeks to understand why neighborhood aggregation outperforms simple MLPs and proposes a more efficient alternative.

Method: RTA (Retrieval-based Transformer Architecture) - an MLP-based framework that replaces structural message passing with label-aware retrieval and propagation. Each layer applies an MLP to node representations along with a permutation-invariant summary of retrieved graph context.

Result: RTA matches or outperforms strong GNN and graph LLM baselines on multiple text-attributed graph benchmarks. It demonstrates improved efficiency and robustness across diverse scenarios while maintaining competitive performance.

Conclusion: The retrieval-augmented view provides a promising alternative to traditional message-passing GNNs, offering theoretical grounding for robustness against mis-retrieved outliers and practical benefits in efficiency and performance.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are typically conceptualized as message-passing neural networks, yet it remains unclear why neighborhood aggregation reliably outperforms node-wise multilayer perceptrons (MLPs). Despite its empirical success, this paradigm can be computationally expensive and sensitive to imperfect graph structures. In this work, we present a retrieval-augmented view of GNNs: each layer makes predictions by applying an MLP to a node representation together with a permutation-invariant summary of retrieved graph context. Motivated by this perspective, we propose RTA, a simple MLP-based framework that replaces structural message passing with label-aware retrieval and propagation. We provide theoretical insights that (i) connect retrieval-based aggregation to softmax-attention message passing, and (ii) establish the robustness of retrieved-context supervision to mis-retrieved outliers. Experiments on multiple text-attributed graph benchmarks show that RTA matches or even outperforms strong GNN and graph LLM baselines while improving efficiency and robustness across diverse scenarios.

</details>


### [132] [Self-Augmented Diffusion Guidance for Physics-Informed Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26748)
*Akira Osaka,Naoya Takeishi,Takehisa Yairi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-informed diffusion model that uses self-generated data augmentation and conditional guidance to generate physically accurate spatiotemporal signals while avoiding expensive numerical simulations during sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diffusion models generate visually plausible spatiotemporal signals (like fluid dynamics) but often violate underlying physical laws, leading to samples that deviate from true dynamics despite appearing realistic.

Method: Proposes a physics-informed approach using diffusion guidance with self-generated data augmentation. The method learns the data distribution conditioned on deviation from physical correctness, then generates samples by setting deviation condition to zero. Crucially, it decouples governing equation evaluation from diffusion training/sampling, avoiding expensive numerical simulations during denoising iterations.

Result: The proposed model significantly reduces physical deviations compared to standard diffusion models. It achieves further improvements when combined with existing physics-constrained diffusion methods. The approach enables faster sample generation while maintaining physical accuracy.

Conclusion: The method provides an effective way to incorporate physical constraints into diffusion models for spatiotemporal signals without requiring computationally expensive simulations during the sampling process, making it suitable for complex physical phenomena where numerical simulations are costly.

Abstract: Diffusion models can be used to generate spatiotemporal signals of physical phenomena, such as time-series images of fluid dynamics. However, a major limitation of standard diffusion models is that they do not incorporate constraints derived from the underlying physical laws. Consequently, generated samples may appear visually plausible while deviating substantially from the true dynamics. In this study, we propose a simple yet effective physics-informed approach based on diffusion guidance with self-generated data augmentation. The proposed method learns the data distribution conditioned on the degree of deviation from the physically correct dynamics and generates samples by explicitly setting the deviation condition to be zero. The method decouples the evaluation of the governing equations from the diffusion model training and sampling processes, avoiding the need to solve the governing equations at every iteration of the denoising process. This design makes the method applicable to problems requiring computationally expensive numerical simulations and enables faster sample generation. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model not only significantly reduces the deviations compared with standard diffusion models but also achieves further reductions when combined with existing physics-constrained diffusion methods.

</details>


### [133] [Safety by Design: Realized-Cost Constraints for Contextual Bandits with Continuous Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26755)
*Spyros Dragazis,Aldo Pacchiano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HPC-UCB algorithm for contextual bandits with continuous actions and high-probability constraints on realized costs, not just expected costs, achieving tight regret bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety-constrained bandit models only constrain expected costs, which is insufficient for heteroscedastic settings where actions affect outcome variability. Single unsafe decisions can have catastrophic consequences in applications like dosage selection or autonomous driving.

Method: Propose High-Probability Constrained UCB (HPC-UCB), an optimistic-pessimistic algorithm that explores for reward while conservatively estimating the safe action set using confidence intervals for both reward and cost functions.

Result: Prove tight Õ(d√T) regret bound for linear reward and cost models, extend to general function classes using eluder dimension, and show experiments demonstrate substantial reduction in cost violations compared to expected-cost constrained baselines.

Conclusion: Enforcing realized-cost safety rather than just expected-cost safety is crucial for practical safety-critical applications, and HPC-UCB provides a principled approach with strong theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: Contextual bandits are a standard framework for sequential decision-making under uncertainty, with applications in clinical trials, dosage selection, recommendation systems, and autonomous systems. Safety is central in many of these applications, since a single unsafe decision in settings such as dosage selection or autonomous driving can have catastrophic consequences. A common way to model safety in bandit problems is to associate each action with both a reward signal and a cost signal, and to optimize reward subject to constraints on cost. Most existing safety-constrained bandit models enforce safety by requiring the expected cost of each action to remain below a prescribed threshold. However, this may be insufficient in heteroscedastic settings, where the chosen action affects not only the expected reward and cost, but also the variability of the observed outcomes. We study contextual bandits with one-dimensional continuous actions and stage-wise high-probability constraints on the realized cost. We propose High-Probability Constrained UCB, an optimistic-pessimistic algorithm that explores for reward while conservatively estimating the safe action set. For linear reward and cost models, we prove a tight $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})$ regret bound, and we extend the analysis to general function classes using the eluder dimension. Experiments show that enforcing realized-cost safety substantially reduces violations compared with expected-cost constrained baselines.

</details>


### [134] [Beyond Client Averaging: A Client-Independent Second-Order Stationary-Bias Component in Stochastic SCAFFOLD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26765)
*Yi-Ping Tang,Guan-Ju Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper provides a refined bias analysis of stochastic SCAFFOLD for homogeneous one-dimensional problems, showing client averaging suppresses O(γ/N) bias but leaves an O(γ²) client-independent bias that persists as client count increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous analysis of stochastic SCAFFOLD identified O(γ/N) stationary mean bias but didn't characterize the first client-independent contribution at coefficient level. Understanding the persistence of higher-order bias is crucial for federated learning optimization.

Method: Theoretical analysis of full-participation stochastic SCAFFOLD with one-dimensional homogeneous clients, fixed local-step count H, and bounded additive gradient noise. Uses uniform analysis over N≥2 to derive exact bias coefficients.

Result: Proved exact formula showing bias = -(f'''(x⁺)σ²)/(4f''(x⁺)²) γ/N - (f'''(x⁺)σ²)/(12f''(x⁺)) ((H-1)(5H-1)/H) γ² + O_H(γ²/N + γ³). Client averaging suppresses O(γ/N) bias but O(γ²) component remains client-independent when coefficient nonzero.

Conclusion: Client averaging in stochastic SCAFFOLD suppresses leading O(γ/N) bias but cannot eliminate client-independent O(γ²) bias when coefficient is nonzero. The mechanism involves indirect effects: controls alter local trajectories and their second moments, which nonquadratic curvature converts into stationary mean bias.

Abstract: Existing constant-step analysis of stochastic \Scaf{} identifies a leading $O(γ/N)$ stationary mean bias and shows that higher-order bias can persist as the client count increases, but does not identify the first client-independent contribution at coefficient level. For full-participation stochastic \Scaf{} with one-dimensional homogeneous clients, fixed local-step count $H$, and bounded additive gradient noise, we prove, uniformly over $N\ge2$,
  $$ \begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{π_{γ,N,H}}[x]-x^\star ={}& -\frac{f'''(x^\star)σ^2}{4f''(x^\star)^2}\fracγ{N}\\ &- \frac{f'''(x^\star)σ^2}{12f''(x^\star)} \frac{(H-1)(5H-1)}{H}γ^2 +O_H\!\left(\frac{γ^2}{N}+γ^3\right). \end{aligned} $$
  Hence client averaging suppresses the leading $O(γ/N)$ bias but does not remove the client-independent $O(γ^2)$ component when its coefficient is nonzero.
  The mechanism is indirect: although the direct control contribution cancels pathwise in the linear global average, the controls still alter within-round local trajectories and their second moments. Fresh gradient noise and persistent control fluctuations therefore generate local second-moment corrections that nonquadratic curvature converts into stationary mean bias. The coefficient vanishes for quadratic objectives. Numerical experiments are consistent with the predicted coefficient, its persistence as client count increases, and the stated joint remainder. The result is restricted to the one-dimensional homogeneous fixed-$H$ setting.

</details>


### [135] [On the Indistinguishability of Human v/s AI Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26797)
*Jaee Ponde,Aritra Das,Mihir More,Debayan Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-generated text detection is challenged by paraphrasing tools that make AI text appear more human. The paper shows how using human writing samples as references during paraphrasing can systematically shift machine-generated text toward the human distribution, with provable convergence rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid advancement of LLMs has created an urgent need to distinguish AI-generated text from human writing. This problem is exacerbated by paraphrasing tools designed to make machine-generated text appear more "human-like," creating a detection arms race.

Method: The study uses a multi-sample setting with both human and machine responses to the same prompts. The researchers analyze how strategic paraphrasing with access to human writing samples can move the machine distribution toward the empirical human distribution. They establish theoretical conditions for convergence and derive explicit convergence rates.

Result: The research proves that repeated paraphrasing with access to human samples moves the machine distribution toward the human distribution under simple mixing and stability conditions. They derive explicit convergence rates, extend the analysis to finite-sample settings, and characterize how the required number of human samples and paraphrasing rounds scale with desired error levels.

Conclusion: Access to human writing samples during paraphrasing provides a systematic way to make AI-generated text more human-like, with mathematically provable convergence properties. This formalizes the threat posed by advanced paraphrasing techniques to AI text detection systems.

Abstract: The rapid improvement of LLMs has made distinguishing AI-generated text from human writing a pressing problem. This challenge is further amplified by paraphrasing tools designed to make machine-generated text appear more "human". We study how access to human writing samples can be used to strategically paraphrase machine-generated responses toward the human distribution. Under a multi-sample setting with human and machine responses to the same prompts, we show that repeated paraphrasing moves the machine distribution toward the empirical human distribution under simple mixing and stability conditions. Our results derive an explicit convergence rate, extend the analysis to a finite-sample setting, and characterize how the required number of human samples and paraphrasing rounds scale with the desired error.

</details>


### [136] [SAGE: Variate-Wise Semantic Augmentation for Vision-Language Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26829)
*Haizhao Fan,Xinyi Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE is a CLIP-based time series forecasting framework that integrates temporal, textual, and visual information to provide semantic knowledge without expensive LLM inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series forecasting models lack semantic knowledge that domain experts use (physical meaning, statistical behavior, temporal dynamics). Existing approaches either rely on expensive LLM inference or use uniform textual prompts that ignore variable-specific semantics.

Method: Proposes SAGE, an end-to-end CLIP-based framework that jointly models temporal, cross-variable, textual, and visual information. Uses CLIP text encoder for frequency-enhanced patches and variable tokens with gated residual paths for variable-specific descriptions. Frozen CLIP vision encoder aligns rendered series with temporal representations through contrastive objective.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across eight long-term benchmarks and M4. Ablations confirm complementary gains from multimodal alignment and variable-level knowledge.

Conclusion: SAGE successfully bridges the semantic gap in time series forecasting by incorporating domain knowledge through multimodal CLIP-based encoding without placing LLMs in the forecasting loop, providing computational efficiency while improving accuracy.

Abstract: Time series forecasting models operate on raw numerical sequences, lacking the semantic knowledge that domain experts implicitly leverage, such as the physical meaning of each variable, its statistical behavior, and its temporal dynamics. Recent efforts to bridge this gap fall into two camps. Some rely on large language models at inference time, which is computationally expensive. Others apply uniform textual prompts at the dataset level, ignoring the heterogeneous semantics across individual variates. We propose SAGE (Seeing and Augmenting with Grounded Encoding), an end-to-end CLIP-based framework that jointly models temporal, cross-variable, textual, and visual information. The CLIP text encoder processes frequency-enhanced patches and variable tokens, while gated residual paths inject variable-specific descriptions and statistical descriptors. In parallel, the frozen CLIP vision encoder aligns rendered series with temporal representations through a training-only contrastive objective. This dual use of CLIP adds complementary semantic and visual supervision without placing an LLM in the forecasting loop. Across eight long-term benchmarks and M4, SAGE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy. Ablations confirm complementary gains from multimodal alignment and variable-level knowledge.

</details>


### [137] [Reinforcement Learning-Based Control of CAV Platoon Joining Maneuvers in Mixed Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26860)
*Biao Yin,Abderrahmane Kasmi,Nadir Farhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a reinforcement learning framework for CAV platoon joining in mixed traffic, showing PPO outperforms DQN/DDQN with ~98% success rate but requires more decision steps, revealing a safety-efficiency trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CAV platooning can improve road safety and traffic capacity, but real-world platoon control is challenging due to uncertainty and heterogeneous driving behaviors in mixed-traffic environments where CAVs coexist with human-driven vehicles.

Method: Developed a generic modeling/simulation framework using agent-based modeling coupled with SUMO simulator to evaluate DRL algorithms (DQN, DDQN, PPO) for CAV platoon joining, with two safety approaches: incorporating risk penalties in rewards or using external safety controllers.

Result: PPO outperformed DQN and DDQN with ~98% joining success rate and <1% collision rate, mainly due to risk penalties in the reward function. However, PPO required more decision steps, revealing a trade-off between safety and decision efficiency. External safety controllers effectively prevented collisions but reduced joining efficiency.

Conclusion: The results highlight the importance of jointly considering safety and efficiency when designing RL-based controllers for CAV platoon joining in mixed traffic environments, with PPO showing strong performance when properly configured with safety considerations.

Abstract: Connected and automated vehicle (CAV) platooning offers a promising approach to improving road safety and traffic capacity. However, platoon control in real-world traffic is challenging due to uncertainty and heterogeneous driving behaviors. Reinforcement learning (RL) has strong potential for addressing such control problems, but its practical deployment raises challenges related to safety and learning efficiency. This paper proposes a generic modeling and simulation framework for investigating CAV platoon joining maneuvers and comparing deep reinforcement learning (DRL)-based control algorithms. The problem is particularly challenging in mixed-traffic environments, where CAVs coexist with human-driven vehicles exhibiting heterogeneous longitudinal and lateral behaviors. The objective is to achieve safe and efficient joining maneuvers by either incorporating penalties for risky behaviors into the learning process or using an external safety controller to constrain the learned policy. An agent-based modeling framework coupled with the Simulation of Urban MObility (SUMO) simulator is used to evaluate Deep Q-Network (DQN), Double Deep Q-Network (DDQN), and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Results show that PPO outperforms DQN and DDQN, achieving a joining success rate of approximately 98 % and a collision rate below 1 %, largely due to risk-related penalties incorporated into the reward function. However, this improved performance requires more decision steps to complete the maneuver, revealing a trade-off between safety, joining effectiveness, and decision efficiency. An external safety controller effectively prevents collisions, although its interventions may reduce joining efficiency. The results highlight the importance of jointly considering safety and efficiency when designing RL-based controllers for CAV platoon joining in mixed traffic.

</details>


### [138] [When Is the Sharp Covariance Envelope Tight? Feature-Only Geometry for Volume-Sampled Least Squares](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26877)
*Kihun Rhee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes a Loewner envelope for coefficient covariance under volume sampling with unweighted least squares, providing sharp spectral phase characterization and practical certificates for cardinality decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work by Derezinski and Warmuth established identities for ordinary volume sampling but only provided exact formulas at the rank-size endpoint. There is a need to understand coefficient covariance properties for all valid sampling budgets (d ≤ s ≤ m) under volume sampling with unweighted least squares.

Method: The paper establishes a Loewner envelope for centered coefficient covariance under ordinary indexed fixed-size volume sampling followed by selected unweighted least squares. It uses a residual-augmented change of measure for response-aware analysis, support saturation for attainment direction, and critical equal-leverage geometry for boundary interpretation.

Result: The coefficient covariance envelope is globally sharp over the full-rank class. A feature-only margin ν_A characterizes the exact fixed-design spectral phase: ν_A > 0 indicates strict spectral envelope for all compatible residuals, while ν_A = 0 indicates some residual is spectrally tight. The same zero-margin residual is tight at every strict-interior budget. Sound lower certificates provide conservative cardinality decisions, with examples showing non-vacuous certificates.

Conclusion: The paper provides a complete characterization of coefficient covariance under volume sampling with unweighted least squares, establishing sharp spectral properties and practical certificates for decision-making. The analysis focuses on conditional centered, full-Gram-whitened coefficient covariance rather than population generalization.

Abstract: Prior analyses by Derezinski and Warmuth established all-size sampling identities, selected-OLS unbiasedness, and inverse moments for ordinary volume sampling, while their exact arbitrary-fixed-response loss and prediction-covariance formulas are at the rank-size endpoint s=d. We establish a Loewner envelope for centered coefficient covariance for every full-rank fixed pool, response, and legal budget d <= s <= m under ordinary indexed fixed-size volume sampling followed by selected unweighted least squares; its coefficient is globally sharp over the full-rank class. Global sharpness does not determine attainability on the pool in hand. Under positive loss, strict-interior budgets, and no coloops, a feature-only margin nu_A gives the exact fixed-design spectral phase: nu_A > 0 if and only if the normalized spectral envelope is strict for every compatible residual, whereas nu_A = 0 if and only if some compatible residual is spectrally tight; the same zero-margin residual is tight at every strict-interior budget. A residual-augmented change of measure supplies the response-aware mechanism and a one-sided quantitative slack bound, while support saturation proves the attainment direction. Critical equal-leverage geometry interprets the boundary, and sound lower certificates yield conservative same-primitive cardinality decisions. Frozen-feature examples show that the certificate is nonvacuous and measure the fixed-pool cost of its authorized reduction. The claims concern conditional centered, full-Gram-whitened coefficient covariance, not population generalization.

</details>


### [139] [Mitigating Strong-Modality Collapse in Multimodal Learning via Inverted Asymmetric Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26879)
*Mary Ogbuka Kenneth,Foaad Khosmood,Abbas Edalat*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multimodal fusion often fails to beat best unimodal baselines due to "strong-modality collapse" where dominant modality performance degrades during fusion. Proposed Inverted Asymmetric Fusion preserves dominant modality unchanged while weaker modalities attend to it, improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal fusion is expected to improve performance but often fails to surpass the best unimodal baseline. The authors identify "strong-modality collapse" where dominant modalities degrade during fusion, explaining why symmetric attention fusion underperforms.

Method: Proposed Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF): avoids mutual attention; dominant modality passes through fusion unchanged; weaker modalities attend to dominant modality as contextual anchor. Weaker modalities are strengthened using Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation before fusion.

Result: IAF preserves dominant modality's internal accuracy at unimodal ceiling across all tested configurations, while symmetric fusion degrades it by up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE. IAF improves over strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

Conclusion: Strong-modality collapse explains why some multimodal models fail to beat unimodal baselines. Inverted Asymmetric Fusion effectively preserves dominant modality while integrating weaker modalities, leading to consistent performance improvements across different modality hierarchies.

Abstract: Fusing multiple modalities is expected to improve model performance. However, on the MultiHuSE dataset, early, late, and symmetric attention fusion often fail to outperform the best unimodal baseline (text). Pathway isolation of a symmetric attention fusion model reveals that the text-pathway accuracy drops from 74.9% to 56.4% after fusion in one such setting, indicating that the dominant modality can be degraded during integration. We term this strong-modality collapse and argue that it helps explain why some multimodal models fail to surpass unimodal baselines. We propose Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF), which avoids forcing mutual attention across modalities. The dominant modality is preserved by passing through fusion unchanged, while weaker modalities attend to it as a contextual anchor. Before fusion, weaker modalities are strengthened using Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation. We evaluate IAF on three benchmarks with different modality hierarchies: text-dominant datasets (MultiHuSE, UR-FUNNY) and an audio-visual-dominant dataset (MUStARD). Pathway isolation shows that IAF preserves the dominant modality's internal accuracy at its unimodal ceiling across all tested configurations, whereas symmetric fusion degrades it by up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE. IAF improves over the strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

</details>


### [140] [A Layer Importance Metric for Quantization Accounting for the Speed-Quality Trade-off in Autoregressive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26926)
*Artem Safronov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A composite metric combining information retention and throughput gains for prioritizing which parts of small language models to quantize for optimal speed-quality trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language models run on devices with limited memory and computational resources. Inference is memory-bandwidth bound, and uniform quantization often harms these models since they have limited redundancies with only a few layers being insensitive to lower precision.

Method: Proposes a composite metric with two orthogonal criteria: 1) information retention measured via normalized SQNR-based coefficient, and 2) throughput gains modeled using roofline-based latency analysis. Profiles models to identify promising acceleration targets, estimates quality scores via simulated quantization and speed scores via roofline modeling, then combines them into a priority coefficient for tuning speed-quality trade-off.

Result: Evaluation shows the method has ~4% prediction error for accelerated speedup. It generally allocates more resources to the most expressive layers compared to evolutionary search, specialized accelerators, or Shapley-value-based approaches. The approach works for prioritizing individual blocks, projection sublayers, or entire transformer layers.

Conclusion: The analytical approach makes small language model quantization a predictable engineering task by providing a systematic method to prioritize quantization targets based on the desired speed-quality trade-off without requiring expensive approximate inference.

Abstract: Small language models (sLLMs) are nowadays hosted on devices with limited memory and computational budget. In an autoregressive setup, inference is memory-bandwidth bound: uniform quantization is often detrimental to such models, since their architecture has limited redundancies and only a few layers are not very sensitive to lower precision. We propose a composite metric that combines two orthogonal criteria: information retention (measured in terms of a normalized SQNR-based coefficient) and throughput gains (modeled using a roofline-based latency analysis). By profiling Gemma 3 1B, we find that Feed-Forward Network blocks and the embedding matrix are the most promising targets for acceleration. For each candidate, we estimate a normalized quality score based on simulated quantization and a normalized speed score based on roofline modeling with no actual execution needed. We combine the two scores in a composite priority coefficient, allowing us to tune the trade-off between speed and quality as needed. Our metric is general and can be used to prioritize individual blocks, their projection sublayers, or transformer layers as a whole. We evaluate our approach on several model architectures, showing that our estimates have at around 4% prediction error for the accelerated speedup. We find that our method generally allocates more resources to the most expressive layers compared to evolutionary search, specialized accelerators, or Shapley-value-based approaches that require expensive approximate inference. Our analytical approach makes sLLM quantization a predictable engineering task.

</details>


### [141] [Scaling Model-Generated Distillation Data Can Make Latent Teacher Traits More Recoverable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26958)
*Zhichen Dong,Zhixuan Liu,Yuyu Fan,Xiangtian Li,Shuyang Zhang,Chao Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling model-generated off-task data can amplify subtle teacher-specific traits in student models, even when the data doesn't mention the trait, requiring trait-aware curation and evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the conventional view that scaling model-generated data only improves distillation through better coverage and noise reduction. It investigates whether larger datasets might unintentionally amplify subtle teacher-specific traits in student models, even when the data appears off-task or benign.

Method: The authors use a controlled subliminal learning setup where a teacher model is induced to express a target trait and generates restricted off-task data (like number-only completions). Students are trained on varying amounts of independent off-task data, with matched no-trait controls to isolate target-specific transfer. Evaluation occurs in a separate domain, and analyses of learned LoRA updates track the effects.

Result: Larger independent datasets make the teacher's induced trait stand out more clearly in the student's later behavior. When small-scale students already favor the target trait, scaling amplifies that behavior; when they favor related or salient alternatives, more data can shift behavior toward the intended trait. These effects persist across model families, trait types, multi-trait settings, and cross-model transfer.

Conclusion: Scaling generated distillation data should be accompanied by trait-aware curation and evaluation, as larger datasets can unintentionally amplify subtle teacher-specific signals even in off-task data, with important implications for safe and effective model training.

Abstract: Scaling model-generated data is usually viewed as improving distillation: more examples should increase coverage, reduce noise, and produce stronger students. We show a second effect: larger datasets can make subtle teacher-specific signals easier to detect in the trained student, even when examples are off-task and never mention the trait. In a controlled setup inspired by subliminal learning, a teacher induced to express a target trait generates restricted off-task data, such as number-only completions. Students trained on different amounts of independent off-task data are evaluated in a separate domain, with matched no-trait controls isolating target-specific transfer. Our main finding is that larger independent datasets make the teacher's induced trait stand out more clearly in the student's later behavior. Other plausible traits may also strengthen with scale, but the target usually grows more. When the small-scale student already favors the target, scaling mainly amplifies that behavior; when it favors a related or salient alternative, more data can shift behavior toward the intended trait. Analyses of learned LoRA updates show a parallel trend. These effects appear across model families, trait types, multi-trait settings, and cross-model transfer. Our results suggest that scaling generated distillation data should be paired with trait-aware curation and evaluation, even when the data appears off-task or benign.

</details>


### [142] [Gromov-Monge Flow Matching for Equivariant Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26961)
*Moritz Piening,Christian Wald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes using graph quotient spaces and Gromov-Monge distances for permutation-equivariant flow matching on graphs, improving sample quality with structure-aware couplings while maintaining compatibility with standard architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphs are symmetric under node permutations, so generative models should respect this symmetry. In flow matching, when comparing graph pairs up to node relabeling, the natural geometry is the graph quotient space, which aligns with the Gromov-Monge distance obtained by optimal node alignment.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework showing quotient couplings can be lifted to aligned representatives without additional cost. In practice, they use Gromov-Wasserstein-type relaxations and lower bounds for inner node alignment, optionally combined with outer assignment between graphs. This approach changes only the training coupling while remaining compatible with standard permutation-equivariant architectures.

Result: The structure-aware couplings substantially improve sample quality at small integration budgets across continuous graph and categorical molecular generation tasks. Scaled-up molecular models remain competitive under conventional many-step sampling.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that leveraging the graph quotient space geometry through Gromov-Monge alignment in flow matching leads to improved generative modeling of graphs and molecules, particularly with limited computational budgets, while maintaining compatibility with existing architectures.

Abstract: Graphs are invariant under node permutations, motivating the use of permutation-equivariant architectures in generative models. In flow matching, however, symmetry may also enter the source--target coupling: once graph pairs are compared up to node relabeling, the natural Wasserstein geometry is that of the graph quotient space. The Euclidean quotient metric of this space coincides with the Gromov--Monge distance, obtained by optimally relabeling the nodes. We develop this perspective theoretically, showing that quotient couplings can be lifted to aligned representatives without additional cost and that symmetrization yields equivariant flow-matching minimizers, including for categorical endpoint prediction. In practice, exact Gromov--Monge alignment is intractable, so we construct minibatch couplings using efficient Gromov--Wasserstein-type relaxations and lower bounds for the inner node alignment, optionally combined with an outer assignment between graphs. The resulting procedure changes only the training coupling and is compatible with standard permutation-equivariant architectures. Across continuous graph and categorical molecular generation, these structure-aware couplings substantially improve sample quality at small integration budgets, while our scaled-up molecular models remain competitive under conventional many-step sampling.

</details>


### [143] [Packora: Systematic Design for Generative Molecular Crystal Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26962)
*Nayoung Kim,Kiyoung Seong,Sungsoo Ahn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Packora is a flow-based generative model for molecular crystal structure prediction that jointly predicts atomic coordinates and lattice parameters from molecular graphs, supporting multi-component and organometallic systems with flexible conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular crystal structure prediction is crucial for pharmaceuticals, agrochemicals, and organic electronics because subtle differences in molecular conformation and packing can significantly impact material properties. Current CSP methods need improvement in handling diverse crystal types and incorporating prior knowledge.

Method: Packora uses a flow-based generative model that predicts both atomic coordinates and lattice parameters from molecular graphs. It features cacheable pairwise reasoning, conditioning dropout, and balanced scaling of pairwise and single representations. The model supports multi-component and organometallic crystals and can condition on molecular conformers, stereochemical labels, and space-group information.

Result: Packora outperforms baselines on both structure generation and ranking benchmarks. It achieves the best matched-budget coverage across all six generation benchmarks, higher experimental-form recovery, lower experimental-form ranks, and faster convergence in ranking tasks.

Conclusion: Packora represents an effective flow-based approach to molecular crystal structure prediction that successfully integrates multiple conditioning modalities and demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on standard CSP benchmarks while providing insights into optimal architecture, training, and inference strategies.

Abstract: Molecular crystal structure prediction (CSP) is important in pharmaceuticals, agrochemicals, and organic electronics, where subtle differences in molecular conformation and packing can strongly affect material properties. We present Packora, a flow-based generative model for molecular CSP that jointly predicts atomic coordinates and the lattice from molecular graphs. Packora supports multi-component and organometallic crystals and can condition on any subset of molecular conformers, stereochemical labels, and space-group information within a single model. Inspired by the CCDC CSP blind test, we evaluate generation and ranking separately, using generation to isolate generator quality and ranking to measure end-to-end performance under a common relaxation and ranking pipeline. We also systematically study architecture, training, conditioning, inference, and scaling, identifying an effective design based on cacheable pairwise reasoning, training objective and numerical solver choices, conditioning dropout, and balanced scaling of pairwise and single representations. Packora outperforms the baselines on both structure generation and ranking benchmarks, achieving the best matched-budget coverage across all six generation benchmarks, as well as higher experimental-form recovery, lower experimental-form ranks, and faster convergence in ranking.

</details>


### [144] [Adversarial Training Without Input Gradients via Low-Rank Householder Expansions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26963)
*Tiana C. Johnson,Donsub Rim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Paper presents a novel adversarial training method using low-rank Householder expansion that eliminates input gradient computation, achieving comparable defense to multi-step PGD training at significantly reduced computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial training is computationally expensive due to inner maximization loops (like PGD steps). The paper aims to develop a more efficient approach that avoids computing input gradients while still defending against small-norm adversarial examples that lie in the approximately linear region around inputs.

Method: Uses low-rank Householder expansion (LRHE) to compute adversarial examples directly from network parameters without input gradient iterations. The method identifies vulnerable directions from existing activation patterns. Adversarial training is performed using only forward evaluations - no differentiation with respect to input is needed, eliminating inner maximization entirely.

Result: The regularizer achieves 8.7× reduction in computational cost (equivalent to 2.8 PGD steps per epoch vs. 40 steps). Models match 3-step PGD training for ε ≤ 0.02 and 40-step training for ε ≤ 0.012 ℓ² budgets on MNIST, though performance degrades for larger perturbations beyond the linear regime.

Conclusion: The main finding is that effective adversarial regularization can be achieved without computing input gradients or performing inner maximization. The LRHE-based approach provides computationally efficient defense against small-norm adversarial examples by exploiting the locally linear behavior of neural networks.

Abstract: This work concerns adversarial training against the small-norm adversarial examples that arise from the inherent input instability of a trained deep neural network. Examples in this class are small as measured in the relative $\ell^2$-norm, and therefore lie in the neighborhood of the input on which the model acts approximately linearly, the regime in which the perturbation remains imperceptible. We first show that such examples can be computed directly from the trained network parameters, without input gradient iterations, by means of a linearization called the low-rank Householder expansion (LRHE). The expansion describes the composed affine map rather than any individual layer, and the directions it identifies are read from the activation pattern already available in the forward pass. We then propose a simple adversarial training scheme built on this construction. No differentiation with respect to the input is performed at any point: training requires only additional forward evaluations, with weight parameters updated by the standard backward pass, and the inner maximization of the usual min-max formulation is eliminated entirely. That such a regularizer exists is our main finding: the methods that dispense with the inner search all obtain their local geometry by differentiating with respect to the input, and we show this is not necessary. The regularizer costs the equivalent of $2.8$ PGD steps per epoch, an $8.7\times$ reduction relative to 40-step adversarial training on MNIST and below the cost of 3-step training. The resulting models match three-step PGD adversarial training for relative $\ell^2$ budgets $\varepsilon \le 0.02$ and 40-step training for $\varepsilon \le 0.012$, falling away beyond, consistent with the locality of the expansion.

</details>


### [145] [Graph-Based Pseudo-multimodal Contrastive Learning for 12-Lead ECG Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26964)
*Mengyu Wang,Kozo Okada,Takafumi Goto,Natsuko Jinba,Hiroki Yamaya,Kiyoshi Hibi,Tomoki Hamagami*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Graph-CMMC: a graph-based pseudo-multimodal contrastive learning framework for 12-lead ECG analysis that captures inter-lead dependencies and global waveform patterns for coronary artery disease diagnosis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing ECG analysis methods focus on single-lead signals or treat leads independently, processing ECGs as 1D time-series. This approach has difficulty capturing inter-lead dependencies and global waveform patterns that are essential for clinical diagnosis of coronary artery disease.

Method: Transforms ECG waveforms into Gramian Angular Difference Field (GADF) images to create complementary representations. Uses a graph-based pseudo-multimodal contrastive learning framework (Graph-CMMC) that aligns waveform and GADF representations in self-supervised manner. Includes a graph-based relational module to model inter-lead dependency and enforce structural consistency across leads.

Result: Achieves competitive performance on multi-label coronary artery occlusion classification task compared to supervised learning methods. Demonstrates effectiveness of using GADF as complementary representation and explicit graph-based modeling of inter-lead dependency.

Conclusion: The Graph-CMMC framework successfully addresses limitations of existing methods by capturing inter-lead dependencies and global waveform patterns through graph-based pseudo-multimodal contrastive learning, leading to robust 12-lead ECG representations for clinical applications.

Abstract: 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) is a standard, non-invasive examination widely used for diagnosing coronary artery disease, where clinical interpretation relies on comparing waveform patterns across multiple leads. However, most existing ECG analysis methods focus on single-lead signals or treat each lead independently, and typically process ECG signals as one-dimensional time-series data using CNNs or RNNs. While effective in modeling local waveform changes, such approaches have difficulty capturing inter-lead dependency and global waveform patterns essential for clinical diagnosis.
  To address this limitation, we propose a graph-based pseudo-multimodal contrastive learning framework called Graph-CMMC. ECG waveforms are transformed into Gramian Angular Difference Field (GADF) images to construct complementary representations of the same cardiac activity, enabling a pseudo-multimodal learning setting. Using all 12 leads, Graph-CMMC aligns waveform and GADF representations in a self-supervised manner, while a graph-based relational module is employed to model inter-lead dependency and enforce structural consistency across leads during contrastive learning.
  Experimental results on a multi-label coronary artery occlusion classification task demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance compared to supervised learning methods. These results further suggest the effectiveness of using GADF as a complementary representation and incorporating explicit graph-based modeling of inter-lead dependency for learning robust 12-lead ECG representations.

</details>


### [146] [ClusterAttention: A training-free speedup of bidirectional attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26965)
*Kasper Nordenram,Amelie Dittmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ClusterAttention is a training-free method that speeds up bidirectional attention by using fast recursive clustering that adapts to key-query geometry, with error compensation and fixed-size clusters optimized for GPU execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing sparse attention methods have limitations: they either rely on input structure (language order or spatial proximity) or use slow clustering processes amortized over multiple forward passes. There's a need for a training-free method that works with unstructured input in a single forward pass while maintaining accuracy.

Method: Uses a fast recursive clustering method that adapts to the geometry of keys and queries in each attention head. All clusters are set to a fixed size (power of two) to enable block-sparse attention with GPU-optimized latency. The method includes error compensation for excluded clusters through centroids, and provides theoretical analysis of output error in sparse attention.

Result: On large-scale tabular data, ClusterAttention speeds up TabPFN-3 by 2-6x while retaining ≥99% dense accuracy. For video generation with Wan 2.1-14B T2V, it achieves better output quality (closer to dense attention) and larger speedup (1.8x vs 1.4x) compared to SVOO, without offline calibration. It's the first successful training-free method for unstructured input with single forward pass.

Conclusion: ClusterAttention provides an effective training-free approach for accelerating bidirectional attention layers, offering significant speed improvements while maintaining high accuracy across different domains (tabular data and video generation), with theoretical foundations for error analysis and compensation.

Abstract: This paper introduces ClusterAttention, a general training-free speedup of bidirectional attention layers. Existing sparse attention methods either rely on structure in the input, such as order in language or spatial proximity in images, or use slow clustering processes amortized over several forward passes. ClusterAttention instead uses a fast recursive clustering method that adapts to the geometry of the keys and queries in each attention head to produce useful clusters. This method allows setting the size of the clusters arbitrarily. We utilize this by setting all clusters to be a fixed size that is a power of two, allowing the block-sparse attention to run at the same latency per query-key interaction as dense attention on GPUs. We also derive an expression for the output error in sparse attention, that explains the counterintuitive experimental finding that tight clusters can lead to larger errors than random clusters. We then derive the error when excluded clusters are compensated through their centroids, and show that this error shrinks with tighter clusters. We integrate this compensation into the method.
  On large-scale tabular data ClusterAttention speeds up TabPFN-3 arXiv:2605.13986 by two to six times, while retaining at least 99% of the dense accuracy. To our knowledge, it is the first training-free method that can be successfully applied in the setting of unstructured input and a single forward pass. For video generation with Wan 2.1-14B T2V arXiv:2503.20314 , ClusterAttention achieves output closer to dense attention and a larger speedup (1.8x versus 1.4x) compared to SVOO arXiv:2603.18636 , a leading method developed specifically for this domain, both run without offline calibration.

</details>


### [147] [TEMPLAR Wales: A georeferenced environmental and toponymic dataset of Welsh settlements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26970)
*Oktay Karakuş,Can Eyupoglu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TEMPLAR Wales is a comprehensive georeferenced dataset linking 3,757 Welsh settlements with deterministic lexical screening of 24 place-name elements and environmental attributes, providing a foundation for toponymy and landscape studies while clearly distinguishing between computational detections and verified etymologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Place names contain valuable historical landscape information, but quantitative analysis requires clear separation between mapped places, lexical annotations, and environmental measurements. There's a need for structured datasets that properly distinguish computational lexical detections from verified etymologies and contemporary environmental data from historical reconstructions.

Method: Created TEMPLAR Wales dataset using: 1) reproducible settlement frame with 3,757 records across Wales, 2) deterministic lexical screening with a frozen registry of 24 Welsh place-name elements yielding 1,350 detections across 1,294 settlements, 3) comprehensive environmental attributes including river/coastal proximity, elevation, terrain context at multiple scales, land cover, and woody cover, with parallel terrain measurements from independent elevation products.

Result: Produced a validated dataset distributed as four relational tables with data dictionary, provenance register, and licensing metadata. Technical validation confirmed relational integrity, deterministic lexical reconstruction, documented environmental coverage, strong agreement between independent terrain sources, and reproducible reconstruction.

Conclusion: TEMPLAR Wales provides a reusable foundation for interdisciplinary research in toponymy, linguistic geography, historical and environmental landscape studies, GIS, and spatial data analysis, with careful epistemological distinctions between computational lexical detections and verified etymologies, and between contemporary environmental measurements and historical landscape reconstructions.

Abstract: Place names provide persistent records of how landscapes have been described and organised, but their quantitative reuse requires explicit separation between mapped places, lexical annotations and environmental measurements. TEMPLAR Wales is a georeferenced environmental-toponymy dataset comprising 3,757 settlement records across Wales. The resource links a reproducible settlement frame to deterministic lexical screening and settlement-level environmental attributes through stable identifiers. It contains 1,350 lexical detections across 1,294 settlements, generated from a frozen registry of 24 Welsh place-name elements, while retaining exact- and prefix-token matches and their provenance separately. Environmental attributes describe river and coastal proximity, elevation and local terrain context at multiple spatial scales, land cover and neighbourhood woody cover, with parallel terrain measurements derived from independent elevation products. The dataset is distributed as four relational tables accompanied by a field-level data dictionary, source-provenance register and licensing metadata. Technical validation confirms relational integrity, deterministic lexical reconstruction, documented environmental coverage, strong agreement between independent terrain sources and reproducible reconstruction of the frozen release. TEMPLAR Wales provides a reusable foundation for research in toponymy, linguistic geography, historical and environmental landscape studies, GIS and spatial data analysis without treating computational lexical detections as verified etymologies or contemporary environmental measurements as historical landscape reconstructions.

</details>


### [148] [Terrain signatures in Welsh settlement names](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26978)
*Oktay Karakuş,Can Eyupoglu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Welsh settlement names containing high-terrain elements (bryn/mynydd) are located 24.4 meters higher than those with low-terrain elements (cwm/pant), showing landscape names retain measurable environmental information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether landscape names retain measurable environmental information beyond just broad geographic structure, specifically examining whether Welsh settlement names still reflect actual terrain characteristics.

Method: Analyzed 3,757 Welsh settlements using a frozen, source-audited 24-element lexical framework, preregistered outcome-specific models, and geographically structured validation. Compared 101 settlements with high-terrain elements (bryn/mynydd) against 139 with low-terrain elements (cwm/pant) using multiple neighborhood definitions (1-, 2-, 5-km). Used spatial blocking validation (10-, 25-, 50-km) and independent elevation sources.

Result: High-terrain names occupied locations 24.4 m higher than their 2-km surroundings (95% CI: 10.8-38.1 m). Association remained positive across different neighborhood definitions and was reproduced with independent elevation data (24.1 m). Adding terrain-name polarity reduced geographically held-out mean squared error by 4.63-7.30%. River-related names showed weaker but directionally consistent evidence; woodland model was non-estimable.

Conclusion: Selected Welsh settlement-name categories retain measurable information about present-day terrain within Wales, but the study does not establish individual etymology, causal naming, historical environmental memory, or transferability to other naming systems. Limitations include residual spatial structure, unresolved name language, and absence of independent external replication.

Abstract: Landscapes are named, but whether names retain measurable environmental information beyond broad geographic structure is rarely tested. We analysed 3,757 Welsh settlements using a frozen, source-audited 24-element lexical framework, preregistered outcome-specific models and geographically structured validation. The central comparison contrasted 101 settlements carrying high-terrain elements (\textit{bryn} or \textit{mynydd}) with 139 carrying low-terrain elements (\textit{cwm} or \textit{pant}). High-terrain names occupied locations 24.4 m higher relative to their 2-km surroundings (95\% CI, 10.8--38.1 m; Holm-adjusted $p$ = 0.00137). The association remained positive across prespecified 1-, 2- and 5-km neighbourhood definitions and was reproduced using an independently produced elevation source (24.1 m; 95\% CI, 10.6--37.6 m). Adding terrain-name polarity to a non-lexical spatial and settlement baseline reduced geographically held-out mean squared error by 4.63\%, 6.22\% and 7.30\% under 10-, 25- and 50-km spatial blocking, respectively, although improvement varied among held-out regions. River-related names provided weaker, directionally consistent evidence, while the preregistered woodland model was non-estimable. Residual spatial structure, unresolved name language and the absence of independent external replication limit interpretation. Selected Welsh settlement-name categories therefore retain measurable information about present-day terrain within Wales, without establishing individual etymology, causal naming, historical environmental memory or transferability to other naming systems.

</details>


### [149] [Decentralized Multitask Learning over Learned Task Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26989)
*Zirui Wan,Stefan Vlaski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized multitask learning with unknown task relationships using two-phase approach: first estimate graph Laplacian from noisy gradients, then use learned graph for cooperative diffusion learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-regularized multitask learning assumes known task relationships, but practical distributed settings often require learning inter-task dependencies directly from data without prior structural knowledge.

Method: Two-phase decentralized strategy: 1) Estimate generalized graph Laplacian from noisy non-cooperative stochastic gradient iterates using decentralized maximum likelihood estimator based on Gaussian Markov random field prior, 2) Exploit learned graph for cooperative multitask diffusion learning.

Result: Theoretical analysis quantifies Laplacian estimation error and its propagation to steady-state performance, introduces topology sensitivity index to capture network heterogeneity effects. Simulations show learned graph enables significant performance improvements over non-cooperative learning and approaches true-graph baseline with small estimation stepsize.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully enables cooperative multitask learning when task relationships are unknown, with performance approaching known-structure baselines when properly configured, providing a practical solution for distributed learning without prior structural knowledge.

Abstract: This paper investigates decentralized multitask learning over networks when the underlying task relationships are unknown. While existing graph-regularized multitask frameworks typically assume a known structure, practical settings often require learning inter-task dependencies directly from distributed data. We propose a decentralized two-phase strategy that first estimates a generalized graph Laplacian from noisy non-cooperative stochastic gradient iterates, and subsequently exploits the learned graph to enable cooperative multitask diffusion learning. This framework is motivated by a Gaussian Markov random field prior, which gives rise to a decentralized maximum likelihood estimator for the graph Laplacian. The analysis quantifies the Laplacian estimation error and its propagation to the steady-state performance of the multitask diffusion recursion, and introduces a topology sensitivity index to capture the effect of network heterogeneity. Simulation results corroborate the theoretical findings and demonstrate that cooperation enabled by the learned task graph significantly improves performance over non-cooperative learning, while approaching the true-graph baseline when the estimation stepsize is sufficiently small.

</details>


### [150] [Benchmarking_Fast_Domain_Adaptation_for_Unsupervised_Speech_Units](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26992)
*Robin San Roman,Manel Khentout,Tu Anh Nguyen,Paul Michel,Yossi Adi,Emmanuel Dupoux*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces ABX-Accent, a benchmark for evaluating speech representation learning on accented English, and shows that adaptive domain normalization improves performance on diverse accents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current speech representation learning methods lack evaluation on out-of-domain and accented speech, particularly important for handling the long tail of English accents that diverge from standard ones.

Method: Created ABX-Accent benchmark using AESRC dataset with 10 English accents, each with <10 hours unlabelled training data. Adapted Zero Resources Challenge ABX metrics for accent evaluation. Developed baseline using adaptive domain normalization to fine-tune a pretrained Contrastive Predictive Coding model.
