Result: The proposed adaptive domain normalization method yields 23.6% relative improvement on average across-speaker ABX scores compared to non-adapted models when applied to the new benchmark.

Conclusion: The work successfully addresses the gap in evaluating speech representation learning on accented speech and demonstrates that domain adaptation methods can significantly improve performance on diverse accents.

Abstract: Representation learning has attracted great atten- tion and managed to reach good performances as a pretraining method for downstream tasks or as a first step towards unsu- pervised speech modeling. Yet, little is known about how such methods deal with out-of-domain speech and how could they be adapted in a few shot to new domains. This is important especially for accented speech where one observes a long tail of accents that diverge from the standard ones. We introduce ABX- Accent, a benchmark based on the AESRC dataset that features 10 different accents of English. It includes a small (< 10 hours) unlabelled training set in each of the accents and adaptations of the Zero Resources Challenge ABX evaluation metrics to each of the accents. We illustrate this benchmark with a baseline model that uses adaptive domain normalization to fine tune a pretrained Contrastive Predictive Coding model on the accents. This method is first developed on LibriSpeech using a male/female split. When applied to the new benchmark, the proposed method yields a relative improvement of 23.6% on across-speaker ABX scores on average compared to non adapted models. The data and metrics will be open sourced upon paper acceptance

</details>


### [151] [Disentangling Optimization Scale from Preference Scale in DPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27032)
*Ivan Kruzhilov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DPO's β parameter has two entangled roles (noise scale and optimization dynamics scaling), causing non-monotonic policy behavior and loss incomparability across β values. The paper proposes a centered-softplus reformulation that disentangles these effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The coefficient β in Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is commonly interpreted as controlling the KL constraint to a reference policy, but the authors discovered it actually entangles two distinct roles: governing the effective inverse preference-noise scale and simultaneously rescaling the optimization dynamics. This entanglement obscures β's role, increases hyperparameter sensitivity, complicates learning-rate scheduling, and creates non-intuitive behaviors.

Method: The authors propose a centered-softplus reformulation of DPO that is argmin-equivalent to original DPO for β > 0, but makes the inverse preference-noise-scale and learning-rate effects explicit and independently tunable. This normalized objective also admits a continuous β→0 endpoint that reduces to a linear preference-margin objective.

Result: The analysis reveals that with fixed learning rate, achieved policy deviation is non-monotone in β: vanishing in a dead zone at small β, peaking at intermediate values, and decreasing again for larger β. Standard DPO loss values are not comparable across β - runs with nearly identical loss curves can differ several-fold in KL divergence from the reference model.

Conclusion: The centered-softplus reformulation successfully disentangles the roles of β, providing clearer interpretability, reduced hyperparameter sensitivity, and more stable learning-rate scheduling. This improves the practical usability and theoretical understanding of DPO objectives.

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a widely used objective for aligning language models from preference data, with the coefficient $β$ commonly interpreted as controlling the KL constraint to a reference policy. We show that $β$ entangles two distinct roles: it governs the effective inverse preference-noise scale and simultaneously rescales the optimization dynamics, coupling this scale with the effective step size. As a consequence, at a fixed learning rate the achieved policy deviation is non-monotone in $β$: it vanishes in a dead zone at small $β$, reaches a peak at an intermediate value, and decreases again for larger $β$. Moreover, standard DPO loss values are not comparable across $β$: runs with nearly identical loss curves can differ several-fold in KL divergence from the reference model. This entanglement obscures the role of $β$, increases sensitivity to hyperparameter choices, and complicates learning-rate scheduling. We propose a centered-softplus reformulation that is argmin-equivalent to DPO for $β>0$, while making the inverse preference-noise-scale and learning-rate effects explicit and independently tunable. The normalized centered-softplus objective also admits a continuous $β\to0$ endpoint that reduces to a linear preference-margin objective.

</details>


### [152] [Performance Foundations of Parallel & Distributed Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27046)
*Maciej Besta,Leonard Schmidt,Lara Nonino,Robert Gerstenberger,Pierre Pang,Patrik Okanovic,Ales Kubicek,Tiancheng Chen,Baraq Lipshitz,Torsten Hoefler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper systematizes RL-for-LLM training, provides compute analysis of RL algorithms, develops parallelism strategies taxonomy, and offers practical guidelines for scalable Reasoning Language Models (RLMs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL-style post-training (RL-for-LLMs) substantially improves LLM reasoning but has massive computational costs - requiring millions of GPU-hours and complex multi-model pipelines that strain modern hardware beyond classical supervised training.

Method: 1) Systematize RL-for-LLM paradigm with compute-centric analysis of RL algorithms (PPO, GRPO variants); 2) Develop taxonomy of intra-/inter-model parallelism strategies covering traditional and novel techniques; 3) Use work-depth model for rigorous analysis; 4) Analyze existing RLM frameworks.

Result: Provides comprehensive framework analysis, parallelism taxonomy using work-depth model, practical guidelines for scalable RLM training, and outlines open research directions.

Conclusion: RLM training is both algorithmic and distributed systems challenge; the paper offers systematic approach to build high-performance, scalable, cost-effective Reasoning Language Models through rigorous parallel computing analysis.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) and other RL-style post-training paradigms have been used for aligning large language models (LLMs) with reasoning standards. The resulting recent Reasoning Language Models (RLMs) such as DeepSeek-R1, o3, and Kimi k1.5 show that such RL-style post-training ("RL-for-LLMs") can substantially improve chain-of-thought reasoning, long-horizon planning, and self-correction. However, the computational footprint of these systems is massive: state-of-the-art RLM training requires millions of GPU-hours and tightly coupled multi-model pipelines that stress modern hardware far beyond classical supervised LLM training. This makes RLM training as much a parallel and distributed systems problem as an algorithmic one. In this work, to facilitate developing RLMs that are simultaneously high-performance, scalable, and cost-effective, we first systematize the RL-for-LLM paradigm and provide a compute-centric analysis of prominent post-training algorithmic frameworks: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), as well as their variants. Second, we develop a taxonomy of intra- and inter-model parallelism strategies for RL-for-LLMs, covering both traditional techniques (data, tensor, pipeline, sequence, context, and expert parallelism) as well as novel forms of parallelism and optimization techniques for multi-model RLM training, for example disaggregated placement, stage fusion, hybrid parallelism, and asynchronous execution. We harness the work-depth model of parallel computing to make our taxonomy and its insights rigorous and portable. Finally, we analyze existing RLM frameworks and we distill practical guidelines and outline open research directions for building scalable, fast, and cost-effective RLMs.

</details>


### [153] [Soft Active Electromyography Interface for Machine Learning-Enabled Silent Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27048)
*Yuta Kurotaki,Shusuke Yamakoshi,Reitaro Yoshida,Yutaka Isoda,Tamami Takano,Yuji Isano,Yusuke Miyake,Kentaro Kuribayashi,Hiroki Ota*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A soft, wearable EMG interface on the fingertip enables silent speech recognition by detecting muscle signals near lips only when needed, achieving 97.2% accuracy for 30 words and demonstrating real-time drone control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current silent speech recognition systems have limitations: they require constant facial attachment, raise privacy concerns, and have unstable signal acquisition. There's a need for a more practical, secure, and user-friendly approach.

Method: Developed a soft, active EMG interface worn on the hand with a fingertip electrode that can be positioned near lips when needed. Uses liquid metal interconnects, transparent flexible printed circuit electrodes, and elastomer encapsulation for mechanical stability. Applied deep neural networks to classify EMG signals.

Result: Achieved mean accuracy of 97.2 ± 1.3% across three subjects for classifying 30-word vocabulary. Demonstrated real-time drone control in noisy environments, validating practical application.

Conclusion: Soft, wearable EMG systems offer a secure, intuitive human-machine interface alternative to conventional voice recognition, especially useful in noisy and privacy-sensitive environments.

Abstract: Silent speech recognition (SSR) provides an alternative communication pathway in the absence of audible speech. However, conventional approaches are limited by the need for constant facial attachment, privacy concerns, and unstable signal acquisition. Here, we propose a soft, active electromyography (EMG) interface that enables word-level SSR using machine learning. Worn on the hand, the device uses a fingertip electrode that can be positioned near the lips to acquire EMG signals only when needed. The interface integrates liquid metal (LM) interconnects, transparent flexible printed circuit (FPC) electrodes, and elastomer encapsulation to ensure high mechanical stability during finger motion. A deep neural network trained on these stable signals achieved a mean accuracy of 97.2 $\pm$ 1.3% across three subjects in classifying a 30-word vocabulary, demonstrating robust linguistic discrimination. Furthermore, real-time drone control validates the practicality of this approach in noisy and privacy-sensitive environments where conventional voice recognition fails. This study highlights the potential of soft, wearable EMG systems as secure and intuitive human-machine interfaces.

</details>


### [154] [Unifying Detection and Adaptation in Task-Free Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27070)
*Dezheng Han,Anbang Zhang,Zhihao Zhu,Shuaishuai Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FiUni is a Fisher-guided unified framework for task-free continual learning in LLMs that detects tasks at batch level and performs parameter-efficient adaptation using Fisher information matrix analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continual learning methods for LLMs rely on explicit task boundaries, limiting their applicability to realistic task-free scenarios where task transitions are unknown during training.

Method: FiUni analyzes Fisher information matrix orthogonality from K-FAC approximations to measure task similarity. It constructs FIM-derived frozen subspaces to guide LoRA, matches Fisher principal subspaces of incoming batches with historical ones, and adaptively decides whether to reuse, expand, or create new subspaces.

Result: FiUni effectively infers latent batch-level task affiliations and achieves competitive performance against advanced task-aware continual learning methods while using fewer trainable parameters.

Conclusion: The proposed Fisher-guided framework enables effective task-free continual learning by dynamically balancing knowledge sharing and task isolation through adaptive subspace management based on Fisher information analysis.

Abstract: To mitigate catastrophic forgetting in downstream continual learning (CL) for large language models (LLMs), existing methods typically constrain parameter updates or introduce task-specific adaptation modules. However, these methods often rely on explicit task boundaries during training, limiting their applicability to realistic task-free scenarios. In this paper, we propose a \textbf{Fi}sher-guided \textbf{uni}fied (\textbf{FiUni}) framework for batch-level task detection and parameter-efficient continual adaptation. FiUni is motivated by a key observation about the Fisher information matrix (FIM) of pre-trained models: the orthogonality among the principal subspaces of its Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature (K-FAC) approximation, estimated from a small number of downstream task samples, can reflect the similarity between different tasks. Based on this observation, FiUni constructs FIM-derived frozen subspaces to guide low-rank adaptation (LoRA), while matching the Fisher principal subspace of each incoming batch window with historical subspaces. This enables FiUni to adaptively determine whether to reuse existing knowledge, expand a related subspace, or create a new subspace, dynamically balancing knowledge sharing and task isolation. Experiments show that FiUni can effectively infer latent batch-level task affiliations and achieve competitive performance against advanced task-aware CL methods with fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [155] [Emotional Preferences as Goal-Priority Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27072)
*Shiqi Liu,Yihua Tan,Hu Fu,Guanyu Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a computational framework where high-level goals autonomously generate state-dependent emotional preferences to regulate priority trade-offs between competing lower-level objectives in multi-objective reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the core question of whether emotional preferences autonomously generated by higher-level goals (rather than being externally prespecified) can determine relative priorities of competing lower-level objectives in changing environments and evolving internal states. It builds on the goal-directed theory of emotion to regulate goal priorities computationally.

Method: The authors propose a two-level framework: (1) a multi-objective reinforcement learning inner controller that provides preference-conditioned goal-directed behaviors, and (2) an outer preference generator that learns a mapping from current state to objective preferences through RL on a high-level goal. Emotional preference is operationalized as state-dependent regulation of relative goal priorities emerging through optimization.

Result: The paper characterizes the policy space induced by preference regulation and derives an upper bound on the optimality gap in terms of the inner behavioral repertoire's representation error. The framework is validated experimentally in self-constructed multi-objective exploration environments, where learned preference functions exhibit contextual priority switching, graded trade-offs, temporal persistence, and outperform fixed/handcrafted preference strategies.

Conclusion: The proposed computational framework successfully demonstrates how emotional preferences can emerge from high-level goals to regulate competing objective priorities in decision-making agents, bridging between goal-directed emotion theories and reinforcement learning. The approach enables adaptive, context-dependent priority regulation without external specification.

Abstract: A core question in decision-making for agents is whether the relative priorities of competing lower-level objectives can be determined by emotional preferences autonomously generated by higher-level goals, rather than being externally prespecified. Under changing external environments and evolving internal states, emotions play an important functional role in regulating the relative priorities of competing goals. Inspired by the goal-directed theory of emotion, this paper studies how such preference regulation can be computationally realized through reinforcement learning. We first propose a conception of emergent emotional preference: a high-level goal autonomously induces state-dependent preferences over competing lower-level objectives. This conception is built upon a framework consisting of a multi-objective reinforcement learning inner controller and an outer preference generator. The inner controller provides a repertoire of preference-conditioned goal-directed behaviors, while the outer preference generator learns a mapping from the current state to objective preferences through reinforcement learning on a high-level goal. We operationalize emotional preference as a state-dependent regulation of relative goal priorities that emerges through optimization. Furthermore, we characterize the policy space induced by preference regulation and derive an upper bound on the optimality gap in terms of the representation error of the inner behavioral repertoire. We show that the gap vanishes when the optimal policy can be represented by the available preference-conditioned policies. Experiments in self-constructed multi-objective exploration environments show that the learned preference function exhibits contextual priority switching, graded trade-offs, and temporal persistence, and outperforms the evaluated fixed-preference and handcrafted-preference strategies.

</details>


### [156] [Tabular Deep Learning for Algorithmic Trading: Cross-Regime Bayesian Optimisation for Equity Signal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27076)
*Joshua Le Grice*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Regime-robust hyperparameter selection improves algorithmic trading model generalization across different market conditions. XGBoost outperforms individual deep learning models, but a hybrid XGBoost+TabNet ensemble achieves best results with 51.26% annualized returns and stock selection-driven alpha.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Algorithmic trading is a $20+ billion market where marginal signal improvements yield significant economic returns. Current equity prediction models lack explicit targeting of regime robustness during hyperparameter selection, which is crucial for models to perform well across different market conditions.

Method: Train five model classes on daily observations from ~300 large-cap US equities over 11 years. Use Bayesian optimization configured to target trading performance across three statistically different market regimes. Compare XGBoost vs. tabular deep learning architectures and create a hybrid ensemble combining XGBoost and TabNet using rank aggregation. Evaluate with regime-robust hyperparameter selection and analyze alternative data's role alongside technical/fundamental features.

Result: Regime-robust hyperparameter selection enables out-of-sample generalization with signal precision above random baseline across all test quarters. Portfolio performance degrades slowly under simulated input noise before collapsing beyond threshold. No individual deep learning model beats XGBoost, but hybrid XGBoost+TabNet ensemble achieves 51.26% annualized return, 2.44 Sharpe ratio, and statistically significant CAPM alpha of 0.423 (p=0.011). Near-zero beta shows outperformance driven by stock selection, not market exposure. Alternative data plays secondary role after technical/fundamental features and varies by model class.

Conclusion: Regime-robust hyperparameter optimization is crucial for trading model generalization. Gradient-boosted trees (XGBoost) outperform individual deep learning models for tabular financial data, but ensemble methods combining different architectures yield best results. The research provides an interactive application for real-time exploration of results, with live data integration as the remaining step for practical deployment.

Abstract: Algorithmic trading now represents a market exceeding $20 billion, where even marginal gains in signal robustness can translate into economically significant returns. Existing evaluations of equity prediction models do not explicitly target regime robustness during hyperparameter selection. Five model classes are trained on daily observations from approximately 300 large-cap US equities over eleven years, with Bayesian optimisation configured to target trading performance across three statistically different market regimes. Regime-robust hyperparameter selection is associated with out-of-sample generalisation, as signal precision remains above the random baseline across all four quarters of the test period, and portfolio performance slowly degrades under simulated input noise before collapsing beyond a defined threshold. No individual tabular deep learning architecture outperforms gradient-boosted trees, but combining XGBoost and TabNet using rank aggregation produces a Hybrid ensemble with an annualised return of 51.26%, a Sharpe ratio of 2.44, and a statistically significant CAPM alpha of 0.423 (p = 0.011). A near-zero beta indicates this outperformance is driven by stock selection, not market exposure. Alternative data plays a secondary role once technical and fundamental features are accounted for, as well as contributing more strongly on the short side than the long, and varies by model class. An interactive application makes these results explorable in real time, with live data integration the remaining step toward practical deployment.

</details>


### [157] [Cone Extended Rayleigh Quotients for Directed Graph Learning: Minimax Spectral Certificates, Sensitivity, and Adaptive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27122)
*Yavdat Sh. Il'yasov,Nur F. Valeev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a spectral analysis framework for directed graph learning that handles nonsymmetric propagation operators, providing spectral certification, sensitivity analysis, and control methods without requiring traditional symmetry or positivity assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directed graph learning produces trainable nonsymmetric operators with distinct right and left spectral structures, but existing methods often rely on symmetry, nonnegativity, or cone preservation assumptions that don't hold for directed graphs. There's a need for spectral analysis tools that work with these more general, trainable operators.

Method: Builds on two-sided cone Rayleigh framework for generalized pencils [B_θ-λG]. Develops learning-oriented spectral certification using computable lower/upper cone bounds that provide a posteriori enclosure of distinguished cone levels. Uses smooth soft-min/max surrogates that preserve rigorous one-sided bounds with explicit approximation errors while remaining differentiable for training. For interior levels, right and left modes satisfy Dλ_C(B)[H]=v_C^T H u_C for sensitivity analysis.

Result: Numerical experiments show applicability beyond cone-preserving operators in directed learning settings. Signed nonsymmetric perturbations reveal transitions from interior eigenpairs to boundary complementary quasi-pairs including non-spectral cone levels. Symmetrization can remove predictive information from edge direction. On directed Cora citation network, adaptive sensitivity recomputation reduces distinguished spectral level by ~21.5% under 0.5% edge-weight reduction budget with no test accuracy change.

Conclusion: The developed framework enables spectral certification, sensitivity analysis, and control for trainable nonsymmetric graph operators without traditional restrictive assumptions. It provides practical tools for spectral optimization in directed graph learning, demonstrating that edge direction carries meaningful predictive information that shouldn't be ignored through symmetrization.

Abstract: Directed graph learning naturally leads to trainable nonsymmetric propagation operators with distinct right and left spectral structures. Building on the two-sided cone Rayleigh framework for generalized pencils \[ B_θ-λG, \] we develop a learning-oriented methodology for spectral certification, sensitivity analysis, and control without requiring symmetry, nonnegativity, or cone preservation. In the positive-orthant setting, computable lower and upper cone bounds provide an a posteriori enclosure of a distinguished cone level, while smooth soft-min/max surrogates preserve rigorous one-sided bounds with explicit approximation errors and remain differentiable with respect to the trainable parameters.
  For a simple interior level, the right and left modes satisfy \[ Dλ_C(B)[H]=v_C^T H u_C, \] yielding first-order optimal graph-supported interventions under prescribed perturbation budgets and motivating adaptive spectral control. Numerical experiments demonstrate the applicability of the approach beyond cone-preserving operators and in directed learning settings. Signed nonsymmetric perturbations reveal a transition from interior eigenpairs to boundary complementary quasi-pairs, including non-spectral cone levels, while controlled experiments show that symmetrization can remove predictive information carried solely by edge direction.
  On the directed Cora citation network, adaptive recomputation of the right--left sensitivity reduces the distinguished spectral level by approximately $21.5\%$ under a cumulative edge-weight reduction budget of $0.5\%$, with no observed change in test accuracy for the trained model and data split considered.

</details>


### [158] [TRACE-CRC: Trajectory-Adaptive Conformal Risk Control for Multi-Step Channel State Information Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27124)
*Kiarash Rezaei,Mehdi Sattari,Javad Aliakbari,Tommy Svensson,Paolo Monti,Carlos Natalino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE-CRC is a trajectory-adaptive calibration method for multi-step CSI prediction that provides reliable uncertainty quantification with calibrated coverage guarantees across future time steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current deep learning-based CSI predictors provide only point predictions without uncertainty estimates, which is problematic for multi-step prediction where downstream decisions (beamforming, scheduling) may fail if any part of the predicted trajectory is unreliable.

Method: TRACE-CRC uses trajectory-adaptive calibration with conformal risk control, constructing Frobenius-norm uncertainty balls around predicted CSI matrices. It combines future-step-dependent error profiling, trajectory difficulty stratification, and learn-then-test risk control to control the risk that at least one future frame is uncovered.

Result: The method achieves reliable trajectory-level coverage with smaller uncertainty balls than conservative multi-step corrections, while avoiding undercoverage of stepwise and adaptive conformal baselines.

Conclusion: TRACE-CRC provides an effective solution for trajectory-aware uncertainty quantification in multi-step CSI prediction, enabling more reliable downstream wireless communication decisions.

Abstract: Reliable prediction of time-varying channel state information (CSI) is essential for efficient wireless communication. Each CSI frame is a matrix-valued representation of the wireless channel response, and a sequence of CSI frames forms a temporal channel trajectory. Modern deep learning-based CSI predictors, however, often provide only point predictions and lack calibrated uncertainty estimates. This limitation is particularly problematic in multi-step CSI prediction, where the target is a sequence of future CSI matrices, and downstream decisions such as beamforming or scheduling may fail if any part of the predicted trajectory is unreliable. We propose trajectory-adaptive calibration and error profiling with conformal risk control (TRACE-CRC), a method for trajectory-aware uncertainty quantification in multi-step CSI prediction. TRACE-CRC constructs Frobenius-norm uncertainty balls around predicted CSI matrices and controls the risk that at least one future frame is uncovered. Instead of calibrating each future step independently, TRACE-CRC combines future-step-dependent error profiling, trajectory difficulty stratification, and learn-then-test (LTT) risk control. Empirically, TRACE-CRC achieves reliable trajectory-level coverage with substantially smaller uncertainty balls than conservative multi-step corrections, while avoiding the trajectory undercoverage of compact stepwise and adaptive conformal baselines.

</details>


### [159] [Ultra Low-Power, Lightweight, Probabilistic RSS-Based Path Reconstruction: A System for Landscape-Scale Bee Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27152)
*Christopher J. Noroozi,Joseph L. Woodgate,Michael Mangan,Michael T. Smith*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel RSS-based localization method for ultra-lightweight, low-power devices using rotating high-gain transmitters and probabilistic modeling achieves 10-15m accuracy while consuming only 180-600uW.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small, power-constrained devices in movement ecology, IoT, and robotics need localization without GNSS. Existing RSS-based AoA methods have limited range and high power demands from requiring many measurements.

Method: Uses minimal RSS measurements from simple rotating high-gain transmitters (300m range) with probabilistic modeling to infer AoA. Models receiver movement with Gaussian processes and reconstructs paths using doubly stochastic variational inference.

Result: Achieves ~15m accuracy tracking of 38mg receivers (including power source) consuming <180uW, improving to ~10m accuracy at <600uW with more measurements. Successfully demonstrated by tracking Bombus terrestris (bumblebee) nest return flights.

Conclusion: The method enables ultra-low-power localization of miniature devices across scalable landscapes, supporting applications like insect behavioral studies and other fields requiring tracking of small, power-constrained moving objects.

Abstract: Applications in fields such as movement ecology, Internet of Things or robotics share the need for systems that localize devices that are too small and power constrained to implement GNSS (Global Navigation Satellite Systems). Alternative low-power localization methods often rely on only measurements of RSS (Received Signal Strength) to infer the AoA (Angle of Arrival) of a transmitted radio frequency signal, but are limited by range and the power demand of the large number of RSS measurements required to infer an accurate AoA. In this paper we address these issues with a novel RSS-based method for tracking ultra lightweight and low-power moving receivers across a complex landscape, achieved by using a minimal number of RSS measurements from simple rotating high-gain transmitters with a range of 300m, and applying probabilistic modelling to infer their AoA. The receiver's movement path is then modelled using a Gaussian process and reconstructed using doubly stochastic variational inference, resulting in approximately 15m accuracy tracking of receivers weighing 38mg (including power source) over a scalable landscape range while consuming less than 180uW, increased to approximately 10m accuracy at less than 600uW by taking more RSS measurements. We anticipate that this method will support fields such as the behavioural study of flying insect species, which we demonstrate by applying the system to track Bombus terrestris nest return flights.

</details>


### [160] [Inductive Correlation Clustering with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27153)
*Francesco Paolo Nerini,Francesco Bonchi,Arijit Khan,André Panisson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNN-based inductive correlation clustering framework that generalizes to unseen graphs, reducing inference time by 5 orders of magnitude while maintaining ~10% approximation ratio compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing correlation clustering algorithms have significant scalability issues and are inherently transductive, requiring re-execution from scratch for each new problem instance.

Method: Proposes using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to solve Inductive Correlation Clustering, learning structural patterns and node features during training to generalize to new graphs from the same distribution.

Result: Framework lowers inference time up to 5 orders of magnitude while maintaining approximation ratio within ~10% of best baselines; achieves competitive results on transductive benchmarks; serves as effective learnable pooling mechanism for graph classification.

Conclusion: The GNN-based approach successfully bridges the gap for inductive correlation clustering, offering efficient generalization to unseen graphs while maintaining solution quality and serving as an effective pooling layer for hierarchical graph representation learning.

Abstract: Correlation Clustering (CC) is a natural formulation of clustering in combinatorial optimization, which uses a graph representation of the input and does not require a pre-specified number of clusters. Given $n$ objects and a pairwise similarity function, the goal is to cluster the objects so that similar objects are put in the same cluster and dissimilar objects are put in different clusters. Despite its versatility, existing CC algorithms suffer from significant scalability issues and are inherently transductive: i.e., the algorithm must be executed from scratch for any new problem instance.
  In this work, we bridge this gap by leveraging Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to solve Inductive Correlation Clustering, a novel generalization of the CC problem designed to handle unseen graph instances. By learning to exploit common structural patterns and node features during training, our framework generalizes to new graphs drawn from the same distribution with minimal computational overhead with respect to standard algorithms. We demonstrate the effectiveness and scalability of our approach through extensive experiments. Our framework not only excels in the inductive setting, e.g., lowering the inference time up to $5$ order of magnitude, while maintaining an approximation ratio within $~10\%$ of the best baseline solution, but also achieves competitive results on standard (transductive) CC benchmarks. Finally, we showcase a practical application of our framework as a learnable pooling mechanism for graph classification. Our results indicate that our method serves as an efficient pooling layer, enhancing the ability of GNNs to capture hierarchical structural information in networks.

</details>


### [161] [Diffusion Policies for Short-Horizon Planning in Robot Crowd Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27158)
*Wendong Li,Jochen Garcke*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PDPO: Offline-to-online RL framework using diffusion policies to generate short-horizon action chunks for safer and more diverse robot crowd navigation, addressing boundary violation issues in benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for robot crowd navigation output single reactive actions, limiting representation of diverse short-term avoidance strategies. Current benchmarks have an artifact where agents can bypass dense crowds by leaving valid domains without explicit boundary constraints.

Method: Proposes Planning Diffusion Policy Optimization (PDPO): 1) Pretrain diffusion policy on collision-avoidance demonstrations offline, 2) Fine-tune online with PPO treating denoising as internal decision process, 3) Generate five-step action chunks executed in receding-horizon manner, 4) Modify benchmarks to treat boundary violations as collisions.

Result: PDPO achieves improved success rates over strong baselines. Ablation studies show action chunks are particularly important for the modified bounded benchmark where boundary violations count as collisions.

Conclusion: PDPO effectively enhances robot crowd navigation by generating diverse short-horizon action strategies through diffusion policies, and addressing benchmark artifacts via boundary-aware evaluation leads to more robust performance assessment.

Abstract: Robot crowd navigation requires safe and efficient decision-making under dense, dynamic, and multimodal human--robot interactions. Existing reinforcement-learning methods typically output a single reactive action at each timestep, which limits their ability to represent diverse short-term avoidance strategies. We propose Planning Diffusion Policy Optimization (PDPO), an offline-to-online reinforcement-learning framework that uses a diffusion policy to generate short-horizon action chunks for crowd navigation. PDPO is first pretrained on collision-avoidance demonstrations and then fine-tuned online with PPO by treating the denoising process as an internal decision process. During execution, the policy generates a five-step action chunk and applies it in a receding-horizon manner. Furthermore, we observe an evaluation artifact in common crowd-navigation benchmarks: without explicit boundary constraints, learned agents may leave the valid domain and bypass dense crowds. To address this, we introduce a setting in which boundary violations are treated as collisions. Experiments show that PDPO obtains an improved success rate over strong baselines, and ablations demonstrate that action chunks are especially important for the modified bounded benchmark.

</details>


### [162] [TraceBench: Controlled Evaluation of LLM Agents for Time-Series Root-Cause Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27182)
*Tommaso Bendinelli,Artur Dox,Christian Holz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers developed TraceBench, a simulation framework for evaluating LLM agents on time-series anomaly detection tasks, finding that agents perform better with domain context and direct prediction submission rather than script generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is increasing application of LLM agents to anomaly detection and root-cause analysis of time-series data from real-world systems, but their performance hasn't been systematically evaluated under controlled conditions. The researchers aim to address this gap by creating a framework for rigorous evaluation.

Method: The researchers developed TraceBench, a simulation-based framework that generates controlled root-cause attribution tasks. Each task involves providing agents with time-series observations from simulated physical dynamical systems, where the agents must determine whether system parameters were altered and identify which ones. They tested four LLM agents across three interpretable mechanical systems under controlled experimental conditions.

Result: The evaluation revealed that: 1) Agents benefit substantially from domain context, 2) They explore data primarily through numerical console output rather than visualizations, and 3) Agents generally perform worse when required to produce Python scripts that map time-series samples to predicted root-cause labels compared to when submitting predictions directly.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LLM agents for time-series anomaly detection and root-cause analysis perform better when provided with domain context and when they can submit predictions directly rather than producing Python scripts. The researchers created TraceBench as a simulation framework for controlled evaluation and released their datasets, results, and leaderboard publicly.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly applied to anomaly detection and root-cause analysis in time-series observations collected from real-world systems; however, their performance on these tasks has not been systematically evaluated under controlled conditions. We introduce TraceBench, a simulation-based framework for generating controlled root-cause attribution tasks. In each generated task, an agent receives time-series observations produced by simulating a physical dynamical system and must determine whether a system parameter was altered during the simulation and, if so, which one. Using TraceBench, we generate tasks from three interpretable mechanical systems and systematically evaluate four LLM agents across controlled experimental conditions, yielding new insights into how these agents analyze time-series observations from dynamical systems. Our results show that agents benefit substantially from domain context and explore data primarily through numerical console output rather than visualizations. We also find that agents generally perform worse when required to produce a Python script that maps each time-series sample to a predicted root-cause label than when they submit predictions directly. We release our datasets, agent trajectories, experimental results, and a leaderboard on our website, tracebench.github.io.

</details>


### [163] [When Interference Graphs Evolve: Doubly Robust Estimation of Dynamic Peer Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27187)
*Xiaojing Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new framework for estimating peer effects in dynamic networks that separates pre-assignment network history, dynamic peer exposure, and post-assignment network changes into distinct causal components, with a doubly robust estimator that works with evolving interaction graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional peer effect estimation methods struggle when interaction networks evolve over time, because network changes before, during, and after treatment assignment have different causal interpretations and need to be distinguished for proper causal inference.

Method: Introduced a controlled contrast framework that indexes potential outcomes by own treatment, temporally aggregated peer exposure, and post-assignment evolution summary. Developed Dynamic Network Doubly Robust estimator (DynaNet-DR) combining temporally factorized propensity with normalized augmentation, with implementation features like representative-score prediction and finite-sample stabilization.

Result: The estimator is consistent under specified assumptions when either the outcome regression or propensity estimator is consistent. Semi-synthetic benchmarks on real temporal graph sequences show favorable estimation accuracy compared to methods targeting the full profile.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully addresses the challenge of estimating peer effects in dynamic networks by properly distinguishing different network evolution components, with practical implementation showing promise in both synthetic benchmarks and observational studies like MathOverflow.

Abstract: Peer effects are difficult to estimate when interaction graphs evolve because pre-assignment network history, dynamic peer exposure, and post-assignment network change have distinct causal roles. We introduce a controlled contrast framework that indexes potential outcomes by own treatment, temporally aggregated peer exposure, and a post-assignment evolution summary. Differences between the resulting means define own-treatment, peer-exposure, controlled network-evolution, and joint controlled contrasts rather than a mediation decomposition. We develop the Dynamic Network Doubly Robust estimator, DynaNet-DR, which combines a temporally factorized propensity with normalized augmentation. Under consistency, summary sufficiency, sequential exchangeability, positivity, nuisance convergence, and weak dependence, its canonical estimator is consistent when either the outcome regression or the propensity estimator is consistent. The reported implementation adds representative-score prediction, fixed clipping, and finite-sample stabilization. Semi-synthetic benchmarks on fixed real temporal graph sequences show favorable estimation accuracy among methods targeting the full profile. These benchmarks assess summary-indexed contrasts rather than counterfactual edge generation, and the MathOverflow study is an observational illustration under the stated assumptions.

</details>


### [164] [Common Geodesics Do Not Guarantee Fisher Consistency of the Structured SVM: Minimal Counterexamples and a Tree-Metric Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27203)
*Jintao Fei,Jiangying Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that the known necessary condition (task loss being a metric with common geodesic points) for Fisher consistency of structured SVM is not sufficient for argmax decoding. Counterexamples exist even with minimal 4 outputs, and argmax consistency only holds for tree metrics that are paths, not branching trees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the gap in understanding Fisher consistency of structured SVMs with argmax decoding. While a known necessary condition exists (task loss must be a metric where every output triple has a common geodesic point), its sufficiency hasn't been established. The authors aim to determine if this condition guarantees that argmax decoding from optimal scores always yields Bayes-optimal predictions.

Method: The authors use geometric and combinatorial approaches to analyze argmax consistency. They construct counterexamples with minimal structures: a 4-output unit star shows insufficiency of the common-geodesic condition. They classify tree metrics, showing argmax consistency holds only for path graphs. They also examine boundary distributions vs full-support distributions, and provide primal-dual certificates for all optimality claims.

Result: Key results: 1) The common-geodesic condition is not sufficient for argmax consistency. 2) Four outputs are minimal for counterexamples among metrics satisfying the condition. 3) For tree metrics, argmax consistency holds if and only if the tree is a path. 4) Five outputs are necessary and sufficient for full-support counterexamples among such metrics. 5) The smallest counterexample family is K_{2,3} bipartite graphs. 6) A full-support counterexample exists for the 3D Hamming cube.

Conclusion: The paper reveals a fundamental decoder gap in structured SVMs: even when an embedding guarantees the existence of a calibrated link function, it doesn't ensure that argmax decoding from every surrogate-risk minimizer yields Bayes-optimal predictions. This shows limitations of the common-geodesic condition and provides precise characterizations of when argmax consistency fails.

Abstract: A known necessary condition for Fisher consistency of the structured support vector machine requires the task loss to be a metric for which every output triple has a common geodesic point. We show that this condition is not sufficient for the canonical coordinate-wise argmax decoder. A four-output unit star admits an exactly optimal score vector whose maximizers are all strictly non-Bayes, and four outputs are minimal among metrics satisfying the condition. We then completely classify positively weighted tree metrics whose vertex set is the output space: argmax consistency holds if and only if the tree is a path. The failure on branching trees is confined to boundary distributions; every tree retains the argmax property at every full-support distribution. Among metrics satisfying the common-geodesic condition, five outputs are necessary and sufficient for a full-support counterexample; $K_{2,3}$ is the smallest member of an infinite $K_{m,n}$ family. We additionally give a full-support counterexample for the three-dimensional Hamming cube. All optimality claims have exact primal-dual certificates. The counterexamples expose a concrete decoder gap: in this polyhedral setting, an embedding can guarantee the existence of a calibrated link without validating a prescribed argmax link on every surrogate-risk minimizer.

</details>


### [165] [Profit based evaluation of machine learning for nitrogen recommendations in winter wheat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27205)
*Xulong Wang,Po Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Machine learning fails as a nitrogen rate predictor for wheat but works as a profit-scored correction to standard advice, improving profit margins without needing complex models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nitrogen fertilizer rates are set before the growing season under uncertainty about prices and weather. Standard UK agricultural advice doesn't respond to price changes, yet recent price volatility has shifted the most profitable nitrogen application rates significantly (by tens of kg/ha). While machine learning is often proposed as a solution, it's typically evaluated on prediction accuracy rather than actual profitability outcomes.

Method: The researchers developed a novel evaluation approach by scoring nitrogen advice directly on the profit it forgoes using measured yield response curves. They created a test bench using 892 yield response curves from two long-running UK experiments. They swept nitrogen-to-grain price ratios to cover all price scenarios. Machine learning models were tested alongside standard advice, and a simple correction step was applied after modeling to improve profitability.

Result: Machine learning failed as a predictor - no model could recover the best nitrogen rate within farm tolerance levels, and benchmark noise showed none could. At normal prices, all models performed worse than standard advice on profit. However, a simple correction step applied after modeling cut profit losses by 25%. Better models and extra features provided no additional gain. The same frozen correction reduced losses by 43% at a second site without retraining. A hybrid approach combining standard advice with a damped correction removed bias and reduced rare large losses.

Conclusion: Machine learning is most valuable as a profit-scored correction to standard nitrogen advice rather than as a replacement for it. The approach also allows pricing of emission cuts at costs comparable to current carbon prices. Simple correction mechanisms can significantly improve profitability outcomes without requiring complex modeling approaches.

Abstract: Nitrogen rates for winter wheat are set before the season, under unknown prices and weather. The standard UK advice does not respond to prices, yet recent price swings moved the most profitable rate by tens of kilograms per hectare. Machine learning is often proposed as the fix. However, it is usually judged on prediction accuracy, and accurate prediction does not by itself make the recommended rate more profitable. Our insight is to score nitrogen advice directly by the profit it forgoes on measured yield response curves. We build a test bench on 892 such curves from two long running UK experiments, and sweep the nitrogen to grain price ratio to cover all price scenarios. On this bench, machine learning fails as a predictor. No model recovers the best rate within farm tolerance, and the benchmark noise shows none can. At normal prices, every model also loses to the standard advice on profit. The gain sits elsewhere. A simple correction step applied after the model cuts profit losses by a quarter, while better models and extra features give no gain. The same frozen correction cuts losses by 43% at the second site without any retraining. A hybrid of standard advice plus a damped correction removes bias and trims rare large losses. The same price sweep also prices emission cuts, at a cost comparable to current carbon prices. Machine learning therefore pays as a profit scored correction to standard advice, not as its replacement.

</details>


### [166] [HALO: A Heterogeneity-Aware Language-Aligned IMU Foundation Model for Open-Set Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27233)
*Zihan Ding,Liyu Zhang,Xiaomin Ouyang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HALO is a heterogeneity-aware, language-aligned foundation model for IMU-based human activity recognition that addresses sensing heterogeneity and poor generalization through two-stage training, achieving state-of-the-art performance with far fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks a unified HAR model that can generalize across diverse subjects, devices, and activities due to two key challenges: 1) sensing heterogeneity (differences in sampling rates, channel configurations, sensor placements), and 2) poor generalization to unseen activities and label vocabularies.

Method: Two-stage training framework: Stage 1 pretrains IMU encoder with heterogeneity-aware self-supervised learning including adaptive-pooling tokenization, channel-independent feature extraction, and contextualized sensor conditioning that injects natural-language sensor descriptions. Stage 2 aligns IMU encoder with text embeddings via synonym-aware soft contrastive learning, enabling open-set recognition via cosine-similarity retrieval.

Result: Trained on 10 public HAR datasets, evaluated on 7 held-out datasets, HALO outperforms five state-of-the-art baselines on all 8 aggregate metrics. Despite using only ~35M parameters (10x fewer than MOMENT's 341.2M), HALO improves zero-shot open-set accuracy by 13.7 percentage points over 87 training labels.

Conclusion: HALO successfully addresses sensing heterogeneity and generalization challenges in HAR through its novel two-stage training approach, demonstrating superior performance with significantly fewer parameters, though it still struggles with severe distribution shifts.

Abstract: Human Activity Recognition (HAR) using inertial measurement units (IMUs) enables a wide range of applications, yet the field still lacks a unified model that can generalize across diverse subjects, devices, and activities. Training such a model is difficult due to two key challenges: sensing heterogeneity -- differences in sampling rates, channel configurations, and sensor placements -- and poor generalization to unseen activities and label vocabularies. We introduce HALO (Heterogeneity-Aware Language-aligned Open-set model), a domain-specific IMU foundation model that addresses both challenges through a two-stage training framework. Stage 1 pretrains the IMU encoder with heterogeneity-aware self-supervised learning, including adaptive-pooling tokenization, channel-independent feature extraction, and contextualized sensor conditioning that injects natural-language sensor descriptions into each channel embedding. Stage 2 aligns this IMU encoder with text embeddings via synonym-aware soft contrastive learning, enabling open-set recognition via cosine-similarity retrieval without per-dataset classifiers. Trained on 10 public HAR datasets and evaluated on 7 held-out datasets, HALO outperforms five state-of-the-art baselines on all 8 aggregate metrics, and still leads on 3 of 4 settings under baseline-matched inputs. Despite using only ~35M trainable parameters -- 10x fewer than the latest foundation model MOMENT (341.2M) -- HALO improves zero-shot open-set accuracy, measured over all 87 training labels, by 13.7 percentage points. On two further datasets with severe distribution shift, every model including HALO collapses zero-shot. A video demonstration of HALO's performance in real world is available at https://youtu.be/rooVKragtFU

</details>


### [167] [Importance Scoring of Transformer Attention Heads in Learning Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27241)
*Ahmad Jad Allah,Kazi F. Akhter,Md. Kamrozzaman Bhuiyan,Manar D. Samad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel importance-scoring metric for interpreting attention heads in transformers applied to tabular data, showing robustness when removing low-importance heads and performance drops when removing high-importance heads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer models are widely studied in computer vision and NLP, but their application to tabular data remains underexplored. There's a need to understand how deep learning models transform tabular data and to develop interpretability methods specific to this domain.

Method: Developed an importance-scoring metric to interpret multi-head transformer models on tabular data. Tested on 40 diverse tabular datasets by systematically removing attention heads based on importance scores and measuring performance impact. Analyzed head importance distribution across six attention layers.

Result: In 72.5% of experiments, models were most resilient when removing heads with lowest importance scores first. Removing most important heads first caused greatest performance degradation. Important heads were scattered across layers without consistent layer-specific trends. Head importance varied considerably across different tabular datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed importance score effectively identifies redundant vs. critical attention heads in transformers for tabular data, enabling architecture optimization and efficiency improvements. Unlike image/language domains, tabular data shows dataset-specific attention patterns, suggesting the need for specialized interpretability approaches.

Abstract: Computationally demanding and opaque deep learning models can be better understood and optimized by analyzing how they transform data. While deep transformers have been widely studied in computer vision and natural language processing, their application in tabular data remains relatively underexplored. This paper presents one of the first applications of an importance-scoring metric to interpret multi-head transformer models in learning from tabular data. Experiments conducted on 40 diverse tabular datasets demonstrate robustness to head drops based on the proposed head importance score. In 72.5\% of experimental examples, the model remains most resilient to performance drops when heads with the lowest importance scores are gradually removed. In contrast, removing the most important attention head first results in the greatest reduction in classification performance. A closer look at individual head importance scores across six attention layers reveals that important heads are scattered across layers, with no consistent layer-specific trends. In contrast to the image and language domains, the importance of individual attention heads varies considerably across tabular datasets with different schemas and feature spaces. The proposed importance score can improve efficiency and redundancy within transformer architectures. We make the source code for measuring the importance of individual attention heads publicly available.

</details>


### [168] [Circuit Condensation: Post-Training that Concentrates a Behavior's Causal Circuit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27254)
*Sai Adith Senthil Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Circuit Condensation is a method that post-trains models to compress behavior circuits into smaller causal graphs by pruning low-attribution edges and training low-rank adapters, achieving 8.1x average size reduction while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frozen discovery methods in mechanistic interpretability often produce hundreds of circuit edges, making them difficult to inspect, compare, or verify exhaustively. There's a need for more compact and interpretable circuits.

Method: Circuit Condensation iteratively prunes low-attribution edges and trains low-rank adapters to match original model behavior through the remaining connections. Each pruning round retains cuts only if task performance and general capabilities survive.

Result: Across four behaviors and eight models, condensed circuits were smaller than strongest frozen baselines in 30 of 32 settings (8.1x average reduction, up to 316x). Weight updates (not just search) drive reduction. On indirect object identification, condensation isolated 24 heads (17 with documented roles) vs 61 heads in frozen circuits.

Conclusion: Circuit Condensation successfully produces compact, interpretable circuits that track original model behavior and predict its errors, revealing fundamental dependencies between edges that cannot be understood independently.

Abstract: One approach to mechanistic interpretability explains behavior through circuits: the components and connections that carry it. Frozen discovery often returns hundreds of edges, making them hard to inspect, compare, or verify exhaustively. We introduce Circuit Condensation, which post-trains models to concentrate behaviors into smaller causal graphs. Each round prunes low-attribution edges and trains a low-rank adapter to match the original through what remains, retaining the cut only if task performance and general capability survive. Across four behaviors and eight models, condensed circuits are smaller than the strongest frozen baseline in 30 of 32 settings, by $8.1\times$ on average and up to $316\times$. Repeating the search without weight updates produces larger circuits in 29 of 32 settings, showing that weight updates, rather than search alone, drive the reduction. Testing every subset of 19 circuits finds 11 that cannot be reduced and reveals removable edges in the rest. Pair ablations expose dependencies between edges, showing that their effects cannot be understood independently. On indirect object identification, condensation isolates 24 heads, 17 of them with documented roles, against 61 heads and 36 undocumented ones for the matched frozen circuit: a sufficient sub-circuit of the published mechanism rather than a reconstruction of it. The resulting circuit tracks the original model's next-token distribution and predicts its errors.

</details>


### [169] [Making Latent Evolution Explicit: Operator-Structured Transitions for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27259)
*Xiaoxiao Lu,Yunlong Dong,Jiahao Shi,Ye Yuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEON introduces operator-based latent evolution for robot world models, improving performance and robustness over Transformer-based approaches by modeling transitions through learned operators and additive forcing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current latent World Action Models (WAMs) use Transformer-based predictors that focus on token interaction rather than temporal evolution. The paper argues that transition realization architecture should be considered separately from predictive representation and policy coupling.

Method: Introduces LEON (Latent Evolution Operator Network) which models latent evolution in learned observable space using context-modulated operator-based propagation and additive forcing. Grounded in controlled Koopman generator theory, it organizes transition variation around shared evolution-operator structure with complementary additive change path.

Result: Controlled dynamical systems verify LEON's evolution-specific inductive bias and complementary roles of operator propagation/forcing. Across two WAM formulations, LEON improves closed-loop performance and robustness while remaining effective under full transition replacement.

Conclusion: Transition realization is a consequential architectural choice in latent WAMs. LEON's operator-based approach provides better inductive bias for temporal evolution compared to Transformer-based methods, establishing transition architecture as distinct dimension for WAM design.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) augment robot policies by predicting how task-relevant scene states may evolve under interaction. Recent WAMs increasingly perform such prediction in latent representation spaces, avoiding full appearance-level generation while preserving control-relevant information. Yet latent transitions are commonly realized with Transformer-based predictors whose inductive structure is centered on token interaction rather than temporal evolution. We study transition realization as an architectural choice distinct from predictive representation and prediction-policy coupling. We introduce the Latent Evolution Operator Network (LEON), which models latent evolution in a learned observable space through context-modulated operator-based propagation and additive forcing. Grounded in the controlled Koopman generator view of evolution, LEON organizes context-dependent transition variation around a shared evolution-operator structure while retaining a complementary path for additive change. Controlled dynamical systems verify the resulting evolution-specific inductive bias and the complementary roles of operator propagation and forcing. Across two WAM formulations that integrate latent prediction into the policy differently, LEON improves closed-loop performance and robustness while remaining effective under full transition replacement. These results establish transition realization as a consequential architectural choice in latent WAMs.

</details>


### [170] [MM-Spectrum: Multimodal Multi-spectral Molecular Structural Elucidation with a Stable MoE Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27286)
*Hai-tao Yu,Nan Min,Zheng Fang,Hongyu Zhan,Yusen Tan,Yuhan Wang,Jun Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MM-Spectrum is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework for multimodal spectral analysis that addresses performance degradation from spectral heterogeneity through modality-aware routing and specialized experts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct concatenation of multimodal spectroscopic measurements suffers from performance degradation due to pronounced heterogeneity and multimodal imbalance across different spectral modalities.

Method: Proposes MM-Spectrum with: 1) modality-aware routing mechanism that considers spectral identity beyond token content, 2) shared and interaction experts to extract both modality-unique and cross-modal information, 3) heterogeneous expert capacities to handle multispectral imbalance while suppressing noise.

Result: Achieves consistent and substantial improvements across full-modality, bimodal, and missing-modality settings for molecular structural elucidation, supported by ablation studies and interpretability analyses.

Conclusion: MM-Spectrum effectively addresses multimodal imbalance in spectroscopic analysis through specialized routing and expert design, enabling robust molecular structure inference from heterogeneous spectral data.

Abstract: Inferring molecular structures from multimodal spectroscopic measurements requires integrating complementary yet highly heterogeneous signals. However, the common paradigm of directly concatenating multispectral sequences can exhibit anomalous performance degradation, primarily due to pronounced heterogeneity and the resulting multimodal imbalance across modalities. As a remedy, we propose MM-Spectrum, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework tailored for multimodal multispectral spectra-to-structure elucidation. To better match the information characteristics under multispectral imbalance, MM-Spectrum introduces an explicit modality-aware routing mechanism that exposes spectral identity to the router in addition to token content representations. Moreover, it incorporates shared and interaction experts, together with heterogeneous expert capacities, to extract multispectral modality-unique and cross-modal synergistic information while suppressing noise-induced interference. Across full-modality, bimodal, and missing-modality settings on molecular structural elucidation, MM-Spectrum achieves consistent and substantial improvements, supported by ablation studies and interpretability analyses.

</details>


### [171] [QuantumBoostNet: A Hybrid Classical-Quantum Architecture for Enhanced Accuracy in Cardiac Ultrasound View Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27302)
*Mihai Udrescu-Milosav,Stefan-Alexandru Jura,Mihai Udrescu,Gerhard-Paul Diller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QuantumBoostNet introduces a hybrid classical-quantum architecture for cardiac ultrasound view identification, outperforming state-of-the-art models through adaptive classical-quantum fusion and noise robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate cardiac ultrasound view identification is critical for anatomical interpretation and reducing clinical errors. Current computer vision models perform poorly on specialized medical imaging due to high noise levels in the data.

Method: Hybrid classical-quantum architecture with classical backbone and two heads (classical and quantum). The quantum head uses a parametrized 10-qubit circuit. Training involves two stages with adaptive transition between heads controlled by a mixing parameter based on loss dynamics.

Result: QuantumBoostNet consistently outperforms state-of-the-art classical and hybrid classical-quantum models in cardiac ultrasound view identification, achieving relative improvement over best competitors. It also shows superior performance on established image classification benchmarks and exhibits robustness to noise.

Conclusion: The findings support continued development of hybrid classical-quantum models for specialized medical imaging applications, demonstrating their potential to address challenges in noisy medical data where traditional methods underperform.

Abstract: Accurate identification of the correct view or angle in cardiac ultrasound (echocardiogram) is a critical component of cardiologic imaging. This step is essential for precise anatomical interpretation, reliable measurement, and the reduction of clinical errors. Although computer vision has advanced significantly, most state-of-the-art models perform well on standard benchmarks but often yield suboptimal results in specialized medical imaging tasks due to the high level of noise present in the data. QuantumBoostNet, a hybrid classical-quantum architecture, is introduced to address these challenges. This model integrates a classical backbone with two heads: one classical and one quantum, with the quantum head implemented as a parametrized 10-qubit quantum circuit. Training occurs in two stages, with an adaptive transition between heads governed by a mixing parameter that monitors loss dynamics. Extensive experiments indicate that, despite the limited number of qubits that can be simulated, QuantumBoostNet consistently outperforms state-of-the-art classical and hybrid classical-quantum models in cardiac ultrasound view identification, achieving a relative improvement over the best competitor. QuantumBoostNet also demonstrates superior performance on established image classification benchmarks and exhibits robustness to noise. These findings support the continued development of hybrid classical-quantum models for specialized medical imaging applications.

</details>


### [172] [Beyond Parallel Blindness: Information Floors and Model Gaps in Block Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27339)
*Xinwei Qiang,Xiang Fang,Chang Chen,Yue Guan,Yufei Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Block drafters have fundamental limitations due to missing future token information - even perfect proposals can only achieve 71% acceptance at final slots. Short-range conditioning removes most of this limitation, but current drafters still fall far short of their theoretical ceilings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why block drafters (which propose multiple tokens before earlier ones are realized) experience rejection, and to separate two confounding factors: missing future information within blocks versus imperfect modeling of observable information.

Method: Introduced an "information floor" concept representing minimum expected rejection at specified conditioning order. Used target rollouts across four domains with four open-weight models and a frontier API target to estimate both the information floor and model gap (rejection above the floor).

Result: 1) All-parallel floor reaches 0.286 at final slot on Qwen3-4B, limiting even best proposals to 71% acceptance. 2) One realized token removes 86-100% of this floor. 3) Current drafters (DFlash, DSpark) remain far above their floors, with final-slot model gaps accounting for 43-64% and 85-92% of rejection respectively.

Conclusion: The analysis separates the value of short-range conditioning from proposal quality, revealing that fundamental information constraints limit block drafter performance even before considering model quality issues.

Abstract: Block drafters propose several tokens in one forward pass, before earlier target tokens are realised. Their rejection mixes two losses: missing within-block path information and imperfect modelling of observable information. Accepted length cannot distinguish them. We separate the two with an information floor, the minimum expected rejection at a specified conditioning order; rejection above this floor is the model gap. Estimating both from target rollouts across four domains, four open-weight targets, and a frontier API target yields three findings. First, the all-parallel floor reaches $0.286$ at the final slot on Qwen3-4B, limiting even the best proposal to $71\%$ per-slot acceptance. Second, one realised token removes $86$--$100\%$ of this floor, a locality also recovered by an independent mutual-information analysis. Third, current drafters remain far above their floors: the final-slot model gap accounts for $43$--$64\%$ of DFlash rejection and $85$--$92\%$ of DSpark's oracle-conditioned rejection. These findings separate the value of short-range conditioning from proposal quality.

</details>


### [173] [Understanding Evolution Strategies for LLM Reasoning: Broader Reasoning Coverage than GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27351)
*Yunpeng Ba,Zhi Zheng,Yue Xie,Jiaqing Li,Xialiang Tong,Tao Zhong,Mingxuan Yuan,Zhichao Lu,Xuyang Wu,Zhenkun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Evolution Strategies (ES) outperform GRPO in LLM reasoning by achieving broader coverage and higher Pass@K, while maintaining functional sparsity and not causing catastrophic forgetting, positioning ES as a distinct post-training paradigm rather than just a memory-efficient alternative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ES has emerged as a memory-efficient post-training approach for LLM reasoning, but its optimization behavior is poorly understood compared to mainstream methods like GRPO. The paper aims to systematically investigate ES dynamics and mechanisms to define its advantage scope and understand why it works.

Method: Systematically investigate ES dynamics through: 1) Theoretical analysis showing ES's advantage in verifier-projected Jensen-Shannon diversity, 2) Empirical comparisons with GRPO on reasoning tasks, 3) Developing sequential GRPO-ES training strategy, 4) Analysis of parameter drift and functional sparsity, 5) Hyperparameter studies on population size effects.

Result: ES outperforms GRPO in broader reasoning coverage and higher Pass@K performance while maintaining Pass@1 improvements. ES exhibits functional sparsity despite substantial parameter drift, preventing catastrophic forgetting. A sequential GRPO-ES strategy combines both methods' strengths. ES requires smaller population sizes in larger LLMs.

Conclusion: ES is a distinct reasoning post-training paradigm with unique advantages over GRPO, not just a memory-efficient alternative. It provides better reasoning coverage, functional sparsity properties, and different optimization characteristics that position it as a valuable approach in the LLM reasoning post-training landscape.

Abstract: Evolution Strategies (ES) have recently emerged as a memory-efficient post-training paradigm for LLM reasoning. However, the optimization behavior of ES remains understudied, making it hard to define its advantage scope compared to mainstream post-training paradigms (e.g., Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)). By systematically investigating ES dynamics and mechanisms, this paper first identifies a performance advantage of ES over GRPO, theoretically and empirically showing that ES can lead to broader reasoning coverage, thereby better exploiting the reasoning capabilities of pretrained LLMs. Theoretically, we show that verifier-projected Jensen-Shannon diversity across the ES population is helpful to higher Pass@K performances. Empirically, unlike GRPO, which exhibits entropy collapse, ES improves Pass@1 while attaining higher Pass@K than GRPO. We further develop a sequential GRPO-ES training strategy that combines GRPO's strength in Pass@1 with ES's gains in Pass@K. Second, we find that despite substantial whole-model parameter drift, the task-performance gains of ES are only contributed to a sparse subset of larger-magnitude updates. This functional sparsity suggests that large parameter movement need not imply widespread functional change, and held-out evaluations further show that it does not necessarily lead to catastrophic forgetting. Finally, we study how hyperparameter design affects the effectiveness of ES, demonstrating that ES requires a smaller population size in a larger LLM. These findings position ES as a distinct reasoning post-training paradigm rather than a less effective, memory-efficient alternative to GRPO.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [174] [Zero-Shot Self-Orchestration with Ledger-Based Control for Improved LLM Coding Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26480)
*Victor Gao,Vida Khosrowshahi,Ali Khosrowshahi,Xihao Sun,Juhyun Lee,Simon,Lee*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Multi-agent LLM systems with manager-worker scaffolds over shared filesystem workspaces provide real but conditional performance benefits on hard coding problems, with significant gains for some models but null/negative effects for others.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent LLM systems are widely reported to beat single-model baselines, but evidence is mixed and comparisons are confounded by simultaneous changes to token budgets, tool calls, and prompts, making it unclear what actually contributes to performance improvements.

Method: Investigates the effect of introducing manager-worker scaffolds over shared filesystem workspaces with no training and no per-benchmark tuning, comparing against single-pass responses. Tests across nine models (five open-weight from 9B to ~2.8T parameters, four frontier closed models) on 100 latest hard LiveCodeBench problems.

Result: The scaffold's benefit is real but conditional: significant gains for some models (Qwen3.8-27B +23.4, GPT-5.6-Luna +10.6, GPT-5.6-Terra +8.0, Kimi-K3 +30.4, Minimax-M3 +11.0) and null/negative for others (Qwen3.6-35B -1 to -9). Manager triples token costs but buys accuracy more cheaply than moving to larger models. Key mechanisms: context management and problem decomposition.

Conclusion: Multi-agent manager-worker scaffolds provide economically viable performance improvements for coding tasks, with benefits being model-dependent and most pronounced for smaller models or those with reasoning disabled, making them a cost-effective alternative to using larger models.

Abstract: Multi-agent large language model systems are widely reported to beat single-model baselines, but the evidence is mixed, and comparisons are usually confounded: pipelines change token budgets, tool calls, and prompts simultaneously, so an aggregate gain rarely reveals what actually helped. We investigate the effect of introducing the manager-worker scaffold over a shared filesystem workspace, with no training and no per-benchmark tuning, measured against the same model answering in a single pass.
